# Rooms content audit, November 2026

Read-only audit of all 110 rooms by 14 agents. Question: can a learner answer every graded question from the room's reading text, is each question fair, and is each subject covered end to end. Nothing here has been applied. `add` lines are drafts and still need checking before they go into a reading.

- Graded items and checkpoints checked: 1419
- Answerable from the text: 1293 (91%)
- Not answerable from the text (gap): 119
- Text points the wrong way (wrong): 7
- Doubtful or disputed answers: 68
- Too hard for the room's level: 16
- Too easy, no learning happens: 182
- Material topics missing, by room: 487

## Text points the wrong way

- **analyst-mindset / mind-ac1** (mind-r2): The room never defines verdict labels, and mind-r2 calls a legitimate backup agent's expected connection a false positive, so a careful reader labels the near-identical signed backup job here the same way; that line must change as well as this sentence being added.
  - Add: Analysts close alerts with one of three labels: true positive means the detection caught real malicious activity, false positive means the rule misfired on something it was not meant to catch, and benign true positive means the rule correctly caught real activity that turns out to be authorized, which you close by citing the justification, such as a ticket, without weakening the rule.
- **asset-context-prioritisation / asset-o1** (asset-r7): The reading's diagram labels the MEDIUM alert on the payment system during launch as Priority 1, so a careful reader ranks it first, while the answer puts the executive laptop above it; nothing explains how to break that tie, or that an alert already contained by a product ranks lower.
  - Add: Business context does not automatically win: an alert on a device whose account holds standing admin access to something like a deployment pipeline can outrank a busy production system, because its compromise reaches many systems rather than one, and an alert the product has already contained ranks below an unresolved alert on a similarly low-value asset, even if that asset is isolated.
- **google-workspace-security / gwsf-la2 (q3)** (gwsf-r6): No reading says containment precedes scoping, and gwsf-r6 calls blocklisting 'the blocklisting step that follows an OAuth-abuse investigation', which points the other way; that phrase needs changing as well.
  - Add: Containment comes before scoping in an OAuth-abuse case: while a grant is live the app can keep pulling data, and cutting its access erases nothing from the audit logs, so the full review of what it touched can safely follow.
- **google-workspace-security / gwsf-o1** (gwsf-r6): No reading gives this sequence, and gwsf-r6's phrase that blocklisting follows the investigation leads a careful reader to place scoping before it; verification before scoping is also untaught.
  - Add: Containment comes before scoping in an OAuth-abuse case: while a grant is live the app can keep pulling data, and cutting its access erases nothing from the audit logs, so the full review of what it touched can safely follow. Confirm in the admin log that the cut-off took effect, and leave wider policy changes until the incident is closed.
- **identity-basics / idbasics-o1** (idbasics-r5): The text and its diagram make re-enabling the third step and describe the review only afterwards as one more step, which points a careful reader to re-enable before reviewing, the reverse of the graded order.
  - Add: Do the review of what the session touched and created while the account is still disabled, and hand the account back to its owner only as the very last step, once that review is finished and the owner has confirmed it is under their sole control.
- **sharepoint-teams-monitoring / spt-teams-la1 (q2)** (spt-teams-r1): No reading mentions MITRE tactics, and the heading 'Bulk Downloads: The Classic Data Exfiltration Pattern' teaches that a bulk download is exfiltration.
  - Add: A download only moves files from SharePoint onto a device, which MITRE ATT&CK classes under the Collection tactic; it counts as Exfiltration only once you have evidence the data then left the organisation, such as a USB copy, a personal cloud upload or a large outbound email.
- **timestamps-and-timelines / time-o1** (time-r4): No reading gives the whole sequence, and time-r3 ('even once every timestamp is correctly converted to UTC') with time-r4 ('normalize everything to one timezone first') points a careful reader to normalize before checking clock sync.
  - Add: For a multi-source timeline, work in this order: collect the events from every source, confirm each source's clock sync so you know what skew to correct, convert everything to one timezone and apply those corrections, sort by the corrected time, scan the result for suspicious gaps, and only then write the story and work out dwell time.

## Not answerable from the text

- **active-directory / ad-la2 (q2)** (ad-r5): The reading lists only long-term prevention (SMB signing, disabling LLMNR) and no immediate containment, so it nudges a reader toward the prevention option instead of removing the attacker's device first.
  - Add: Prevention comes after containment: during a live relay, first cut off the unknown device doing the relaying, since it can keep poisoning every user on that subnet, then reset the victim's credentials and end their sessions, because a password reset alone does not close a session the relay already opened; the victim's own PC only lent its name and is not the source.
- **active-directory / ad-ac1** (ad-r3) [taught in another room]: The reading tells analysts to treat unusual privileged activity as a priority but never teaches checking it against change records, and it does not define a benign true positive.
  - Add: Before escalating a privileged logon that looks alarming, compare the account, target, time and source with the approved change record and the known admin hosts. A rule can fire correctly on real activity that turns out to be authorized: that outcome is a benign true positive, closed by citing the approval, while a false positive means the rule misjudged what happened, and mixing the two up invites weakening a detection that works.
- **ai-attacks-soc-llm-risks / aisoc-m1** (aisoc-r7) [taught in another room]: This room names the Part 1 risks but never gives the LLM01, LLM02 and LLM04 numbers or says what Sensitive Information Disclosure and Data and Model Poisoning actually do.
  - Add: As a recap of Part 1: LLM01 Prompt Injection is attacker text steering the model away from its instructions, LLM02 Sensitive Information Disclosure is the application revealing what should stay hidden such as its system prompt or documents the user may not see, and LLM04 Data and Model Poisoning is tampering with what a model learns from or retrieves so that it picks up false or backdoored behavior.
- **ai-attacks-soc-social / aisoc-o1** (aisoc-r8): The reading teaches verification controls but no response sequence: freezing first, evidence packaging and a blame-free briefing appear only in the task.
  - Add: Handle a suspected deepfake request in a fixed sequence: stop the request so no money moves while keeping the contact details it arrived with, then verify through an independent channel. If that fails, gather the evidence, open an incident around it and stop any payment in progress, and close with a staff briefing on the pretext that blames nobody.
- **ai-attacks-soc-social / aisoc-la1 (q3)** (aisoc-r10): No reading covers the response to a confirmed paste: assessing what the content held and involving the manager appear only in the task explanation.
  - Add: When a paste to an unsanctioned AI tool is confirmed, treat it as an exposure to assess. Review what the content held, such as embedded keys or sensitive logic, replace any exposed secret, and bring in the employee's manager with guidance on the approved tool. The cause is usually good intent, and cutting off one site does nothing for data already sent.
- **alert-triage / alert-triage-q1** (alert-triage-r1): The text names analysis paralysis and asks whether an alert is part of a pattern, but never teaches what to do with a large cluster of alerts on one host.
  - Add: When a large cluster of alerts lands on one host, do not start at the top: open two or three as a sample and search the SIEM across everything for that host over the last few days, because that shows whether one noisy rule is repeating or several different alert types are building into an attack, and only that answer tells you how to handle the rest.
- **av-vs-edr-masterclass / edr-la1 (q2)** (edr-r4): The text says explorer.exe is trusted but never says why attackers inject into it: that it runs for the whole session under the user's account and outlives the injecting process.
  - Add: Attackers move injected code into a process like explorer.exe because it normally runs for as long as the user stays logged on, under that user's own account, so the code outlives the original powershell.exe and its network traffic appears to belong to a normal Windows program.
- **azure-security / azure-la1 (q2)** (azure-r2): No reading says that SecretGet on a named secret with HTTP 200 returns the secret's value, or that a database connection string usually carries a username and password.
  - Add: In Key Vault diagnostic logs, SecretGet with a Success result (HTTP 200) means the vault handed the secret's value to the caller, while SecretList only returns secret names; a database connection string usually embeds a username and password, so treat it as a live credential.
- **bec-investigation / bec-ac1** (bec-r4) [taught in another room]: No reading defines the verdict labels or says when an inbox rule is authorized activity (internal destination, known egress IP, a ticket); the benign true positive versus false positive distinction appears only in the task's explanation.
  - Add: Not every new inbox rule is an attack: a rule forwarding a copy to an internal colleague, created from the known corporate egress IP and backed by a helpdesk ticket, is authorized activity. Analysts close alerts with one of three labels: true positive means the detection caught real malicious activity, false positive means the rule misfired on something it was not meant to catch, and benign true positive means the rule correctly caught real activity that turns out to be authorized, which you close by citing the justification, such as a ticket, without weakening the rule.
- **cloud-security-monitoring / cloud-ac1** (cloud-r2) [taught in another room]: The only IAM escalation taught is CreateUser plus AttachUserPolicy; no reading mentions attaching a stronger role to an EC2 instance, AssociateIamInstanceProfile, iam:PassRole, or why an AWS-owned source IP does not clear a call.
  - Add: Not every IAM escalation creates a new user: an identity can check who it is with GetCallerIdentity, list roles with ListRoles, then use AssociateIamInstanceProfile to give a more powerful role to an EC2 instance it can run code on, gaining that role's permissions; a source IP in AWS address space proves nothing here, since any AWS customer can run from those ranges.
- **commodity-initial-access / mcia-ac1** (mcia-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading says that real vendors publish one-line download-and-run installers, that a verified change record can explain the shape, or what the closing verdict labels mean.
  - Add: Some real software vendors publish official one-line PowerShell installers that look the same in telemetry as a paste-and-run attack, so check the destination domain and any verified change record before judging the shape. Analysts close an alert with one of three labels: a true positive is a real attack, a false positive is a rule that misfired, and a benign true positive is a rule that fired correctly on real activity that was approved, with that approval cited at closure.
- **commodity-initial-access / mcia-o1** (mcia-r1): No reading gives a triage order for these alerts, and none mentions checking for an approved business reason before deciding.
  - Add: When no obviously bad file exists, work the alert in a fixed sequence: first establish what started the process and whether any file came before it, then look at where the activity connected and how far it got, then look for an approved business reason, and only after all of that decide and write it up.
- **credential-attacks-practice / cred-la1 (q3)** (cred-r2) [taught in another room]: Neither reading says what to do after a burst is identified, and neither explains Event ID 4624.
  - Add: Event ID 4624 records a successful logon, so after identifying a failure burst, block or rate-limit the source, search the whole aggregate for a 4624 from that source because one record cannot show that the burst failed, find out why lockout did not stop the run, and ask the account owner whether there is a mundane cause.
- **credential-attacks-practice / cred-la2 (q3)** (cred-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading says that a spray's outcome must be checked with 4624 searches or that a lack of lockouts proves nothing.
  - Add: A spray is built to avoid lockouts, so seeing none proves nothing: block or rate-limit the source, then search every targeted account for a 4624 successful logon from that source, and treat any account with a match as compromised.
- **credential-attacks-practice / cred-ac1** (cred-r2) [taught in another room]: The readings give no benign explanation for repeated failures and never tell the learner to check them against change records, although the room's description promises this trap.
  - Add: Before calling a repeated-failure burst an attack, compare its sources with your own assets and change records: failures from your own known servers, clustered just after a ticketed password rotation, are real events with an authorized cause, the lag before each host picks up the new credential, so you close them citing the ticket instead of escalating them or weakening the rule.
- **credential-attacks-practice / cred-o1** (cred-r2): No reading describes what follows a successful spray.
  - Add: When a spray finds a working password, the failures are followed by a 4624 from the same source; the attacker then opens a working session, often from a different source, uses the account to list groups and shares, and finally reaches sensitive servers the account has no history with.
- **crowdstrike-falcon / cs-falcon-la1 (q2)** (cs-falcon-r2): The text only lists a service account as a possible UserName value; nothing says attackers abuse established service accounts or why their activity blends in.
  - Add: Service accounts are attractive to attackers because they hold broad rights, run around the clock and draw little scrutiny, so a long-established account doing something outside its normal job is a sign of abuse, not of safety.
- **detection-engineering / deteng-la1 (q2)** (deteng-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading explains account lockout or why a spray keeps attempts per account low, so the reason behind the two-per-account figure is absent.
  - Add: A password spray tries only a guess or two per account across many accounts because lockout policies commonly trip after roughly 3 to 10 failures on a single account, so dividing total failures by distinct accounts shows whether the attacker is staying below that limit.
- **detection-engineering / deteng-la1 (q3)** (deteng-r2) [taught in another room]: The room covers building rules, not acting on them, and nowhere says which response comes first after a spray succeeds.
  - Add: When a correlation shows a spray succeeded, the accounts that logged on are the live threat because valid credentials work from any address, so taking those accounts out of the attacker's hands and ending their sessions outranks blocking the source IP or collecting evidence, and escalation runs alongside that containment rather than delaying it.
- **detection-engineering / deteng-queryfill1** (deteng-r2): SPL's stats command and its distinct-count function appear in no reading, so the first blank cannot be filled from the text.
  - Add: In Splunk SPL the stats command aggregates events: count(field) counts every event while dc(field), short for distinct count, counts how many different values the field holds, and the by clause gives one result per value of a chosen field, so a threshold on different accounts per source combines the two.
- **device-registration-persistence / devreg-o1** (devreg-r3): The text says the rogue method must be removed as its own step, but no reading gives the sequence, and blocking sign-in, re-enabling the account and out-of-band re-registration appear nowhere.
  - Add: When the attacker holds both the password and a working factor, work in a fixed order: first stop the account from signing in at all, because revoking sessions on an enabled account only lets the attacker log in again; next delete the rogue factor or device; then change the password and kill every session and refresh token in one move; then check the sign-in logs for any further use; only then let the real user back in to enrol their own methods through a verified channel outside the account; and write up the timeline last.
- **device-registration-persistence / devreg-ac1** (devreg-r5) [taught in another room]: The benign context is taught, but no reading defines the verdict labels, so a reader cannot tell which label fits a real, authorised registration.
  - Add: When you close an alert like this the label matters: a false positive means the rule matched something it was not built to catch, while a benign true positive means the rule caught a real event of the kind it watches for and the surrounding context shows that event was authorised, and the ticket or record that proves it is what you cite at closure.
- **discovery-and-enumeration / disc-la2 (q2)** (disc-r5) [taught in another room]: No reading explains how to read CloudTrail identity fields, so a beginner cannot rule out that AssumedRole or the session name proves the Lambda function made the call.
  - Add: In a CloudTrail record, userIdentity shows which credentials were presented, not who is holding them: type AssumedRole only means temporary role credentials were used and the session name travels with those credentials, so compare userAgent and sourceIPAddress with how that role normally runs, because stolen credentials work from anywhere.
- **dns-investigation / dns-ac1** (dns-r3) [taught in another room]: The text teaches each signature but no rule for mixed signals, so nothing tells a reader to hand the case up instead of forcing a verdict.
  - Add: When indicators conflict, such as heavy queries to one domain from an unexplained process but short labels, a resolving domain of some age and no EDR finding, do not force a verdict: pass it to the next tier with what you checked and what is still open, such as the process command line and the query types and label lengths across all the traffic.
- **edge-case-usecases / edge-la3 (q2)** (edge-r2): The reading explains how to spot the low-and-slow pattern but never says what an analyst does next: scoping, onward transfer checks, involving the manager and HR, or why not to act alone.
  - Add: Once the trend and the role mismatch line up, do not lock the user out or quietly cut access on your own: confirm what was taken, look for onward copies to personal email, cloud storage or USB, and pass it to the manager and HR through the insider-threat playbook, because an approved project or role change may still explain it.
- **edge-case-usecases / edge-ac3** (edge-r5) [taught in another room]: The text shows how to see that the regsvr32 run is legitimate but never defines a benign true positive or separates it from a false positive, and it says nothing about activity that starts slightly outside its change window.
  - Add: A rule can fire correctly on real activity that turns out to be authorized: that outcome is a benign true positive, closed by citing the approval, while a false positive means the rule misjudged what happened, and mixing the two up invites weakening a detection that works. If authorized work starts a few minutes outside its approved window, record that as a process note for the change board, not as evidence of intrusion.
- **email-protocols-forensics / email-m1** (email-r2): The Reply-To pair has no support: no reading mentions the Reply-To header; the other five pairs are covered.
  - Add: Mail clients send a reply to the Reply-To address when a message has one, not to the From address. Nothing ties it to From or Return-Path, and none of SPF, DKIM or DMARC look at it, so attackers use it to steer replies to a mailbox they control.
- **email-protocols-forensics / email-la1 (q1)** (email-r2): The question turns on what Reply-To does, and no reading mentions that header.
  - Add: Mail clients send a reply to the Reply-To address when a message has one, not to the From address. Nothing ties it to From or Return-Path, and none of SPF, DKIM or DMARC look at it, so attackers use it to steer replies to a mailbox they control.
- **email-protocols-forensics / email-la1 (q3)** (email-r2): No reading defines Business Email Compromise or gives any response to a spoofed message; purging all copies, blocking and warning the targeted team appear only in the task explanation.
  - Add: A message that impersonates an executive to push a payment is called Business Email Compromise (BEC). Respond at organisation scope: search the gateway for every copy, since the To line can hide other recipients, remove those copies, block the sending infrastructure, warn the team being targeted, and never check a request by replying to the message itself.
- **email-protocols-forensics / email-la2 (q3)** (email-r6): No reading covers the response to a phish from a correctly configured lookalike domain, or why action must cover the whole domain.
  - Add: When a lookalike domain is confirmed, act on the whole domain rather than one address, since its owner can create new senders at will. Hold or remove the message, alert the people it targeted, and feed the case into detection based on registration age and name similarity, because authentication will keep passing for that sender.
- **email-protocols-forensics / email-ac1** (email-r5) [taught in another room]: The verdict labels are never defined in this room: nothing separates a benign true positive from a false positive, though the facts about list forwarding and ARC are taught.
  - Add: When a detection fires on something that really happened but is authorised, analysts call it a benign true positive and close it citing the authorisation. A false positive is different: the rule matched something it was never meant to catch, so the behaviour it describes did not occur.
- **encoding-encryption-hashing / enc-ac1** (enc-r2): No reading defines the verdict labels, and none says that a change ticket only covers an encoded command once the decoded content matches it.
  - Add: A true positive is a rule firing on real malicious activity, a benign true positive is a rule firing correctly on real activity that was authorised, such as ticketed IT work, and a false positive is a rule that misfired on something it was never meant to match. A change ticket explains an encoded command only after you decode it and confirm the content matches what the ticket describes.
- **entra-id / entra-la1 (q2)** (entra-r3): No reading says a failed sign-in still needs investigation, or that the next steps are checking for successful logins and resetting the password; this appears only in the task explanation.
  - Add: A failed sign-in from a suspicious location does not close the case: error 50126 still shows that someone outside holds a real username and is trying it, so look for successful sign-ins by that account elsewhere and reset the password as a precaution.
- **escalation-procedures / esc-log-1 (q2)** (esc-read-2) [taught in another room]: Domain controllers are only listed as a sensitive system; no reading says what a domain controller does or why reaching one changes the urgency.
  - Add: A domain controller is the server that checks every login and holds the accounts for a whole Windows domain, so an attacker who reaches one can act on every machine in that domain; when spread is active, whether the domain controller is still untouched tells responders how much time is left to contain it.
- **esxi-virtualization-security / esxf-ac1** (esxf-r5) [taught in another room]: The readings say a change record matters, but none defines the verdict labels, so a beginner cannot separate authorised real activity from a false positive.
  - Add: When a matching change record exists, the alert is still correct, so close it with the right label: a true positive is real malicious activity, a benign true positive is real activity the rule was built to catch but which is authorised, and a false positive is the rule misfiring on something it was never meant to catch.
- **firewall-log-analysis / fw-ac1** (fw-r2): The text teaches the beacon shape but never says legitimate software also polls on a timer, how to tell the two apart, or that an address missing from reputation lists proves nothing, which is the deciding point of this verdict.
  - Add: A steady timer alone does not prove malice, because legitimate agents such as endpoint, sync and update clients also poll on a schedule; weigh whether the pattern is new, whether the destination matches any software installed on the machine, and remember that an address missing from reputation lists clears nothing, since fresh attacker infrastructure is often not listed yet.
- **firewall-network-security / fw-la1 (q2)** (fw-r3): The text only says port 3389 is RDP; nothing explains which exposed ports are most dangerous or why.
  - Add: Not all open ports carry the same risk: Remote Desktop (RDP, port 3389) reachable from the internet is one of the most common ways ransomware crews get in, by guessing passwords or exploiting RDP flaws, so it belongs behind a VPN or jump server, whereas ports 80 and 443 are expected on a public web address.
- **google-workspace-security / gwsf-ac1** (gwsf-r4) [taught in another room]: The discriminators are taught, but this room never defines the verdict labels, so nothing separates a benign true positive from a false positive.
  - Add: When a detection fires on something that really happened but is authorised, analysts call it a benign true positive and close it citing the authorisation. A false positive is different: the rule matched something it was never meant to catch, so the behaviour it describes did not occur.
- **html-smuggling-and-lolbins / hsl-ac1** (hsl-r9) [taught in another room]: The readings teach why this rundll32 run is routine, but none defines the verdict labels, so a beginner cannot separate authorised real activity from a false positive.
  - Add: An alert closes under one of three labels: a true positive is real malicious activity, a benign true positive is real activity the rule was built to catch but which is authorised, so you cite the change ticket when closing it, and a false positive is the rule misfiring on something it was never meant to catch.
- **identity-basics / idbasics-ac1** (idbasics-r6) [taught in another room]: No reading says how to tell an MFA-fatigue burst from a user's own retries, or what the closing verdict labels mean.
  - Add: A burst of push prompts is not proof of attack by itself: check whose device started the sign-ins and whether the user confirms them. Analysts close an alert with one of three labels: a true positive is a real attack, a false positive is a rule that misfired, and a benign true positive is a rule that fired correctly on real activity that turned out to be legitimate.
- **incident-response-methodology / ir-method-la1 (q1)** (ir-method-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading mentions svchost.exe, where genuine Windows system binaries live, or malware posing under a system process name.
  - Add: During identification, check where a suspicious process runs from, not only its name: the genuine svchost.exe is launched from C:\Windows\System32, so a file with that name in C:\Windows\Temp, AppData or any other folder is almost certainly malware masquerading as a Windows process (MITRE ATT&CK T1036.005, Match Legitimate Resource Name or Location).
- **intro-cybersecurity / intro-cyber-la1 (q2)** (intro-cyber-r2b): The phishing entry describes fake emails only; nothing teaches look-alike domains, how to compare a link's domain with the company's real one, or what a foreign country ending may signal.
  - Add: Phishing links often lead to a look-alike domain: the real company's name mixed with extra words on an address the attacker owns, sometimes under a foreign ending such as .ru, so compare the full domain with the company's real one instead of trusting a familiar word inside it.
- **investigate-alert-workflow / inv7-la1 (q1)** (inv7-r1): No reading explains the Defender process-creation schema or mentions FileName, ProcessCommandLine or the InitiatingProcess fields.
  - Add: A Defender process-creation record describes two processes: plain fields such as FileName, FolderPath and ProcessCommandLine belong to the new process, and every field beginning InitiatingProcess belongs to its parent, the process that started it.
- **investigate-alert-workflow / inv7-la1 (q2)** (inv7-r1) [taught in another room]: The text says to check that the parent process fits, but never says what a fitting parent is, what a .docm file is, or what the hidden and encoded PowerShell flags mean.
  - Add: A document editor only displays documents, so an Office program starting PowerShell is out of character, and it usually means a macro ran: a .docm file is a macro-enabled Word document, and the PowerShell flags -w hidden and -enc mean no visible window and a base64-encoded command.
- **investigate-alert-workflow / inv7-la2 (q2)** (inv7-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading mentions logon types or NTLM, and the text never says that a logon is judged against the account's normal pattern.
  - Add: Windows logon records carry a LogonType: 2 is a seat at the console, 3 is a network logon, 5 is a service; NTLM is one authentication method and is common, so neither value proves misuse alone, and the useful test is comparing the logon with where this account has normally connected from and to.
- **investigate-alert-workflow / inv7-qf1** (inv7-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading gives the Windows event ID for a successful logon.
  - Add: In the Windows Security log a successful logon is event ID 4624 and a failed one is 4625, so a pivot on a workstation starts from those records.
- **ioc-analysis / ioc-la1 (q1)** (ioc-r2): The readings mention Tor only as a shared anonymising relay and never explain what Tor is or what an inside-to-outside connection to a relay means.
  - Add: Tor is an anonymity network that passes traffic through several volunteer relays, and a device using it opens outbound connections to those relays, so an internal host connecting out to a Tor relay address points to Tor software on that host, whereas a Tor exit address arriving inbound as the source means someone is using Tor to reach you.
- **ioc-analysis / ioc-la1 (q2)** (ioc-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading explains why port 443 suits malicious traffic; the only port discussed is 50050.
  - Add: Almost every organisation must allow outbound port 443 for HTTPS, so attackers often run command-and-control (C2) traffic over it to blend in with normal web browsing, and the port alone therefore tells you little about what the traffic is.
- **kerberos-authentication / krb-la1 (q3)** (krb-r5): No reading says how to respond to Kerberoasting; rotating the exposed service-account passwords before cracking finishes appears only in the task explanation.
  - Add: When Kerberoasting is confirmed, the secret at risk belongs to the service accounts behind the requested SPNs, not to the requester, so change those passwords before offline cracking can finish, look for the tool on the requesting host, and restrict those accounts to AES through msDS-SupportedEncryptionTypes.
- **kerberos-authentication / krb-la2 (q3)** (krb-r5): No reading says how to respond to AS-REP roasting; retiring the account, changing its password regardless, and hunting for other flagged accounts appear only in the task explanation.
  - Add: When AS-REP roasting is found, assume the crackable material has already left and that its cracking cannot be observed: change that account's password even after fixing the flag, disable the account if nothing still uses it, and search the directory for other accounts carrying DONT_REQ_PREAUTH.
- **kubernetes-container-security / k8s-la2 (q2)** (k8s-r3): No reading says what to do with a suspicious action by a real engineer, so the rule of verifying before closing or containing is only in the task's explanation.
  - Add: A kubectl exec by a known engineer outside their normal pattern proves neither routine debugging nor a stolen account, so the first step is to confirm it against a ticket or with the person or their manager, before you either close the alert or take containment action.
- **lateral-movement-practice / lat-la2 (q2)** (lat-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading mentions PsExec, its PSEXESVC service, or why real intrusions often show a tool's default name.
  - Add: PsExec, a common remote-execution tool, registers a service called PSEXESVC running PSEXESVC.exe by default; the name can be changed, yet many real intrusions leave it as shipped, so learning default footprints like this pays off, and the name says nothing about legitimacy because administrators use the same tool.
- **lateral-movement-practice / lat-ac1** (lat-r2) [taught in another room]: The reading gives the context checks (jump host, ticket, timing) but never defines the benign true positive verdict or separates it from a false positive.
  - Add: When a rule correctly catches real remote administration and the source and a change ticket show it was authorized, the verdict is a benign true positive: the detection did its job, so it is not a false positive, and the ticket is the justification for closing it.
- **lateral-movement-practice / lat-m1** (lat-r1) [taught in another room]: Only the SMB pair is covered; no reading mentions RDP's LogonType 10, WMI's TCP/135 and WmiPrvSE.exe, or WinRM's ports 5985/5986 and wsmprovhost.exe.
  - Add: Each remote-execution method leaves its own trace: RDP is a 4624 with LogonType 10 and a full interactive session; WMI is a connection to TCP/135 then a dynamic high port, with a new process under WmiPrvSE.exe and no new service; WinRM/PowerShell Remoting is an HTTP or HTTPS session on TCP 5985/5986, with spawned processes under wsmprovhost.exe.
- **log-entry-anatomy / loganat-ac1** (loganat-r7) [taught in another room]: The reading shows why a critical label is not proof of an attack but never defines a benign true positive or separates it from a false positive, so the reader cannot pick the verdict.
  - Add: A rule can fire correctly on real activity that turns out to be authorized: that outcome is a benign true positive, closed by citing the approval, while a false positive means the rule misjudged what happened, and mixing the two up invites weakening a detection that works.
- **log-management / log-mgmt-la1 (q3)** (log-mgmt-r3): No reading says how to respond to a log-clear event, why a domain controller raises the stakes, or what Tier 2 and IR mean.
  - Add: A cleared log is itself an alarm: unless an approved change explains it, pass it to senior analysts or the incident response team at once rather than waiting for more signs. This matters most on a domain controller, the server that manages every account in the domain.
- **log-sources-integration / log-src-flag1** (log-src-r1) [taught in another room]: No reading links a destination port to its service; the only clue is the rule name inside the log itself.
  - Add: The dpt field names the destination port, and well-known ports reveal the service being targeted: 22 is SSH, 25 is SMTP, 53 is DNS, 80 is HTTP, 443 is HTTPS and 3389 is RDP.
- **macos-security-fundamentals / macf-ac1** (macf-r8) [taught in another room]: No reading in this room defines the verdict labels, so a reader can see the install is legitimate but cannot tell which closing label fits or why it differs from a false positive.
  - Add: Analysts close an alert with one of three labels: a true positive is a real attack, a false positive is a rule that misfired on something that did not match what it looks for, and a benign true positive is a rule that fired correctly on real activity that turned out to be legitimate, with the evidence for that cited at closure.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals / malware-q1** (malware-r2): The text explains looking up a hash on VirusTotal but never says what a zero score means or does not mean.
  - Add: A VirusTotal score counts only the engines that recognised the file, so a score of zero means nothing matched, not that the file is safe: attackers test new samples against these engines and change them until none fire, so a suspicious file with no detections still needs its behaviour examined.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals / malware-la1 (q1)** (malware-r1): No reading explains what an antivirus quarantine action does and does not cover, and the only use of the word is the sandbox analogy.
  - Add: When antivirus quarantines a file it locks away that one copy so it cannot run; it does not undo anything the file already did before detection, it does not isolate the machine, and it does not tell you whether other machines received the same file, so a quarantine is a reason to keep investigating.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals / malware-la1 (q2)** (malware-r2): PEStudio's version info is named but nothing says it can hold a publisher name, how to weigh a blank one, or that it is separate from the digital signature.
  - Add: Version info is text inside the file that can include the publisher's company name; commercial software normally fills it in and malware authors often leave it empty, so a blank value is weak evidence alone but counts when other signs agree, and it is separate from the digital signature, which must be checked on its own.
- **malware-types / malware-la1 (q3)** (malware-r2): The text only says to block the destination; no reading says to take the host off the network or to keep the file for analysis.
  - Add: Blocking the destination is only half of the response to a suspected loader: also take the host off the network, because stage 2 may already have landed or may arrive another way, and keep the file for analysis rather than deleting it.
- **malware-types / malware-ac2** (malware-r7) [taught in another room]: The room never defines the closing labels, so a reader who sees the rollout was approved cannot choose between a benign true positive and a false positive.
  - Add: Analysts keep two closing labels apart: a false positive means the detection misfired and the thing it claims is not there, and a benign true positive means the detection correctly found something real that turned out to be authorized.
- **memory-disk-forensics / mdf-ac1** (mdf-r5) [taught in another room]: The room teaches shadow-copy deletion only as a ransomware signal: it never says backup software prunes copies legitimately, what separates the two, or how a correct alert on authorized activity is labelled.
  - Add: Backup software legitimately prunes old shadow copies on a schedule, so before escalating check the parent process, the account, the change record, and how much the command removes: one volume's oldest copy is retention, while /all wipes every restore point. When a rule fires correctly on real activity that a change record authorizes, the verdict is a benign true positive: nothing misfired, so the analyst closes it citing that record and does not weaken the rule.
- **mitre-attack / mitre-la1 (q2)** (mitre-r3): No reading explains the UserAgent or IsInteractive fields, or how to tell a script from a browser in a sign-in log.
  - Add: The UserAgent field names the software behind a request: browsers send strings beginning Mozilla/5.0, while programming libraries send their own name and version. IsInteractive false marks a sign-in that no one entered at a sign-in prompt.
- **okta-identity-fundamentals / oktaf-la1 (q3)** (oktaf-r3): No reading says what to do once a password is confirmed correct, or why cutting off one source address or touching the factors does not solve it.
  - Add: Once a password is confirmed correct, the password itself is the lost asset: it works from any address, so it must be changed at once, and the same source and account should be searched for anything else done in that window. The second factor that held is not the problem and does not need replacing.
- **okta-identity-fundamentals / oktaf-la2 (q3)** (oktaf-r8): No reading covers containment after a privilege change, including revoking live sessions and removing leftover delegated admin roles.
  - Add: When one account shows a stolen credential and a live session used for a privilege change, every foothold has to go in the same step: the credential, any open sessions, and each access the account gained or still holds, including old delegated roles nobody removed. Then review what that access was used for, because fixing only one of these leaves the others working.
- **osint-fundamentals / osint-la1 (q1)** (osint-r2): Tor appears only as a passing name, and no reading says what it is, what a relay is, or what a Shodan tor tag means, so the keyed reading of the fields and the onion-service distractor cannot be judged.
  - Add: Tor is a volunteer network that hides where traffic comes from by passing it through a chain of relay servers, and Shodan labels such servers with a tor tag. A public IP with that tag is a relay, while hidden .onion sites are reached only from inside the network and do not sit at a public address. A corporate laptop rarely has a business reason to talk to one, so the contact suggests that someone or something is deliberately hiding traffic.
- **ot-ics-security / ot-ac1** (ot-r5) [taught in another room]: The readings say source and function code decide the case, but none mentions change tickets or maintenance windows, and none defines the benign true positive verdict.
  - Add: A write from the sanctioned engineering workstation that a change ticket places inside an approved maintenance window is authorized work: if a rule fires on it, the detection worked on real activity, so the verdict is a benign true positive rather than a false positive, and the ticket is what you cite when closing it.
- **playbook-execution-and-escalation / pbk-la1 (q1)** (pbk-r1) [taught in another room]: No reading says which Entra sign-in fields prove a sign-in happened, what ResultType 0 means, or that risk fields and CorrelationId do not prove it.
  - Add: To validate an Entra ID sign-in, read the outcome fields: ResultType 0 is the success code and AuthenticationDetails shows whether each authentication step succeeded, while risk fields only give Entra's opinion of how unusual the sign-in looked and CorrelationId only groups records from one request flow.
- **playbook-execution-and-escalation / pbk-la1 (q2)** (pbk-r1) [taught in another room]: No reading explains what Conditional Access is or what ConditionalAccessStatus and AuthenticationRequirement describe, including that they apply to the application and not the user's whole account.
  - Add: Conditional Access is the set of tenant policies that can demand MFA or block a sign-in for a given application; ConditionalAccessStatus notApplied means no policy was in scope for this sign-in, AuthenticationRequirement singleFactorAuthentication means one factor was enough, and both describe this application's sign-in, not whether the user has MFA elsewhere.
- **protocols-masterclass / proto-la1 (q3)** (proto-r10): No reading says what to do once DNS tunneling is suspected: containment first, then host history, then the same domain on other hosts appears only in task explanations.
  - Add: Treat suspected DNS tunneling as a leak in progress: cut the host's network access first, then read its DNS history to learn when the channel began, and search every other machine for the same parent domain.
- **ransomware-full-lifecycle / rw-la1 (q1)** (rw-r4) [taught in another room]: No reading in this room mentions comsvcs.dll or its MiniDump function, so a beginner cannot confirm what this rundll32 command does or that it writes a dump to disk.
  - Add: A common way to dump LSASS without dropping any new tool is rundll32.exe calling the MiniDump function exported by comsvcs.dll, a signed DLL that ships with Windows: the command line names the target process ID, an output file and the word full, and the memory copy lands on disk at that path.
- **ransomware-full-lifecycle / rw-ac1** (rw-r7) [taught in another room]: No reading in this room defines the verdict labels or says how a correct detection of authorized activity differs from a misfire.
  - Add: When a rule fires correctly on real activity that a change record authorizes, the alert is a benign true positive: nothing misfired, so the analyst closes it by citing that justification and does not weaken the rule. A false positive is different, because there the rule matched something it never should have matched.
- **ransomware-full-lifecycle / rw-la3 (q1)** (rw-r8): No reading says what the genuine Windows Update client is called or where it lives, so the near-match name cannot be recognized or told from a real 32-bit file.
  - Add: Masquerading gives a malicious binary a name a character or two away from a real Windows file and hopes nobody looks closely. Genuine system binaries run from System32 (the Windows Update client, for instance, is wuauclt.exe there, and the 32-bit copy under SysWOW64 keeps that same name), never from a Temp folder.
- **remote-email-collection / remc-o1** (remc-r4) [taught in another room]: No reading gives any response order for a confirmed mailbox compromise; the room says only that some cases need containment.
  - Add: Once mailbox compromise is confirmed, cut access before cleaning up: block sign-in so no new session or token refresh can succeed, reset the password and revoke all sessions back-to-back with MFA re-registration, remove malicious rules only after that because a live session can recreate them, then scope what was read through the SessionId, notify exposed finance contacts or vendors, and document the timeline.
- **remote-email-collection / remc-q4** (remc-r4) [taught in another room]: No reading explains that a session token outlives a password change; the stem relies on the Identity Basics room.
  - Add: A session token stands for a login that already happened, so it stays valid until it expires or is explicitly revoked, and changing the password does not reliably end a session issued before the change.
- **security-products-behaviour / secprod-la1 (q2)** (secprod-r5): The reading warns that an ALLOW is not proof of a clean request but never says what an analyst does next, or that the unread part of the body has to be fetched from somewhere other than the WAF log.
  - Add: A WAF log records the verdict and the headers, not the body, so when a request went past the inspection limit the analyst looks for the uninspected part in whatever the application itself logged and then checks how the oversize setting is configured, because a verdict on bytes nobody read settles nothing.
- **sentinel-fundamentals / sentinel-ac1** (sentinel-r3) [taught in another room]: No reading defines the verdict labels or tells the analyst to check who owns a source and whether a change record explains it before deciding.
  - Add: An analytics rule measures the shape of activity, not its intent, so before deciding check who owns the source and whether a change record covers that time. When the rule fired correctly on real activity that a change record authorizes, the verdict is a benign true positive: nothing misfired, so the analyst closes it citing that record and does not weaken the rule. A false positive means the rule matched something it never should have.
- **sentinelone / s1-la1 (q1)** (s1-r3): No reading covers malware borrowing a trusted name, how to judge a binary by its signature and install location, or what Temp means.
  - Add: Malware often borrows the name of a trusted program, so judge an unfamiliar executable by its digital signature and by whether it sits in that vendor's usual install folder: the name alone proves nothing, a Temp folder alone proves nothing because real installers unpack there briefly, and running with ordinary user rights is still enough to encrypt that user's documents and shares.
- **siem-fundamentals / siem-fund-la1 (q2)** (siem-fund-r3): No reading says what a Tor exit node is or that source enrichment should change confidence in a verdict; Tor appears only inside one example.
  - Add: SIEMs often enrich an alert with context about its source, such as country, provider or reputation. A Tor exit node is a relay in an anonymity network that hides the real sender, and ordinary staff rarely sign in through one. Treat context like this as evidence that shifts your confidence, and weigh it together with the event pattern rather than on its own.
- **siem-fundamentals / siem-fund-la1 (q3)** (siem-fund-r2): No reading tells the analyst that the deciding pivot after a password attack is whether any guess worked; the sequence rule example only shows the chain.
  - Add: After any burst of failed logins, the question that sets the severity is whether a guess worked, so look for a success on one of the targeted accounts after the failures began.
- **sigma-yara-rule-authoring / sigyara-ac1** (sigyara-r2) [taught in another room]: The room uses -nop and -enc only as example strings and never says what they do or that an Office parent for PowerShell is suspicious, so the verdict rests on a rule found only in the explanation.
  - Add: The -nop and -enc flags tell PowerShell to skip the user profile and run a base64-encoded command, which hides the script from a casual reader. Encoded PowerShell has legitimate uses in management tooling, but when the parent process is an Office application opening a macro document, there is almost no legitimate explanation and the alert should be escalated.
- **sigma-yara-rule-authoring / sigyara-la1 (q4)** (sigyara-r3): The readings are about writing rules and never say what a Detected, no action taken disposition means or what an analyst does when a critical rule fires unsuppressed, so waiting for proof or asking the user cannot be ruled out.
  - Add: When a high or critical rule fires and its filter did not suppress the event, treat it as a likely real attack until shown otherwise. An EDR disposition that records detection without any action means the activity was seen, not stopped, so the analyst escalates and contains. Memory can be read and sent out without a file ever reaching disk, and a user's word cannot settle a critical alert.
- **soar-automation / soar-la1 (q2)** (soar-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading in this room says why a service account raises priority: that software logs in with it from stored credentials and that it often holds broad standing rights.
  - Add: Failed logons against a service account deserve extra attention: software signs in with it from a stored credential, so nobody can have mistyped the password, and these accounts often hold broad standing privileges that make a compromise costly.
- **soc-structure / soc-struct-m1** (soc-struct-r3): Firewalls appear only as a log source and IPS is never mentioned, so the Firewall / IPS pair has no support; the SIEM, EDR and SOAR pairs are covered.
  - Add: A firewall sits at the boundary between parts of a network and decides which connections may pass, and an IPS (Intrusion Prevention System) looks inside the traffic and stops attacks it recognises; both also send logs to the SIEM showing who talked to whom.
- **soc-structure / soc-struct-la1 (q2)** (soc-struct-r3): No reading mentions Tor or explains what a Tor exit node means for triage.
  - Add: Threat intelligence may label an address as a Tor exit node: Tor is a network that relays traffic through several computers so the real sender cannot be seen, and although it has lawful privacy uses, repeated failed logons arriving through it point away from a normal employee and towards someone deliberately concealing who they are.
- **tcpip-deep-dive / tcpip-la2 (q3)** (tcpip-r4): The text calls a fingerprint mismatch worth a second look but never says what to do once the benign explanations have been ruled out, and it never mentions checking host telemetry or other sessions from the same address.
  - Add: Once change records rule out a re-image, a routing change and a NAT change, a fingerprint mismatch can no longer be explained away, so treat the link between that address and that device as unverified: raise it to the next tier, review the host's own endpoint telemetry for tampering or unauthorised use, and search other sessions from the same address for the same mismatch, rather than writing it off as a glitch or declaring a breach on one signal.
- **tcpip-deep-dive / tcpip-la1 (q3)** (tcpip-r3) [taught in another room]: No reading says what an internal-to-internal scan implies, that the source host is where to investigate, that endpoint telemetry finds the process behind it, or that a perimeter firewall never sees east-west traffic.
  - Add: A scan coming from an internal workstation with no business reason to scan is usually reconnaissance by an attacker already on that machine, so the investigation starts at the source: use endpoint telemetry to find the process that sent the traffic and look for the same pattern on other hosts; a target that answered a probe is not thereby compromised, and a perimeter firewall never sees traffic that stays inside the LAN.
- **tcpip-deep-dive / tcpip-ac1** (tcpip-r6) [taught in another room]: The load-balancer health-check pattern is taught, but no reading defines the closing labels, so a reader cannot choose between false positive and benign true positive.
  - Add: When you close a flow alert the label matters: a false positive means the rule matched something it was not built to catch, such as a scan rule firing on traffic that is not a scan, while a benign true positive means the rule caught exactly what it was built to catch and that activity turned out to be authorised, such as an approved vulnerability scanner.
- **threat-hunting-fundamentals / threat-hunt-la1 (q1)** (threat-hunt-r3): The text names the MiniDump argument but never gives the command's syntax, so nothing tells the reader what the number in the command line stands for.
  - Add: The comsvcs.dll command line has a fixed shape: MiniDump, then the numeric ID Windows gave the target process (attackers look it up first, for example with tasklist), then the output file name, then the word full for a complete dump.
- **timestamps-and-timelines / time-ac1** (time-r2) [taught in another room]: The conversion is teachable from the text, but the room never defines the closing labels, so a reader who finds the logon was normal cannot choose between a false positive and a benign true positive.
  - Add: Two closing labels are easy to mix up: a false positive means the rule misfired and the behaviour it claims did not happen, and a benign true positive means the behaviour really happened but was authorized; before judging a time-of-day rule, convert the stored UTC value to the site's own local time.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic / tls-la1 (q3)** (tls-r4) [taught in another room]: The room never mentions EDR or says that the step after a strong metadata case is to find the process behind the sessions on the endpoint.
  - Add: Metadata can tell you a session looks like C2 but not what produced it, so once several signals line up, pivot to endpoint telemetry (EDR, the agent that records processes on the host) to find the program that owns those sessions and compare its JA3/JA3S with known toolkit fingerprints; blocking the destination alone leaves the implant on the host, and a user's word cannot show what a program is doing.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic / tls-ac1** (tls-r4) [taught in another room]: The readings show a self-signed internal appliance can be legitimate, but none defines the benign true positive verdict or how it differs from a false positive.
  - Add: When a rule correctly sees what it looks for, such as a real self-signed certificate, and context shows the activity is legitimate (an old certificate, a well-known destination, an IT confirmation), the verdict is a benign true positive: the detection worked and the use is explained, so it is not a false positive, because nothing misfired.
- **tunneling-c2-channels / tunnel-f1** (tunnel-r1): No reading mentions the plink -P option or says that the ports inside a forwarding specification differ from the port of the SSH server itself.
  - Add: In an ssh or plink command, the numbers inside the -L, -R or -D specification are the forwarded ports, while a separate option (-p in ssh, -P in plink) sets the port the SSH server listens on, and that separate port is the destination port a firewall records for the outer connection.
- **tunneling-c2-channels / tunnel-la2 (q2)** (tunnel-r6): No reading says what it means when the owning process of a suspicious connection is a core server engine rather than a separate tool.
  - Add: When a suspicious outbound connection is owned by a core service process such as a database engine or web server, rather than by a separate utility, treat it as more severe: the service itself is doing something it was never built to do, which points to attacker code executing through it, for example after an injection flaw.
- **tunneling-c2-channels / tunnel-la2 (q3)** (tunnel-r6): The playbook stops at the escalate decision and no reading teaches how to respond to a confirmed active compromise.
  - Add: Once a finding has no legitimate explanation and points to an active compromise, contain before you clean: cut the host off from the network while keeping its memory and disk intact as evidence, block the channel at the proxy or firewall, and investigate how the attacker got execution, because blocking one domain leaves the foothold alive, and restarting or reimaging destroys what you need to learn.
- **tunneling-c2-channels / tunnel-qf1** (tunnel-r4) [taught in another room]: No reading names the KQL stdev() aggregation or the term standard deviation, and none states that 443 is the standard HTTPS port; this room has no KQL primer.
  - Add: Standard deviation is one number for how far values spread around their average, so a small value means tightly clustered gaps; in KQL the aggregation is stdev(column), used inside summarize next to count() and avg(), and when you hunt HTTPS traffic you filter on its standard port, 443.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice / uc-q2** (uc-r3): The text calls the BEGIN PRIVATE KEY string the highest-fidelity signature but never says why, or that certificates and signing requests use different headers.
  - Add: High-fidelity means a match is rarely noise: the BEGIN PRIVATE KEY line is a standard header that marks private-key material, while certificates and certificate requests carry their own different headers, though the header says nothing about who made the key or whether it was ever used.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice / uc-la1 (q1)** (uc-r5): No reading explains what a user agent is in CloudTrail, how a Lambda's own calls look, or what it means when a role's temporary credentials are used from outside AWS.
  - Add: A Lambda role's calls normally come from inside AWS and carry an SDK user agent such as Boto3 with an AWS_Lambda exec-env tag, so the same role appearing with a hand-run aws-cli agent from an address outside AWS means its temporary credentials were copied out and are being used from somewhere else.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice / uc-la1 (q3)** (uc-r5): No reading mentions the CloudTrail errorCode or responseElements fields or says that secret values are left out of the log.
  - Add: In a CloudTrail record an empty errorCode means the call succeeded, a failure fills it with the reason, and AWS never writes a retrieved secret into the log, so responseElements stays null on a successful GetSecretValue and proves access without showing the value.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice / uc-ac1** (uc-r6) [taught in another room]: The room never defines benign true positive or how it differs from a false positive, so the learner can see the job is legitimate but cannot choose the verdict label.
  - Add: When an alert correctly describes activity that really happened and an approved ticket or known schedule makes it legitimate, it is a benign true positive; a false positive is a rule misfiring on something it was never meant to catch.
- **use-case-development / ucd-m1** (ucd-read-2) [taught in another room]: The only technique the readings map is T1059.001; none of the five tested mappings, nor the idea that one observable can carry two tags, is taught, so a reader can only match by shared words and elimination.
  - Add: To map what you see to ATT&CK, name the behaviour behind it: encoding a command to hide it is obfuscation (T1027.010, and the same alert can also carry T1059.001 for PowerShell running it); opening LSASS memory steals credentials (T1003.001); trying one password across many accounts is spraying (T1110.003); a scheduled task pointing into a temp folder is T1053.005; and small regular HTTPS check-ins to one host are command and control over web protocols (T1071.001).
- **use-case-development / ucd-log-1 (q2)** (ucd-read-2): No reading explains why a writable temp folder as the working directory, or a cmd.exe parent starting hidden encoded PowerShell, raises confidence; this rule exists only in the task's explanation.
  - Add: Context changes how much a detection matters: legitimate enterprise scripts normally run from known script or application folders, while any user can write to a temp folder, so a shell launching hidden encoded PowerShell from a temp directory is a common attacker staging chain and deserves a higher priority than the encoded flag alone.
- **vpn-monitoring / vpn-q3** (vpn-r1): The reading says the security stack has zero visibility of split-tunnel traffic and never says where evidence of it survives, so the answer is reachable only by eliminating the other options.
  - Add: Traffic that bypasses the tunnel never reaches corporate network controls, but it still happens on the device itself. Evidence of it is found in endpoint telemetry from an EDR (endpoint detection and response) agent, which records file, process and network activity on the laptop wherever it is connected.
- **vpn-monitoring / vpn-ac1** (vpn-r3): No reading says a completed MFA can still be an attacker or what to do when a new-country login is ambiguous, so a careful reader has no basis for not closing it on the MFA success.
  - Add: A login from a new country that completed MFA is ambiguous, not cleared: a code can be stolen in real time by a phishing page, so MFA proves only that a prompt was answered. When nothing confirms the trip, do not close the alert on MFA alone. Escalate to Tier 2, who verify with the user through a verified channel, check physical presence such as badge records, and review what the session reached.
- **vulnerability-management / vm-o1** (vm-r5): No reading lays out the program as an ordered cycle; rescanning appears only inside IT's duties and SLA tracking appears as ongoing work, so the placement of the last stages cannot be read from the text.
  - Add: A mature program runs as a repeating cycle: scanning finds the issues, analysts rank them, owners fix or formally decide on them, a rescan proves the fix worked, and tracking against deadlines then shows what is still stalled.
- **web-application-security / webapp-la2 (q3)** (webapp-r4): The reading says where the real address is visible but gives no response guidance: where a block belongs, pivoting on the source to scope its other requests, or why the code fix and the block are both needed.
  - Add: Once a web attack is confirmed, act where the real address is visible: only the WAF or edge proxy can match it, since the web server sees just the load balancer; pivot on that address to find what else it requested, and also get the flaw fixed in the code, because an attacker can move to a new address but a block buys time while the fix ships.
- **web-application-security / webapp-ac1** (webapp-r4): No reading says that %27 is an encoded apostrophe, that a signature can match an innocent value, what a false positive is, or that the outcome of one is a tuning request.
  - Add: Signatures match characters, not intent: an apostrophe, URL-encoded as %27, in a name such as a surname can trip a SQL injection rule even though no SQL follows it, so decode the value first and look for keywords that would do something after the quote. When a rule fires on harmless input like this it is a false positive, and the right outcome is a rule tuning request instead of an escalation.
- **web-attacks-practice / web-la1 (q3)** (web-r1): No reading says what to do once an injection is shown to have executed; the database audit log is never mentioned.
  - Add: Once status and response size show that an injection executed, handle it as a confirmed breach: use the database's own audit log to establish which rows were actually read, and contain the source, before moving on to code fixes or mass resets.
- **web-attacks-practice / web-f1** (web-r2): No reading mentions the WAF fields httpSourceName and httpSourceId or the type/name/id format of the identifier.
  - Add: An AWS WAF record also names the resource that handed it the request: httpSourceName gives the resource type, for example ALB for an Application Load Balancer, and httpSourceId gives its identifier in the form type/name/id.
- **web-attacks-practice / web-q2** (web-r1): No reading explains how to separate an authorized vulnerability scan from real attacker probing; these discriminators appear only in the task explanation.
  - Add: Authorized vulnerability scanners send real attack payloads, so judge a blocked burst by its context rather than its payloads: does the traffic keep to one unchanging address, does the client announce itself as a scanning product, and does an approved change ticket cover that window? Attacker probing usually fails these checks.
- **web-attacks-practice / web-o1** (web-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading covers the web shell stage: the WAF body-inspection limit, w3wp.exe writing a file, and w3wp.exe spawning cmd.exe are all absent.
  - Add: After an injection reveals enough, an attacker may plant a web shell, a script file placed inside the site: a large POST can pass a WAF when its body exceeds the size the WAF inspects, the IIS worker process w3wp.exe then writes the new file to disk, and when the attacker calls that file w3wp.exe starts a command shell.
- **web-attacks-practice / web-qf1** (web-r2) [taught in another room]: No reading names the child processes to watch for under w3wp.exe; PowerShell, script hosts and living-off-the-land binaries are never mentioned.
  - Add: A web worker has no normal reason to start other programs, so detections watch w3wp.exe for children such as the shells cmd.exe and powershell.exe, the script hosts cscript.exe and wscript.exe, and built-in Windows utilities that attackers repurpose, known as living-off-the-land binaries, such as mshta.exe and certutil.exe.
- **windows-privilege-escalation / privesc-la1 (q3)** (privesc-r3): No reading in this room covers response: nothing says to trace what the elevated process did before rebuilding the host, or to review the admin rights the bypass relies on.
  - Add: Once a UAC bypass is confirmed, stop the process tree, remove the planted registry value, and use Sysmon process ancestry to trace everything the elevated process did before you rebuild anything, because a rebuild destroys that evidence; then ask why the account held the local admin rights the bypass depends on.
- **windows-privilege-escalation / privesc-ac1** (privesc-r5) [taught in another room]: The text teaches how a normal elevation looks but never defines the closing labels, so a reader cannot tell a benign true positive from a false positive here.
  - Add: When a rule correctly catches a real integrity jump that turns out to be authorized, the rule did its job, so the case closes as a benign true positive; a false positive is kept for a rule that misfired on something that did not happen.
- **windows-protocols-lateral / winproto-m1** (winproto-r6): No reading mentions WinRM or RDP, so the WinRM and RDP pairs (ports 5985/5986 and 3389) have no support; the other four pairs are covered.
  - Add: Two common remote-access methods do not use RPC or named pipes at all: WinRM (PowerShell Remoting) carries commands over web protocols on TCP ports 5985 and 5986, and RDP (Remote Desktop Protocol) gives the user a whole remote desktop on TCP port 3389.
- **windows-protocols-lateral / winproto-la1 (q3)** (winproto-r6): The text never says what to do after this pattern: check for an approved change, investigate the source host as well as the account, and keep the evidence on the target.
  - Add: When remote service creation comes from an account with no IT role, first look for a change record or support ticket that covers it; if there is none, handle it as lateral movement by investigating the source host as well as resetting the account, and keep the target intact until you know what the service ran.
- **windows-protocols-lateral / winproto-ac1** (winproto-r6): No reading defines the verdict labels or says that a verified jump host, IT account and open ticket make a correct detection authorised rather than false.
  - Add: A true positive is a rule firing on real malicious activity, a benign true positive is a rule firing correctly on real activity that was authorised, such as ticketed IT work, and a false positive is a rule that misfired on something it was never meant to match. For remote service creation, the deciding checks are the source host, the account, a matching ticket and the identity of the installed service.

## Doubtful answers

- **ai-attacks-soc-detection / aisoc-ac1**: A real, unapproved attempt that failed could defensibly be logged as a low-severity true positive, and the room gives no rule separating that from informational.
- **ai-attacks-soc-llm-risks / aisoc-q8**: The reading defines Excessive Permissions against what the task needs, and a write-capable account is broader than a status lookup needs, so that option is defensible and read-only credentials would also make refunds impossible.
- **asset-context-prioritisation / asset-o1**: Executive laptop first and payment server first are both defensible, and the room itself labels the payment case Priority 1, so the single accepted order is doubtful.
- **auth-identity-monitoring / auth-q1**: The stem's numbers do not add up: 80 events against 75 accounts cannot mean each account was tried exactly once, although the answer still stands.
- **av-vs-edr-masterclass / edr-q3**: The stated answer says full access was granted, but the field and the reading describe DesiredAccess, the mask requested, so the alert alone does not prove the handle was granted.
- **bec-investigation / bec-r2**: The correct option says nothing in the sender's own logs looks wrong, but the reading says that of the target's logs; the compromised vendor's logs may well show it.
- **bec-investigation / bec-la2 (q2)**: A matching ClientIP points to the same actor or infrastructure, but the record carries no sessionId, so the correct option's claim of the same session goes further than the evidence.
- **bec-investigation / bec-q3**: The answer stands, but a correlationId pattern is not a real hunting pivot across mailboxes, because a correlationId belongs to one flow; the source IP, ASN and rule shape are the usable pivots.
- **cloud-attacker-defense-evasion / cad-q2**: The stem calls the sharing step the T1578.001 exfiltration pattern, but ATT&CK files transfer to an attacker-held cloud account under T1537 (Exfiltration), so the label is doubtful even though the chosen option stands.
- **cloud-attacker-defense-evasion / cad-m1**: Pair p4 tags the external share as T1578.001, while that share also fits T1537 Transfer Data to Cloud Account; the eventName match itself is sound.
- **cloud-security-monitoring / cloud-ac1**: The scenario gives no change record or owner check, and the explanation's 'instance the attacker controls' and 'had never attached before' are not in the evidence, so escalate is also defensible.
- **commodity-initial-access / mcia-o1**: The domain and context steps could reasonably swap, since many analysts check for a change ticket before assessing the destination, so the graded order is a convention rather than the only defensible one.
- **credential-attacks-practice / cred-o1**: The last two steps have no fixed order in real intrusions, because an attacker who already knows the target can reach the file server before enumerating groups and shares.
- **credential-attacks-practice / cred-qf1**: The threshold accepts only a fixed list of values, so equally defensible entries such as 12 or 60 are marked wrong.
- **crowdstrike-falcon / cs-falcon-la1 (q3)**: The bundled order puts containment last, yet containing first is defensible when spread is the concern, and the room's own verdict task says to capture memory before killing a process.
- **customer-communication / cc-ac1**: The scenario asks about notifying the client while the verdict is escalate, and with a confirmed detection true_positive is also defensible, since no reading says a P1 notice means escalating to a team lead.
- **detection-engineering / deteng-q2**: Whether the query really moves to firewall data depends on the conversion pipeline: without a logsource mapping a backend may ignore the logsource and search default data, and a mapped pipeline may refuse the rule, so silent non-firing is typical but not guaranteed.
- **detection-rules-tuning / det-rules-q1**: Excluding corporate and VPN egress ranges removes the sources a spray from a compromised internal host would use, so the option's claim of keeping coverage is doubtful even though it is the best of the four.
- **detection-rules-tuning / det-rules-flag1**: The reading's Step 2 says exception list (allow list), so allow list is an equally valid two-word answer that the flag rejects.
- **dlp-fundamentals / dlp-la1 (q3)**: The stem calls the approval check the FIRST step, while the reading and dlp-o1 place it fourth after content, destination and user context.
- **dlp-fundamentals / dlp-ac1**: The false_positive verdict stands, but the sample DetectedValues both fail the Luhn check that the explanation says they pass, so a real card classifier would not have matched them.
- **dns-deep-dive / dns-qf1**: The task allows any threshold from 30 to 200 and any ratio from 0.7 to 0.95, but the accepted lists omit in-range values such as 55 or 0.72, which are marked wrong.
- **dns-deep-dive / dns-ac1**: Nothing positively authorizes the bypass, only the absence of a blocking policy, so Informational is also defensible beside the stated benign true positive.
- **dns-investigation / dns-ac1**: The title offers two outcomes and the key is a third, and benign is arguable on the 8-month-old domain, the Cloudflare hosting and the clean EDR, so the case for escalate depends on a rule the room never gives.
- **edr-detection-investigation / edr-ac1**: The change ticket authorizes a rollout to finance-team workstations, but regsvr32 ran on the deployment server SRV-DEPLOY-07, so the explanation's 'on this host' is not supported by the ticket as written.
- **email-security / email-sec-q2**: SPF is checked against the Return-Path domain and the stem shows only From, so spf=fail proves the server is unauthorised for corp.com only if the envelope domain is also corp.com, which the stem never states.
- **escalation-procedures / esc-log-1 (q1)**: The threat intel lookup has no timestamp, so 'confirmed by about 09:17' is inferred from list order, and the reading's 30 to 60 minute limit could make 09:45 look acceptable to a careful reader.
- **firewall-log-analysis / fw-ac1**: The explanation's own next step is to identify the process with EDR, so firewall data alone leaves the beacon unconfirmed and an escalate verdict is defensible alongside true positive.
- **gcp-security / gcp-la1 (q3)**: The correct option lists removing bindings before disabling keys, while gcp-r7 and its checkpoint teach disabling the key first, so the room gives two orders for the same containment.
- **google-workspace-security / gwsf-ac1**: The event data carries expected_verdict fp and an fp_explanation while the stated correct_verdict is benign_true_positive, so the two records disagree.
- **google-workspace-security / gwsf-o1**: The placement of verify relative to scope_activity is not the only defensible order, since scoping does not depend on verification and the two can run side by side.
- **identity-basics / idbasics-o1**: The reading and its diagram put re-enabling third and the review afterwards, so the order with re-enable before review is also defensible from the text.
- **incident-report-writing / doc-wr1**: The rubric requires current status and impact, but the task context never states them, so the learner must borrow them from the sample sentence in Reading 1.
- **incident-response-methodology / ir-method-q4**: The answer is right, but the explanation attributes the double reset to replication when the reason is that KRBTGT keeps its previous password as valid, so tickets signed with it survive one reset.
- **investigate-alert-workflow / inv7-o1**: The document item says as you go and the explanation admits it runs alongside every step, so its sixth place rests only on the reading's numbering and an earlier place is also defensible.
- **ioc-analysis / ioc-la1 (q1)**: The record labels the IP an exit node, yet a Tor client connects to a guard relay, so the stated answer holds only through the explanation's note that flagged exits often act as guards too.
- **linux-log-analysis / linux-logs-q2**: On Debian and Ubuntu, the systems this room's paths assume, the unit is normally ssh.service, so journalctl -u sshd can return nothing there.
- **linux-log-analysis / linux-logs-q4**: The 02:47 modification time equals the moment entries resume, so it does not show the reading's tell of a time newer than the last entry, and the explanation itself says benign causes must first be ruled out.
- **macos-security-fundamentals / macf-la1 (q1)**: The context puts this write minutes after the installer finished, but pre and postinstall scripts run during the install, so the explanation's 'moments after the install' does not match the timeline unless the script left something running.
- **macos-security-fundamentals / macf-o1**: Only the instruction's own rule fixes this order: macf-r2 says the account check comes before anything else, while here root activity is fourth, and signature and quarantine could swap in practice.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals / malware-q4**: The stem describes a macro added to an .xlsx, but that format cannot store VBA macros, so it should be .xlsm or .xls; the stated answer still holds.
