- **memory-disk-forensics / mdf-o1**: With encryption already running, Reading 6's first factor favours immediate containment, so capture before isolate holds only through the 'if time allows' clause, and network isolation does not by itself stop a process encrypting the local disk.
- **mfa-session-token-attacks / mfast-q4**: By the reading's own limits on browser coverage, T1550.004 replay and AiTM also remain plausible, so fatigue wins only because the rival option adds a false clause; the stem should ask which statement is fully accurate.
- **phishing-analysis / phishing-ord1**: Blocking before purging, or resetting m.okafor before purging, is equally defensible in practice, so only the room's own stated order makes this the single answer.
- **powershell-for-soc-analyst / ps-soc-la1 (q1)**: The keyed option and explanation call j.alvarez a standard user, but nothing in the record shows that, because a SID does not reveal group membership.
- **privileged-access-monitoring / priv-ac1**: The room says a missing ticket means treat as malicious and calls it an escalation trigger, while the answer rests on staying undecided, so true positive and escalate are both defensible unless the text says when to choose each.
- **ransomware-full-lifecycle / rw-la1 (q4)**: The keyed option omits isolating the host and starts domain-wide rotation before scoping, which fits this room's playbook but conflicts with persistence-mechanisms reading persist-r6, which puts credential resets after the hunt.
- **remote-email-collection / remc-o1**: Removing the rule before scoping is doubtful as a strict order, because the rule's parameters are evidence that should be exported first and the two steps can run together.
- **reporting-documentation / rep-ac1**: The incident is also a true positive, so that label is defensible; only the question's focus on what happens before closure makes escalate the single answer, and the explanation's P3 severity cannot be derived from the room.
- **rmm-abuse-remote-access-tools / rmm-q1**: The stem asks for the single biggest advantage, but the answer bundles three advantages and the reading never ranks them.
- **rmm-abuse-remote-access-tools / rmm-q3**: The stem asks for the strongest standalone indicator, but the answer is a combination of two signals.
- **rmm-abuse-remote-access-tools / rmm-o1**: The account, ticket and destination checks have no real dependency on each other, so several orders are defensible and only the reading's numbering makes one correct.
- **rmm-abuse-remote-access-tools / rmm-la1 (q2)**: The context already says the ITSM system shows no ticket for this host today, so the stated next check has already been done.
- **security-products-behaviour / secprod-la1 (q2)**: The keyed step assumes the body can be pulled, but WAF logs and ALB access logs do not store POST bodies, so it exists only if the application logged it; the option is still the best of the four.
- **sentinel-fundamentals / sentinel-q3**: The stem says 'failed logins' without saying Windows, so the SigninLogs option is ruled out only by a missing Account column that no reading mentions; adding 'Windows' to the stem would fix it.
- **soar-automation / soar-ac1**: False positive is also defensible, since no brute force occurred and a rule that reads failures as guessing misjudged the cause, the same logic that makes the impossible-travel VPN case a false positive elsewhere; the rotation 17 hours earlier also does not by itself explain failures spanning only 40 minutes.
- **soc-structure / soc-struct-q3**: The reading gives leading major incidents to Tier 3 and calls in an Incident Response Specialist for major breaches, neither of which is offered, so Tier 2 is only the best of the listed options.
- **tcpip-deep-dive / tcpip-ac1**: The rule matched the repeated RST pattern it describes on real, authorised infrastructure traffic, so benign true positive is as defensible as false positive, and the task should state plainly that the rule was built to find scans.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic / tls-q2**: Near-identical byte counts alone also fit telemetry or health-check polling, so that option is defensibly as weak as a self-signed certificate, and the stated answer holds only because the reading labels byte counts strong.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic / tls-qf1**: The explanation says any floor from 10 to 90 and any ceiling from 5 to 20 is accepted, but the answer lists hold only selected values, so correct entries such as 11 or 35 are rejected.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice / uc-q2**: The keyed wording says the header appears only in genuine key files, but sample keys in documentation and test fixtures carry it too, so near-certain holds for key-shaped content rather than for a real live key.
- **vpn-monitoring / vpn-ac1**: Reading 3 says to disable the account when compromise is suspected and also to contact the user, so true_positive or a contact-first handling can be defended alongside the keyed escalate.
- **vulnerability-management / vm-o1**: SLA tracking and escalation run throughout remediation, so reporting before verification is also defensible; the order shown is one convention.
- **wazuh-fundamentals / wazuh-r2**: The severity table calls levels 4-6 'worth logging but not alerting on', which supports the level-4 distractor until the reader reaches the log_alert_level paragraph below it.
- **web-attacks-practice / web-la2 (q3)**: The correct option calls the WAF the one layer that sees the address, but ALB access logs also record the client IP; blocking at the WAF still stands as the best choice.
- **windows-privilege-escalation / privesc-ac1**: An SCCM push normally runs from ccmcache as SYSTEM with no UAC prompt, so an installer in the user's Downloads folder with a consent prompt does not fit deployment record DPL-4471, which makes the benign verdict shakier than the task implies.
- **windows-protocols-lateral / winproto-la2 (q3)**: AD has no per-user ticket revocation, so 'revoke its tickets' in the stated answer is loose: a password reset leaves issued tickets valid until they expire, and the real actions are disabling the account and purging sessions.
- **windows-protocols-lateral / winproto-qf1**: The task allows a threshold from 5 to 30 and a window of 2 to 15 minutes, but the accepted list omits in-range values such as 11, 18 or 4min, so a correct entry can be marked wrong.

## Too hard

- **cloud-security-monitoring / cloud-ac1**: It needs a recon-then-role-attach chain and the PassRole idea, and no reading walks through either.
- **commodity-initial-access / mcia-o1**: This beginner room asks for a triage sequence that no reading walks through, including a change-record step it never mentions.
- **credential-attacks-practice / cred-o1**: It needs a five-step attack chain that no reading walks through.
- **device-registration-persistence / devreg-o1**: Six ordered remediation steps are asked for, and no reading walks through the sequence or even mentions blocking sign-in.
- **dns-investigation / dns-ac1**: It requires weighing DGA, tunnelling and benign signals against each other, a step no reading walks through.
- **encoding-encryption-hashing / enc-ac1**: A beginner must decode UTF-16LE Base64, read a multi-stage PowerShell script, compare it with the ticket and pick a verdict label, and no reading walks through those steps.
- **exchange-online-security / exch-sec-la1 (q1)**: It needs several unwalked steps: action=none means p=none, the policy belongs to the From domain rather than yours, and SCL 1 sits below the Junk threshold.
- **firewall-log-analysis / fw-ac1**: The verdict needs four signals weighed together, including an unlisted address and a hosting-provider ASN, and no reading walks through that reasoning.
- **linux-fundamentals / linux-fund-la1 (q1)**: For a beginner room it stacks SUID, uid versus euid, the sudoers decision and auth.log, and all of it rests on one sentence of text with no worked record.

- **remote-email-collection / remc-o1**: It needs a six-step response sequence that no reading in the room walks through.
- **sigma-yara-rule-authoring / sigyara-o1**: No reading lays out this eight-step loop: the learner must merge Reading 3's six steps, which have no test step, with Reading 6's testing step and rely on wording cues for the rest.
- **threat-intelligence / threat-intel-la1 (q2)**: Weighing krbtgt resets, disk imaging and domain controller isolation needs incident-response steps that no reading walks through; only the words 'immediate containment' point to the answer.

- **web-application-security / webapp-ac1**: A beginner must decode %27, reason about what a signature matches and choose a verdict label, and no reading walks through any of those steps.
- **web-attacks-practice / web-qf1**: The third blank asks for a script host or living-off-the-land binary, and no reading names any of them or explains the term.

- **vulnerability-management / vm-la1 (q2)**: The correct option announces that it is exactly the pattern from the Reading 3 worked example, and the others rest on always or only.
- **vulnerability-management / vm-la1 (q3)**: The correct option is the only hedged one; every distractor says always, never or only.
- **vulnerability-management / vm-f1**: The prompt names the field and says what format the value takes, so it is a lookup with no understanding needed.
- **web-application-security / webapp-q3**: The stem says the anti-CSRF token is missing and the correct option opens with CSRF, so the name of the attack is handed over.
- **windows-event-logs / win-evtlogs-r1**: The stem asks about seeing the command line and only one option contains the words command line.
- **windows-event-logs / win-evtlogs-q4**: The stem says RDP and the correct option is the only one labelled (RDP), so no logon type needs to be known.
- **windows-event-logs / win-evtlogs-la1 (q2)**: The correct option is by far the longest and the only one listing a full sequence, so it stands out without reading the event.
- **windows-fundamentals / win-fund-q1**: The path in the stem contains the folder name 'attacker', which gives the verdict away before any reasoning about System32.
- **windows-fundamentals / win-fund-la1 (q1)**: The task context calls this parent-child pair highly suspicious and the event description says 'likely malicious Office macro', so the option is already stated.
- **windows-fundamentals / win-fund-f1**: The context and the previous question's stem both name winword.exe as the parent, and the hint names the field.
- **windows-privilege-escalation / privesc-qf1**: All three blanks are single words the context spells out (High, consent.exe, the fodhelper example) and the join logic is already written, so no KQL thinking happens in an advanced room.
- **windows-protocols-lateral / winproto-la2 (q2)**: The stem already says the aggregate is the stronger signal, which removes one option, and the context removes another, so only one choice survives.

## Missing topics

- **active-directory** (ad-r3): Responding to AD attacks. The room teaches detection but not containment, and the krbtgt reset appears only in a task explanation.
  - Draft: Response depends on what was exposed. For Kerberoasting, reset the targeted service account passwords to long random values or move them to group Managed Service Accounts. For DCSync or a Golden Ticket, treat the domain as compromised: escalate to incident response, remove the replication rights, and reset krbtgt twice, waiting for replication and at least the maximum ticket lifetime (10 hours by default) between resets so services keep working. For Pass-the-Hash or Pass-the-Ticket, isolate the host and reset the account.
- **active-directory** (ad-r3): Benign lookalikes of AD attacks. Without knowing the common innocent causes, an analyst will escalate routine directory traffic as an attack.
  - Draft: Several tells have well-known innocent sources. Replication rights in 4662 are normal for Domain Controllers and for the Entra Connect sync account, often named with an MSOL_ prefix, so check any non-DC account against that inventory before calling DCSync. RC4 in 4769 can come from a legacy application, and heavy LDAP queries can come from vulnerability scanners or identity tools. Confirm the source host, its owner and whether the behaviour is new.
- **active-directory** (ad-r3): Windows logon types. LogonType appears in detections and graded tasks, but only type 3 is explained.
  - Draft: The LogonType field in 4624 and 4625 says how the logon happened. Type 2 is at the keyboard, 3 is over the network such as a file share, 4 is a batch job, 5 is a service, 7 is an unlock, 9 is NewCredentials (runas /netonly), and 10 is RemoteInteractive, meaning RDP. Compare the type with the account: a human using type 5 or a service account using type 10 deserves a closer look.
- **active-directory** (ad-r3): Password spraying in Kerberos logs. Spraying often uses Kerberos, so an analyst watching only 4625 will miss it.
  - Draft: Password spraying tries one password across many accounts to stay under lockout limits (T1110.003). When it runs over Kerberos, the Domain Controller logs 4771 with failure code 0x18 for a wrong password, instead of 4625. Look for one source failing against many different accounts in a short window, and for 4740 lockouts in the same period. A success straight after the failures is the account to investigate first.
- **active-directory** (ad-r3): Kerberoasting without RC4. RC4 is the room's only indicator, and it is losing value: Microsoft has announced moves to disable RC4 by default on Domain Controllers, and the current state of that rollout should be verified before teaching it as fact.
  - Draft: Kerberoasting does not require RC4: an attacker can also request AES tickets, which are slower to crack but still crackable if the password is weak. A rule that only looks for 0x17 goes quiet in an AES-only domain. So also detect the behaviour: one requester asking for many service tickets in a short window, or for accounts it never normally uses. A honeypot service account no real system uses gives a very clean alert.
- **ai-attacks-soc-detection** (aisoc-r3): Responding to a successful injection. The room stops at detection, and an analyst needs the first moves once an agent has acted on an attacker's text.
  - Draft: When an injection succeeds, treat the agent like a compromised service account. Disable its tools or take the application offline, rotate any keys and tokens it held, and preserve the gateway logs for that session. Review every action the agent took on the systems it touched, then remove the poisoned ticket, email or document from the source and from the vector index, because it will fire again if it stays there.
- **ai-attacks-soc-detection** (aisoc-r2): Excessive agency (LLM06). It sets the blast radius of every injection and already appears as an answer option without being taught.
  - Draft: OWASP LLM06:2025, Excessive Agency, is the risk of giving an LLM more tools, permissions or freedom than its job needs. It is not an attack by itself, but it decides how much damage a successful injection can do: a summariser with no tools can only produce bad text, while one that can send email can leak data. Analysts should know each agent's approved tool list and service identity, and flag any tool call outside it.
- **ai-attacks-soc-detection** (aisoc-r4): Improper output handling (LLM05). Data often leaves through how an answer is rendered rather than through a visible reply, so output-side monitoring must look for it.
  - Draft: OWASP LLM05:2025, Improper Output Handling, is what happens when an application trusts model output and passes it on unchecked. A common leak channel is a markdown image whose URL carries stolen data: when the client renders the answer, it calls the attacker's server with no click from the user. EchoLeak (CVE-2025-32711) in Microsoft 365 Copilot used this kind of chain from a single crafted email. Watch for responses containing external URLs with long query strings, and for outbound requests from the chat client right after a response.
- **ai-attacks-soc-detection** (aisoc-r3): Evidence for vendor-hosted assistants. Many organisations buy assistants where no custom gateway can be placed; check record names and prompt-text availability against current Microsoft documentation.
  - Draft: Not every organisation runs its own gateway; many buy an assistant such as Microsoft 365 Copilot, where the vendor hosts the model. There the evidence comes from the vendor's audit records: Copilot activity appears in the Microsoft Purview audit log as CopilotInteraction records, showing the user, the time and the resources referenced. The prompt text itself may need separate tooling, so find out what your licence records before an incident. Ask the same questions as with a gateway: who asked, what was retrieved, and what action followed.
- **ai-attacks-soc-llm-risks** (aisoc-r12): What agent activity looks like in logs. The room has no log example at all, so an analyst could name these risks but not recognise one in a console.
  - Draft: An agent investigation starts from the tool-call audit trail. A useful record holds the agent identity, the session ID, the tool name, its arguments, the result, the time and any approval ID. Compare the tool with the agent's documented tool set, and the arguments, such as recipient or record count, with what the task normally uses. Many deployments do not keep this by default, so first find out whether the gateway or orchestrator logs prompts, responses and tool calls at all.
- **ai-attacks-soc-llm-risks** (aisoc-r12): Responding to a misused agent. The readings cover signs and engineering controls but never say what the SOC does once an agent has been abused.
  - Draft: Treat a confirmed agent misuse like a compromised service account. Disable the agent or the abused tool, revoke and rotate the API keys and OAuth tokens it held, and preserve the prompts, retrieved content and tool-call logs. Scope by listing every action that identity took since the malicious content arrived, then remove that content from the ticket queue, mailbox or RAG index, or it will fire again on the next run.
- **ai-attacks-soc-llm-risks** (aisoc-r12): Telling new agent behaviour from abuse. A first-time tool call is the main sign taught, and legitimate releases produce the same signal.
  - Draft: Before calling a new tool call malicious, check the agent's change history, because a new release, an added tool or an approved test explains many first-time calls. Then look at what came just before the call: a human request in the session points to normal use, while newly ingested external content with no request points to injection. Confirm with the agent's owner before closing, and record the approved tool set so the next alert is quicker to judge.
- **ai-attacks-soc-llm-risks** (aisoc-r12): MCP and third-party agent tools. Agents increasingly gain tools through the Model Context Protocol, which widens excessive agency; this area moves fast, so check current OWASP agentic guidance before publishing.
  - Draft: Many agents now connect to tools through the Model Context Protocol (MCP), where a server advertises tools and their descriptions to the model. A malicious or tampered server can hide instructions in a tool description, known as tool poisoning, and a tool that looked safe at approval can change later. So inventory which MCP servers each agent uses, allow-list them, pin versions, and log every tool call as you would any third-party integration.
- **ai-attacks-soc-social** (aisoc-r8): Response when money may have moved. The room stops at prevention, but recovery from a fraudulent wire depends on speed, and an analyst must know the first calls to make.
  - Draft: If a fraudulent transfer may have gone out, time decides the outcome. Tell finance leadership at once and have the bank's fraud desk attempt a recall, giving the account details, amount and time. Open an incident, preserve the call details and any precursor email, and report to law enforcement; in the United States that means IC3, whose teams work with banks on recent transfers. Then check whether a mailbox was also compromised.
- **ai-attacks-soc-social** (aisoc-r11): A worked AI detection use case. r11 claims to build detection use cases yet only names log sources, so a trainee cannot turn one into a working search.
  - Draft: A use case has five parts: what you want to catch, the data source, the logic, the expected benign cases, and the response. For shadow AI in Microsoft Defender: DeviceNetworkEvents | where RemoteUrl has_any ("chatgpt.com", "chat.openai.com", "claude.ai", "gemini.google.com") | summarize Visits = count() by InitiatingProcessAccountName, RemoteUrl. Benign cases are users of the sanctioned, licensed tool, so exclude those accounts by name, and hand the remainder to the shadow-AI response process.
- **ai-attacks-soc-social** (aisoc-r10): Current AI domains. The room and its log use chat.openai.com, but ChatGPT moved to chatgpt.com in 2024, so a hunt built only from this text misses most current traffic.
  - Draft: AI services change addresses and appear constantly, so never hunt from a fixed list in a lesson. ChatGPT now lives mainly at chatgpt.com, with chat.openai.com redirecting there. Keep the domain list in a maintained DLP or proxy category such as a generative AI group, and review it regularly.
- **ai-attacks-soc-social** (aisoc-r8): ATT&CK mapping for these attacks. The room maps only the DLP event, and a ticket on a deepfake call needs a technique name; ATT&CK IDs below are as understood for v19 and should be checked against the platform's verified library.
  - Draft: Tag these cases in ATT&CK so they count in coverage reports. A targeted voice or video call is T1566.004 Phishing: Spearphishing Voice, posing as a trusted person is T1656 Impersonation, and the money loss itself is T1657 Financial Theft. Pasting data into a web AI service maps to T1567 Exfiltration Over Web Service when the data leaves against policy.
- **alert-triage** (alert-triage-r2): Windows logon events. Authentication alerts are the most common triage work, and the tasks use 4625 and 4624 without any reading saying what they are.
  - Draft: Windows records a failed logon as Event ID 4625 and a successful one as 4624. Read LogonType (3 is a network logon, 10 a Remote Desktop logon), the source IP, and on failures the SubStatus: 0xC000006A means a wrong password for a real account, while 0xC0000064 means the account does not exist. Searching for a 4624 from the same source after a burst of 4625s tells you whether an attack succeeded.
- **alert-triage** (alert-triage-r2): Using enrichment results. The room says to check threat intel but not how to read a result, and analysts over-trust both clean and dirty scores.
  - Draft: Tools such as VirusTotal and AbuseIPDB report how many sources flag an indicator, and that count is a lead, not a verdict. One or two detections on a shared hosting or CDN address may be noise, and a clean result on a new attacker domain means little. Check the age of the data, and never upload an internal file to a public service, because uploads can be visible to others.
- **alert-triage** (alert-triage-r2): What to check per alert type. Step 1 gives only two examples, so a Tier-1 analyst has no starting checks for other common alert types.
  - Draft: Match your checks to the alert type. For a reported phishing email, read the SPF, DKIM and DMARC results, the sender, the link or attachment, and who else received or clicked it. For impossible travel, compare device, ASN and VPN use. For data movement, compare volume, destination and the user's normal baseline.
- **alert-triage** (alert-triage-r3): Priority levels. The room requires P1 to P3 tickets and escalates on them without defining them.
  - Draft: Most SOCs turn the priority matrix into levels with response times. As an example, P1 is an active or crown-jewel compromise handled at once, P2 is a confirmed compromise of limited scope handled within hours, and P3 is confirmed suspicious activity with limited reach handled within the working day. Use your own organisation's definitions, and state in the ticket why you chose the level.
- **analyst-mindset** (mind-r6): When to escalate. A Tier 1 analyst's most frequent judgment is whether to hand a case up, and the room only teaches keep digging or close.
  - Draft: Keep digging and close are not the only choices. Escalate when the evidence supports an attack, when the blast radius is larger than you can scope alone, when a high-value asset is involved, or when you have used your time limit without an answer. Escalating with an open question is good work if you hand over what you checked, what you found, what you still do not know and what you would check next.
- **analyst-mindset** (mind-r6): Time-boxing an investigation. A queue never stops, so an analyst needs a limit on digging as well as a reason to dig.
  - Draft: Set a time limit for an alert before you start, and match it to the cost of being wrong. When the limit arrives, stop and choose: close it with your evidence, or escalate it with your notes. An investigation that drifts for an hour without a new question usually needs a second pair of eyes, not more time.
- **analyst-mindset** (mind-r6): Writing the reasoning down. Closing notes are how another analyst checks the conclusion, and the room requires reproducible notes without showing one.
  - Draft: A good closing note fits in a few lines: the hypothesis you tested, the evidence you checked with where it came from, what you could not verify, and your verdict with the reason. For example: tested whether the 03:12 logon was r.patel; badge at 03:05 and calendar entry agree; no earlier 03:00 logons in 30 days, documented; closed as authorized overtime. Anyone reading it can repeat each check without asking you.
- **asset-context-prioritisation** (asset-r7): A written priority matrix and a tie-break. Two analysts should reach the same queue order, and the room gives inputs but no method for combining them.
  - Draft: Most SOCs turn these inputs into a published matrix: severity on one axis, asset tier on the other, and each cell gives a priority such as P1 to P4 with a response target. Confidence then moves an alert up or down a step, and a note records why. When two alerts land in the same cell, a common tie-break is to work first the one whose compromise reaches more systems, then the one with an active business deadline. Use your own SOC matrix, because the cell values are local policy and not a standard.
- **asset-context-prioritisation** (asset-r3): Tier 0 beyond the domain controller. Compromise of these systems is equal to domain compromise, yet an analyst trained here would rank them as ordinary servers.
  - Draft: Several other systems carry the same blast radius as a domain controller and belong in the same top tier. Active Directory Certificate Services can issue certificates that authenticate as other users, Microsoft Entra Connect servers bridge on-premises and cloud identity, and password vaults, EDR consoles and remote-management tools can reach or command most of the fleet. Microsoft calls this control plane Tier 0 in its enterprise access model. Treat an alert on any of them as you would one on a domain controller.
- **asset-context-prioritisation** (asset-r6): Where asset context appears in the console. An analyst can use criticality only if they know where the tools surface it; product names and features change, so check current documentation.
  - Draft: In practice asset context reaches you through enrichment. Microsoft Sentinel can read a list of critical hosts in a query with _GetWatchlist('alias'), Splunk Enterprise Security applies priority through its assets and identities lookups, and Microsoft Defender can mark devices through critical asset management. Learn where these appear in your alert view and how they are maintained, because a tag inherits every weakness of the CMDB behind it.
- **asset-context-prioritisation** (asset-r1): First checks after a failed-logon burst. The room tests the first response to a burst on a domain controller, but the readings never say what to check.
  - Draft: For a burst on a domain controller, first find out whether anything succeeded: look for a 4624 success for a targeted account from the same source after the 4625 failures. Kerberos failures appear on a domain controller as 4771, and lockouts as 4740, so search those too. Many accounts with few tries each suggests password spraying, while many tries on one account suggests brute force. Then investigate the source host, since an internal source may already be compromised.
- **auth-identity-monitoring** (auth-r2): Kerberoasting. The room promises Kerberos-based attacks, yet this is the common one analysts actually meet, and it is absent.
  - Draft: Kerberoasting (T1558.003) lets any domain user request service tickets for accounts with a Service Principal Name, then crack them offline to recover the service account's password. On the Domain Controller it appears as Event 4769, often with Ticket Encryption Type 0x17 (RC4) in a domain that normally uses AES. The strongest pattern is one account requesting tickets for many different services in a short time. Check whether that account has a reason to touch those services, since some legacy applications still request RC4.
- **auth-identity-monitoring** (auth-r1): Spotting Pass-the-Hash. The room names Pass-the-Hash in the logon table but never shows how to recognise it, and it is a core lateral movement signal.
  - Draft: Pass-the-Hash (T1550.002) reuses a stolen NTLM hash instead of a password. On the target it usually appears as a 4624 Logon Type 3 with AuthenticationPackageName NTLM, which alone also matches normal traffic. On the source host, tools commonly leave a 4624 Logon Type 9 with LogonProcessName seclogo. Raise suspicion when an admin account authenticates by NTLM from a workstation it does not normally use, or when one account reaches many hosts in minutes.
- **auth-identity-monitoring** (auth-r3): Benign failed logons and lockouts. Most 4625 and 4740 events are harmless, so an analyst needs a way to clear them quickly and correctly.
  - Draft: Repeated failures on one account usually come from a stale password saved on a phone, a mapped drive or a service. Event 4740 names the Caller Computer, which is the machine that sent the bad attempts, so start there and find the process or device using the old password. Failures from one internal host at a steady rhythm after a password change point to this cause, not to an attack. Failures across many accounts do not fit this explanation.
- **auth-identity-monitoring** (auth-r2): Distributed and slow sprays. The room's signature of one source IP misses how modern sprays run, so an analyst trained on it alone would miss them.
  - Draft: Real sprays often rotate through many IP addresses and spread attempts over hours, so a per-IP threshold never fires. Also group failures by time window and by password-related failure code across all sources, and watch for many accounts each failing once from addresses never seen before. Internet-facing services such as VPN gateways and cloud sign-in are the usual targets, so check those logs as well as the Domain Controller.
- **auth-identity-monitoring** (auth-r2): Responding to a Golden Ticket. The room says how to suspect one but not what to do, and a forged ticket survives ordinary password resets.
  - Draft: A suspected Golden Ticket (T1558.001) means the domain itself is compromised, so escalate at once. Resetting user passwords does not help, because the ticket is signed with the krbtgt key. Recovery means resetting the krbtgt password twice, with enough time between resets for replication and for existing tickets to expire, usually after the attacker's access is removed. Plan this with your Active Directory team, because the resets can disrupt services.
- **av-vs-edr-masterclass** (edr-r7): Telling benign script activity from malicious. Most Office and PowerShell alerts an analyst sees are benign, and the room teaches attack patterns without teaching how to clear one.
  - Draft: Before calling a PowerShell alert malicious, decode the -enc value, then check the signer of any loaded file, the parent process, the user and whether this host normally does this. Admin tools and software deployment also use encoded commands and hidden windows, but they run from known parents under known accounts on a schedule. PowerShell Script Block Logging (Event ID 4104) records the decoded script text, so it can confirm what really ran.
- **av-vs-edr-masterclass** (edr-r5): Response order on a live host. An analyst who reboots or wipes first loses the memory evidence that a fileless attack lives in.
  - Draft: On a Critical endpoint alert, contain the host first, because network containment keeps the agent connected while cutting off C2 and lateral movement. Keep the machine powered on, since injected code exists only in memory and a reboot erases it; collect memory and volatile data through the EDR's remote response tool, such as Falcon Real Time Response. Then pivot on the C2 address, file hashes and user across the fleet to find other affected hosts, and block the address. Clean up only after you know the scope.
- **av-vs-edr-masterclass** (edr-r6): How attackers blind EDR. A quiet sensor can mean the sensor was disabled, and analysts must not read silence as a clean host.
  - Draft: Attackers try to blind EDR before they act. Common methods are patching AMSI inside the PowerShell process, stopping ETW events, and loading a legitimately signed but vulnerable driver to kill security processes from kernel mode, known as bring your own vulnerable driver. ATT&CK v19 groups these under the Defense Impairment tactic (TA0112). Treat a sensor that suddenly goes offline, a new kernel driver load or a stopped security service as an alert in its own right.
- **aws-security** (aws-r7): Rolling back a malicious policy version. An analyst who contains the identity but leaves the attacker's version as default leaves admin rights on every identity the policy is attached to.
  - Draft: If an attacker used CreatePolicyVersion, restore the earlier version with aws iam set-default-policy-version, naming the policy ARN and the old version ID, and use list-policy-versions to see them all. Save the malicious document as evidence before you delete that version. A customer managed policy's default version is the one IAM enforces, and it applies to every user, group and role the policy is attached to.
- **aws-security** (aws-r7): Hunting for attacker persistence. An attacker with admin rights usually creates a second way in, so containment that stops at the known credentials does not end the incident.
  - Draft: After any confirmed identity compromise, search CloudTrail for CreateUser, CreateAccessKey, CreateLoginProfile, AttachUserPolicy, PutUserPolicy and UpdateAssumeRolePolicy made by the compromised identities or their source IPs. UpdateAssumeRolePolicy matters because it can let an outside account assume one of your roles. Treat anything you find as part of containment, not as follow-up work.
- **aws-security** (aws-r5): ConsoleLogin and root account activity. Sign-ins and root use are among the first things an analyst checks in AWS, and their outcome is read differently from other CloudTrail events.
  - Draft: Console sign-ins appear as ConsoleLogin from signin.amazonaws.com. Their outcome sits in responseElements.ConsoleLogin (Success or Failure), and additionalEventData.MFAUsed shows whether MFA was used. Any activity with userIdentity.type Root deserves review, because IAM policies cannot limit the root user and it is rarely needed for daily work.
- **aws-security** (aws-r4): S3 Block Public Access. A bucket normally cannot go public until this control is removed, so the change is an early detection point; confirm the two CloudTrail event names against current AWS documentation before publishing.
  - Draft: S3 Block Public Access overrides public bucket policies and ACLs, and it is on by default for buckets created since April 2023. A bucket therefore usually goes public in two steps, so watch for PutBucketPublicAccessBlock or DeleteBucketPublicAccessBlock shortly before PutBucketPolicy or PutBucketAcl. To respond, remove the public statement, turn the block back on, and use data events or S3 server access logs to learn who read the objects.
- **azure-security** (azure-r6): Role-assignment abuse. The room calls RBAC its most important concept, yet never shows what a malicious role grant looks like, and that is the classic Azure privilege-escalation record an analyst must recognise.
  - Draft: A new role assignment appears in the Activity Log as Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments/write, and the request body holds the principal, the role and the scope. Treat Owner, Contributor or User Access Administrator granted at subscription scope to a new or unusual principal as high priority. Microsoft.Authorization/elevateAccess/action is rarer and more serious: a Global Administrator gives themselves User Access Administrator over every subscription in the tenant. Check who made the change, from which IP, and whether a change ticket covers it.
- **azure-security** (azure-r8): Containing a managed identity. There is no secret to rotate and no session to revoke, so an analyst trained here has no action for the room's own main incident. Not sure of the current token-cache window, so verify it against Microsoft's documentation before publishing.
  - Draft: A managed identity has no password or secret you can reset, so containment means removing its role assignments and locking down or isolating the resource it is attached to. Tokens already issued can stay valid for some time, and Azure caches managed identity tokens, so a removed role may not take effect instantly; check Microsoft's current documentation for the exact window. For a web app, restrict inbound access or stop the app after preserving evidence, then rotate every secret the identity could read.
- **azure-security** (azure-r7): Storage data-plane evidence. After a container is opened the first business question is what was taken, and the room says to review storage logs without showing what they hold.
  - Draft: When diagnostic settings send blob logs to Log Analytics, reads appear in the StorageBlobLogs table. OperationName GetBlob or ListBlobs shows what was done, CallerIpAddress shows from where, Uri shows the object, and AuthenticationType shows how the caller proved itself: Anonymous, SAS, AccountKey or OAuth. Filter the exposed container on Anonymous and SAS for the exposure window to list what left. If these logs were not enabled before the incident, say plainly that the read history cannot be reconstructed.
- **azure-security** (azure-r6): Tampering with logging. Attackers turn logging off before acting, and an analyst must treat a sudden gap as evidence rather than as quiet.
  - Draft: Deleting a diagnostic setting appears in the Activity Log as Microsoft.Insights/diagnosticSettings/delete, and a changed one as Microsoft.Insights/diagnosticSettings/write. Either one on a Key Vault, NSG or Storage Account near other suspicious activity means data-plane visibility may have been removed on purpose. Alert on these operations and compare them with the time your diagnostic logs stop.
- **azure-security** (azure-r4): VNet flow logs replacing NSG flow logs. Microsoft has announced NSG flow logs' retirement (announced date 30 September 2027, verify the current date) in favour of virtual network flow logs, so an analyst will meet both formats.
  - Draft: Microsoft is moving customers from NSG flow logs to virtual network (VNet) flow logs, which are enabled on the network rather than on each NSG. The question they answer is the same: which connections happened and whether they were allowed. Field names and tuple layout differ, so confirm which of the two your environment forwards before you search.
- **bec-investigation** (bec-r2): Checking a suspect message. The room says lookalike-domain evidence lives in the message headers, then never shows how to read them, so one of its four vectors cannot be investigated.
  - Draft: To test a suspicious message, open its full headers and compare the From domain with the Reply-To and Return-Path domains character by character. The Authentication-Results header shows whether SPF, DKIM and DMARC passed and for which domain, and a pass only proves the mail came from that domain, not that the domain is the one you trust. Check when the sending domain was registered, then use message trace to find who else received mail from it.
- **bec-investigation** (bec-r6): What the attacker read and sent. Scoping and the written summary need to state which mail was exposed and which messages the attacker sent; check the tenant's audit licence, because MailItemsAccessed availability has changed over time.
  - Draft: Sign-in logs show that the attacker got in, not what they did. In the Unified Audit Log, MailItemsAccessed records mail that was opened or synced, and Send records messages sent from the mailbox. Filter both by the attacker's IP and the compromise window to list what was read and sent. That list decides who must be notified and which threads the attacker could have learned from.
- **bec-investigation** (bec-r6): OAuth app consent as persistence. A consented malicious app keeps mailbox access after a password reset and session revocation, so containment that skips it leaves the attacker in.
  - Draft: During containment, review the account's enterprise applications and consent grants in Entra ID, along with any Consent to application records in the audit log from the compromise window. An attacker who gets a user to approve an application holds its own token, which does not depend on the user's password. Remove any grant that the user or IT cannot explain.
- **bec-investigation** (bec-r6): Token lifetime after revocation. Analysts must know revocation is not always instant, or they will misread attacker activity seen just after containment; exact behaviour depends on Continuous Access Evaluation support, so confirm for the tenant.
  - Draft: Revoking sessions invalidates refresh tokens, but an access token already issued can keep working until it expires, often around an hour, unless the service supports Continuous Access Evaluation. Keep watching the account after you revoke it. A disabled account or a Conditional Access block closes that window faster.
- **cloud-attacker-defense-evasion** (cad-r7): Containing a compromised cloud identity. Every technique is taught up to detection, but an analyst must also stop it without destroying evidence.
  - Draft: Containment starts with the identity: revoke the role's active sessions, which on AWS adds a deny policy for tokens issued before that moment, because a stolen temporary credential keeps working until it expires. Undo the attacker's changes one by one: RevokeSecurityGroupIngress removes an added rule, and ModifySnapshotAttribute with the remove operation withdraws a snapshot share, though it cannot recall a volume the other account already built. Isolate a suspect instance with a quarantine security group and snapshot its volumes rather than terminating it, because termination can destroy the evidence.
- **cloud-attacker-defense-evasion** (cad-r7): T1685.002 telemetry. cad-r0 promises seven techniques with telemetry, yet log tampering gets only a parenthetical, and it is the step that blinds everything else.
  - Draft: Cloud log tampering leaves its own audit events if you watch for them. On AWS look for StopLogging, DeleteTrail, UpdateTrail and PutEventSelectors in CloudTrail, plus DeleteDetector and UpdateDetector from GuardDuty and DeleteFlowLogs from EC2. On Azure watch for deleted diagnostic settings (Microsoft.Insights/diagnosticSettings/delete), and on GCP for deleted or edited log sinks in the Admin Activity log. Treat any of these as high priority unless a change ticket explains it, and check that the trail covers every Region, because a coverage gap looks the same as silence.
- **cloud-attacker-defense-evasion** (cad-r3): Snapshot sharing as T1537 and its other forms. Analysts tag and hunt this step, ATT&CK files moving data to an attacker-held cloud account under Exfiltration, and the room teaches only the EBS API.
  - Draft: The snapshot itself is T1578.001, but handing it to another account is also T1537, Transfer Data to Cloud Account, under the Exfiltration tactic, so tag both. The same move has other forms: ModifySnapshotAttribute with the group all makes a snapshot public, ModifyDBSnapshotAttribute shares an RDS snapshot, and ModifyImageAttribute shares a machine image. Hunt all of them and compare every account ID against the list of accounts the organisation owns.
- **cloud-attacker-defense-evasion** (cad-r4): Telemetry for T1531 and T1578.003. The reading explains these endgame moves but gives no event names, so an analyst cannot hunt or confirm them.
  - Draft: On AWS, instance deletion lands in CloudTrail as TerminateInstances, and access removal shows as DeleteUser, DeleteLoginProfile, UpdateLoginProfile or DeleteAccessKey from iam.amazonaws.com. In Entra ID, the audit log records events such as Remove member from role, Delete user and Disable account. Because a tenant has no physical console to fall back on, organisations keep emergency break-glass administrator accounts, stored apart from daily admin access and alerted on whenever used.
- **cloud-attacker-defense-evasion** (cad-r2): Azure and GCP compute telemetry. The room claims cross-cloud scope but only firewall changes get Azure and GCP names, so an analyst in those tenants has nothing to search for.
  - Draft: The same moves exist outside AWS under different names. In the Azure Activity Log look for Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachines/write for a new VM, Microsoft.Compute/snapshots/write for a snapshot, Microsoft.Compute/snapshots/beginGetAccess/action for a snapshot export link, and Microsoft.Compute/virtualMachines/delete for a deleted VM. In GCP Admin Activity logs the matching API methods are compute.instances.insert, compute.snapshots.insert and compute.instances.delete.
- **cloud-security-monitoring** (cloud-r1): S3 data events are off by default. The room calls CloudTrail a record of every API call and teaches GetObject detection, but by default a trail does not log object reads, so an analyst could wrongly conclude no data was taken.
  - Draft: CloudTrail logs management events by default, such as CreateUser or PutBucketPolicy. Data events, such as S3 GetObject and PutObject, must be turned on for the trail and may cost extra. Before you say no data left a bucket, confirm data events were enabled for it during that time. If they were not, the absence of GetObject records proves nothing.
- **cloud-security-monitoring** (cloud-r1): Azure and GCP detections. The room's title promise covers all three clouds, yet Azure and GCP get one paragraph each with no detection an analyst could use.
  - Draft: The same attacks appear under other names. In Azure, a new role grant is Microsoft.Authorization/roleAssignments/write in the Activity Log and a widened network rule is Microsoft.Network/networkSecurityGroups/securityRules/write. In GCP Cloud Audit Logs, a permissions change is a SetIamPolicy call, and a new key is google.iam.admin.v1.CreateServiceAccountKey. Ask the same questions in each: who, from where, and was it approved.
- **cloud-security-monitoring** (cloud-r2): Stolen instance credentials used elsewhere. The reading only says the calls come from the instance's own IP, yet attackers usually replay stolen role credentials from their own machines, which is the signal to hunt for.
  - Draft: Stolen instance credentials are often used from outside the instance. Compare sourceIPAddress with the instance's own address: an AssumedRole session whose ARN ends in an instance ID but whose source IP is unknown is a strong sign of theft. GuardDuty reports this as UnauthorizedAccess:IAMUser/InstanceCredentialExfiltration, with OutsideAWS and InsideAWS variants.
- **cloud-security-monitoring** (cloud-r2): Other IAM escalation paths. Attackers often skip CreateUser because it is the best-known alert, so an analyst who knows only that sequence misses most real IAM escalation.
  - Draft: Watch for other high-risk calls: CreateAccessKey for a different user, AddUserToGroup into an admin group, CreatePolicyVersion on an attached policy, UpdateAssumeRolePolicy to let an outside account assume a role, and AssociateIamInstanceProfile or RunInstances passing a powerful role. CloudTrail never logs PassRole as its own event, so the record of the role being passed is the call that used it.
- **commodity-initial-access** (mcia-r2): ClickFix variants beyond Run and PowerShell. The reading says the paste is almost always PowerShell via the Run dialog, so a hunt built on it misses current variants. The variants are from 2025 reporting and may have moved on, so check current threat reports before publishing.
  - Draft: The lure changes faster than the idea. Variants tell the victim to paste into Windows Terminal through Win+X, or into the File Explorer address bar (known as FileFix), and Mac versions ask for a paste into Terminal. The command may start mshta, curl or another built-in tool rather than PowerShell. A paste that did not go through the Run dialog will not be in RunMRU, so hunt for any interpreter launched from the user's shell with a download in its command line, not only explorer.exe starting powershell.exe.
- **commodity-initial-access** (mcia-r2): Responding to each technique. Only the miner reading says what to do next, while the four techniques that lead to stealers and loaders have no response steps.
  - Draft: When a paste-and-run or fake installer ran, isolate the host and save the command, the destination and the second-stage file before cleaning anything. Block the delivery and payload domains and search for other hosts that reached them. Because the next stage is so often an infostealer, treat every password and session saved in that browser as exposed, then reset the passwords and revoke the sessions. Reimage if a loader or stealer executed, since you cannot list everything it fetched.
- **commodity-initial-access** (mcia-r6): Telling these shapes from legitimate activity. The room teaches each shape as suspicious but rarely says what the benign twin looks like, and the same patterns occur daily in normal work.
  - Draft: Each shape has a harmless twin. Chat apps, shared documents and trading dashboards hold WebSockets open for hours, and web apps such as design tools and video editors use WebAssembly and heavy CPU. What separates a miner is the combination: sustained CPU the page's job does not explain, plus a long connection to a domain with no relationship to the site. Ask what the user was doing, and check whether the script host belongs to the site.
- **commodity-initial-access** (mcia-r3): Finding a clipper on a host. A graded question tells investigators to check for a clipboard listener, but the room never says how, and ordinary Windows logs do not record those API calls.
  - Draft: Windows does not log clipboard listener calls by default, so hunting starts from the side effects. Check startup registry keys for a recently added entry that points to a new binary, and look for a hidden cmd.exe running reg add from an installer. Where Sysmon is configured for it, Event ID 24 records clipboard changes. A simple test on an isolated host is to copy a sample wallet address, paste it into Notepad and compare the two strings.
- **commodity-initial-access** (mcia-r5): Checking Mark-of-the-Web on a file. The reading's key question is whether the file that ran carried its own Zone.Identifier, but it never shows how an analyst answers that.
  - Draft: To read the mark, run Get-Content on the file with -Stream Zone.Identifier in PowerShell: ZoneId=3 means the internet zone, and an error means the file has no mark. Sysmon Event ID 15 records when such a stream is created. For ISO delivery, also look for a process started from a drive letter other than C: shortly after an .iso file reached Downloads, which is what a mounted image looks like in telemetry.
- **credential-attacks-practice** (cred-r2): Confirming success and responding. The dangerous outcome of any credential attack is the login that worked, so an analyst who only classifies failures misses the compromise.
  - Draft: Classifying the burst is only the start. Search for 4624 successful logons from the same sources against every targeted account, then block or rate-limit the source. Treat any account with a match as compromised: reset its password, end its sessions and review what it did after the logon. Also find out why lockout did not stop the attempts.
- **credential-attacks-practice** (cred-r2): Benign repeated failures. Most repeated-failure alerts are not attacks, and an analyst with no benign patterns will over-escalate.
  - Draft: Common benign causes include a service still using a rotated password, a phone holding an old password after a change, and a user who forgot a new password. These usually come from known internal hosts or the user's own devices, and they often start right after a change. Check the sources against your asset list and your change tickets. Close a confirmed case citing the ticket, and do not weaken the rule.
- **credential-attacks-practice** (cred-r2): Kerberos failures in Event 4771. A spray over Kerberos against domain accounts does not show up as the 4625 pattern this room teaches, so an analyst would miss it.
  - Draft: Not every failed password produces a 4625. When an attacker sprays over Kerberos, the domain controller logs Event ID 4771, Kerberos pre-authentication failed, and failure code 0x18 means a wrong password. Apply the same two axes: count distinct TargetUserName values per client address. If you hunt only on 4625, a Kerberos spray stays invisible.
- **credential-attacks-practice** (cred-r2): Cloud sign-in failures. Spraying and stuffing mostly hit internet-facing identity services, so analysts must read the same shapes in Entra ID logs as well as Windows logs.
  - Draft: In Entra ID sign-in logs the same shapes appear with different fields. Group on UserPrincipalName and IPAddress, and read ResultType for the outcome: 50126 means invalid username or password, and 50053 means the account is locked. A spray shows one or a few IPs across many users with 50126. Stuffing shows many IPs, about one attempt per user, and an unusually high number of ResultType 0 successes.
- **credential-attacks-practice** (cred-r2): Detecting credential dumping. The room says dumping never appears in 4625 but stops there, so an analyst has no way to find it; GrantedAccess values vary by tool, so treat them as a lead and confirm with your EDR.
  - Draft: LSASS memory dumping (T1003.001) appears on the host, not in logon events. With Sysmon, look for Event ID 10 where TargetImage is lsass.exe, then read SourceImage to see which program opened it and GrantedAccess to see what rights it asked for. Security tools touch lsass.exe legitimately, so judge the source process and its path. An unknown binary, rundll32.exe or a tool in a user folder opening lsass.exe needs escalation.
- **crowdstrike-falcon** (cs-falcon-r2): Searching raw telemetry. Scoping an alert across hosts means querying events directly, and the room never shows an event name or a query; console menu names and search products change by licence and release, so confirm them against the current tenant.
  - Draft: Beyond the detection card, Falcon stores raw sensor events that you can search. Each event has an event_simpleName, such as ProcessRollup2 for a process start, DnsRequest for a DNS lookup and NetworkConnectIP4 for an outbound connection, and aid identifies the host. A process's TargetProcessId matches the ContextProcessId on events it later caused, so you can join a process to its DNS and network activity. To scope, search one command line, hash or domain across every aid.
- **crowdstrike-falcon** (cs-falcon-r2): Triage, disposition and tuning. An analyst must close detections correctly and know whether Falcon actually stopped the activity; status names differ between the older Detections page and the newer Alerts view, so confirm the current list.
  - Draft: Read the disposition before anything else, because it tells you whether Falcon only observed the activity or also stopped it, for example by killing the process. Assign the detection, investigate it, and close it with a status and a comment stating your evidence. When a trusted tool keeps firing, add a narrow exclusion scoped to the exact path, command line or host group, with an owner and a reason. Never exclude a whole product or folder to quiet the queue.
- **crowdstrike-falcon** (cs-falcon-r3): RTR permissions and memory capture. Analysts need to know which actions their role allows, and the room's verdict task expects memory capture without teaching it; confirm the role-to-command mapping against current Falcon documentation.
  - Draft: RTR access is split by role. Read-only commands such as ps, netstat and reg query suit first looks, Active Responder adds actions such as get, kill and memdump, and RTR Administrator adds put and run for pushing and starting tools. Memory disappears when a process dies, so when you need an injected payload or decrypted strings, capture memory before you kill the process.
- **crowdstrike-falcon** (cs-falcon-r3): Macro chains and process injection. These are among the most common critical detections, and the room grades them without explaining them.
  - Draft: Office applications almost never start PowerShell or cmd.exe during normal work, so a child process like that usually means a malicious macro ran. Process injection (T1055) places code inside another running process, such as explorer.exe, so that the malicious activity appears to come from a trusted program. In Falcon, a request for full access to another process is the clue: treat it as serious when the requesting process has no debugging or support reason to make it.
- **customer-communication** (cc-read-2): Who to contact and verification. A P1 call to the wrong or unverified person can leak client data or warn an attacker, and the room never says who receives the call.
  - Draft: Each client should supply a contact list with named people, phone numbers, an escalation order and after-hours cover. Call in that order and log every attempt. If someone calls asking for details, verify them against the list before sharing anything. If the client's email or chat may be compromised, stay on the phone or a separate channel, and never put one client's data into another client's update.
- **customer-communication** (cc-read-2): What a P1 call says. The room requires a phone call in 15 minutes but never shows what is said on it.
  - Draft: A P1 call has a short shape: who you are, what is happening in one plain sentence, what you have already done, and what you need from them now. For example, say that ransomware is spreading on two of their file servers, that you have isolated both, and that you need their approval to isolate a third. Close by agreeing the time of the next update, then record the call on the ticket.
- **customer-communication** (cc-read-2): When the client still refuses. The reading says to hold your ground but not who owns the decision or what happens if the client says no.
  - Draft: It is the client's environment, so check the contract for what the SOC may do without approval. If they still refuse after you explain the risk, escalate to your team lead or service manager and record who decided, when, what risk you stated and what they chose. Keep monitoring and raise it again if the situation worsens. A written risk acceptance protects both sides.
- **customer-communication** (cc-read-3): Stating confidence and keeping a cadence. Early notices are often wrong, and guesses stated as facts cost more trust than a delay.
  - Draft: Separate what you know, what you assess and what you do not know yet, and use confirmed and suspected with care. Do not name an attacker or say no data was taken until the evidence supports it. During an active incident, state when the next update will come and send it on time even if nothing is new. Correct any earlier statement openly when the facts change.
- **customer-communication** (cc-read-3): Legal and regulatory boundaries. An analyst's wording can affect the client's legal duties, and deciding those duties is not the SOC's job.
  - Draft: Whether an incident is a reportable breach is for the client's legal team, so report facts and use the word breach only as your contract or lead directs. Deadlines may apply to the client: for example, GDPR gives a controller 72 hours to notify the regulator after becoming aware of a reportable personal data breach. Give the client the facts quickly so they can meet those duties, and route legal questions through your lead.
- **cyber-kill-chain** (ckc-r3): Working the chain in both directions. An alert shows one stage, and the analyst needs to know how to use that stage to plan the investigation and judge urgency.
  - Draft: An alert tells you one stage, so use it to plan the rest of the investigation. Look backward for how the attacker got in, such as the phishing email, because the same delivery may have reached other users. Look forward for the next stages, and treat a later stage as more urgent, since a host at C2 means the attacker already has a foothold. Stages 1 and 2 mostly happen outside your network, so you rarely see them in your own logs and depend on threat intelligence.
- **cyber-kill-chain** (ckc-r3): The six courses of action. The defender matrix in the original paper is what turns stages into decisions, and the room's table gives controls without it.
  - Draft: The original Lockheed Martin paper pairs each stage with six defender actions: detect, deny, disrupt, degrade, deceive and destroy. Detect sees the activity, deny prevents it, disrupt interrupts it in progress, degrade slows it, deceive feeds the attacker false information, and destroy removes their capability. When you review coverage, ask which of these you have at each stage, not only whether you can see it.
- **cyber-kill-chain** (ckc-r1): What happens inside the network. Much of a SOC queue is post-compromise activity the seven stages do not model; the room names the limit but not how analysts work around it.
  - Draft: Real intrusions repeat the cycle inside the network: after a foothold the attacker does internal reconnaissance, steals credentials and moves to other hosts before acting on the objective. The Unified Kill Chain, published by Paul Pols in 2017, extends the model to 18 phases to cover this. For an analyst, one incident can therefore show several small chains, one per host, and the visible impact can come days after the first foothold.
- **data-staging-exfiltration-channels** (dsx-r4): Host telemetry for staging and archiving. The room names only 5145 and firewall records as real logs, while the two middle stages of the chain are found in process and file events.
  - Draft: Process-creation records (Sysmon Event ID 1, or Windows Event ID 4688 with command-line logging on) show the Robocopy and archiver command lines, and Sysmon Event ID 11 (FileCreate) shows the archive appearing on disk. Defender for Endpoint exposes the same facts in DeviceProcessEvents and DeviceFileEvents. Query these first when walking back from an egress alert, because they give you the account, the parent process and the exact path in one place.
- **data-staging-exfiltration-channels** (dsx-r8): Scoping what was taken. The workflow ends at containment, but legal and the data owner need to know what data left.
  - Draft: Before closing containment, work out what was actually taken: the 5145 records give the paths touched, the archive size or bytes sent give the volume, and a captured command line can supply the password for a recovered archive. Block the destination, isolate the host without powering it off, and hand the file list to legal and the data owner, because notification duties depend on what the data was.
- **data-staging-exfiltration-channels** (dsx-r6): Detecting chunked transfers. T1030 is taught as a way past volume rules, with no method for catching it.
  - Draft: Chunking defeats rules that judge one session at a time, so add up bytes per host, account and destination over hours or days instead. Many small uploads of near-identical size to one destination, often at a steady interval, are the signature. This catches what a per-session size rule misses, at the cost of more tuning for sync clients and backups.
- **data-staging-exfiltration-channels** (dsx-r6): Finding Rclone on a host. The room calls Rclone a strong signal but never says what it looks like in telemetry, and attackers often rename it.
  - Draft: Rclone leaves a process record with a telling command line, such as copy or sync followed by a local path and a remote name, and it normally keeps its remotes and tokens in an rclone.conf file in the user's profile. Attackers often rename the binary, so match on the original file name or hash and on arguments like --transfers, not only on rclone.exe. On the network it appears as sustained HTTPS to the provider's API hosts.
- **data-staging-exfiltration-channels** (dsx-r6): Detecting DNS tunnelling. DNS tunnelling is called the hardest channel to catch, yet the room gives one indicator and no method.
  - Draft: Review DNS logs per parent domain rather than per query: a high count of unique subdomains, long labels of random-looking characters and heavy use of TXT or NULL records all point to tunnelling. Compare against the host's normal lookups, and check the parent domain's age and owner. Zeek dns.log, resolver logs or Sysmon Event ID 22 give you the query names.
- **defender-xdr** (def-xdr-r2): Closing an incident: classification and tuning. The room stops at containment, so an analyst does not know how to record the outcome or stop a benign pattern from repeating. Portal labels change over time, so confirm the current wording.
  - Draft: Every incident ends with a classification: True positive, Informational expected activity, or False positive, with a determination such as the threat type. The label feeds reporting and tuning, so choose the one that matches what was found. Authorized activity that really happened is expected activity, not a false positive. For a repeating benign pattern, create a narrow alert tuning rule for that exact file, account or device rather than broadening an exclusion.
- **defender-xdr** (def-xdr-r2): Automated investigation and the Action center. AIR acts on devices and mail without an analyst, so a working analyst must know where to approve, review or undo its actions.
  - Draft: Automated investigation and response starts an investigation when certain alerts fire and proposes actions such as quarantining a file or removing a mail message. Depending on the automation level, these run automatically or wait for approval in the Action center. The analyst reviews pending actions there and can undo completed ones from the History tab, so check it before repeating work or concluding that nothing was remediated.
- **defender-xdr** (def-xdr-r2): Email investigation in Defender for Office 365. The room promises Office 365 investigation but gives only a product row, while phishing is the most common incident start.
  - Draft: For a phishing incident, open Threat Explorer and search by sender, subject or URL to find every recipient, not just the one who reported it. Check whether mail was delivered, blocked, or removed by zero-hour auto purge (ZAP), which pulls already delivered messages when a verdict changes. In Advanced Hunting, UrlClickEvents shows who clicked a Safe Links URL, and that list is your compromised-account candidates. Remediate the remaining copies from Threat Explorer.
- **defender-xdr** (def-xdr-r3): Advanced Hunting limits and custom detection requirements. An analyst must know how far back hunting reaches and why a saved query can fail to become a rule. Frequency options have changed recently, so confirm them in the portal.
  - Draft: Advanced Hunting keeps 30 days of data, so older activity needs the device Timeline or a long-term store such as Microsoft Sentinel. A custom detection query must return Timestamp and identifying columns such as DeviceId and ReportId, or the rule cannot be saved. Rules can run on a set frequency, and for some tables continuously, so match the frequency to how quickly the alert needs to fire.
- **defender-xdr** (def-xdr-r3): Identity investigation data. The room's identity side is an alert table, so an analyst cannot verify an MDI alert or hunt around it.
  - Draft: Defender for Identity sensors run on domain controllers, so they see authentication and directory traffic from every client, including unmanaged ones. Beyond IdentityLogonEvents, IdentityQueryEvents shows LDAP, DNS and SAMR queries against AD, and IdentityDirectoryEvents shows changes such as group additions. Use them to confirm a reconnaissance or Kerberoasting alert and to find which account and source device were involved.
- **detection-engineering** (deteng-r4): Writing rules that resist evasion. The room's model rule is easy to bypass, and an advanced learner should be able to see why.
  - Draft: Test each rule against how an attacker would dodge it. PowerShell accepts shortened parameters such as -e and -ec, so a list holding only -enc and -EncodedCommand misses them, and pwsh.exe or a renamed copy escapes Image|endswith. Match on the OriginalFileName field where your telemetry has it, and aim at behaviour that is costly to change rather than at one string.
- **detection-engineering** (deteng-r3): Validating with adversary emulation. Stage 3 says to test against simulations but gives no method, and only precision is measured, so missed attacks stay invisible.
  - Draft: Run a known test of the technique, for example an Atomic Red Team test for T1059.001, in a lab and confirm the rule fires with the expected fields. Keep that test and re-run it after every rule, parser or logging change, so a broken rule is found before an attacker finds it. Precision shows how noisy a rule is; only such tests show what it misses.
- **detection-engineering** (deteng-r2): Sigma correlation rules. The reading calls its spray example legacy syntax but never shows the current form, so a learner cannot write the room's central rule.
  - Draft: Current Sigma expresses a threshold as a separate correlation rule that points at a base rule. For the spray, the base rule matches the 4625 events, and the correlation sets type value_count, group-by IpAddress, a timespan such as 5m, and a condition of gte 10 on the field TargetUserName. Keeping the two apart lets you reuse the base rule and convert the threshold with pySigma where the backend supports correlations.
- **detection-engineering** (deteng-r2): What the heatmap does not prove. Analysts need to know a covered cell can still be blind, for example when the log source is not being collected.
  - Draft: A coloured heatmap cell only says a rule is tagged with that technique. It does not prove the log source is onboarded, that the fields parse, or that the rule catches every variant of the technique. Before trusting a cell, confirm the data is flowing and that a test of the technique fires the rule.
- **detection-rules-tuning** (det-rules-r2): A real rule query. The room teaches rule writing but shows no query with the threshold in it, which is what an analyst actually writes.
  - Draft: In Microsoft Sentinel the spray logic looks like this: SigninLogs | where ResultType == "50126" | summarize Users=dcount(UserPrincipalName), Failures=count() by IPAddress, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m) | where Users > 10. ResultType is a string column, so the value goes in quotes. dcount counts distinct accounts, which separates a spray from one user retrying. In Splunk the same idea uses stats dc(user) by src.
- **detection-rules-tuning** (det-rules-r1): Testing with simulated attacks. History shows how noisy a rule is, not whether it catches the attack, so an untested rule may never fire.
  - Draft: Running a rule on history shows its noise, but if the attack is not in that data it proves nothing about detection. So also trigger the behavior on purpose in a test environment, for example with an Atomic Red Team test mapped to the ATT&CK technique, and confirm the alert fires with the expected fields. Keep the test and re-run it after changes to the rule or the log source.
- **detection-rules-tuning** (det-rules-r3): Owned exceptions. Exceptions are a common way rules quietly lose coverage, and the room says owned without saying what that requires.
  - Draft: Every exception needs an owner, a reason, a ticket and a review or expiry date, because the account or host it trusts can change or be compromised. Make it as narrow as the evidence allows by combining fields such as account, host and destination. Review exceptions on a schedule and remove those no longer needed.
- **detection-rules-tuning** (det-rules-r1): Behavior over indicators. Rules built on hashes or exact strings break on trivial change, so an analyst must know what makes a rule durable.
  - Draft: A rule on a hash or one IP address breaks as soon as the attacker changes it, while a rule on behavior, such as Word starting PowerShell, is costly to evade; this idea is called the Pyramid of Pain. Command-line rules need care too, because PowerShell accepts -e, -enc and -EncodedCommand for the same parameter. Prefer fields that are harder to change, such as the parent process and original file name, over the process name alone.
- **detection-rules-tuning** (det-rules-r3): Detection health. A rule can stop working with no error, and a quiet rule looks the same as a working one.
  - Draft: A rule that never fires may be quiet or may be broken, because a renamed field, a parser update or a stopped log source fails with no error. Check on a schedule that each important data source still arrives and that each rule's test still triggers, and alert when a source goes silent. Treat these checks as part of the Monitor step of the lifecycle.
- **device-registration-persistence** (devreg-r3): Removing a method in practice. The room insists on explicit removal but never says where an analyst does it or what to record first.
  - Draft: In the Entra admin center, open the user and then Authentication methods to list and delete individual methods; Microsoft Graph offers the same through the user's authentication methods endpoints. A rogue device is disabled or deleted under Devices, which also stops it satisfying device-based policy. Record each method's details before deleting it, because it disappears from the live view afterwards.
- **device-registration-persistence** (devreg-r2): Other audit records for method changes. An analyst searching one operation name will miss related records; exact record shapes vary, so verify on a live tenant which operations carry the StrongAuthentication properties.
  - Draft: Method changes also appear under other operations, such as an admin registering or updating security info for a user, a user deleting security info, and Update user records in Core Directory. Search by target user and time window, not only by one operation name. Treat removals as seriously as additions, because an attacker may delete the victim's own factor to lock them out.
- **device-registration-persistence** (devreg-r2): Spotting a rogue device object. Authentication methods get full field-level treatment, while the device path has no evidence to read.
  - Draft: For a new device, compare its display name, operating system, join type, owner and registration time with the user's known hardware. Attackers often use a generic name or copy an existing one. Apply the same correlation as for a new method: a registration from an unfamiliar IP close to a suspicious sign-in deserves escalation, and an organisation-issued device will normally also appear in its management tool such as Intune.
- **device-registration-persistence** (devreg-r5): Preventing registration from risky sessions. The room shows a replayed session adding a factor with no fresh challenge but no control that stops it.
  - Draft: Conditional Access can target the user action Register security information, so new methods can be added only from a compliant device, a trusted location or under a stronger check. This closes the path where a replayed session with an old MFA claim registers a factor with no fresh challenge. A Temporary Access Pass gives genuinely locked-out users a controlled way to re-register.
- **digital-forensics-basics** (dfir-r1): Triage collection in a SOC. Most SOC work uses targeted collection rather than full images, and the room shows only the full-image path. Tool features change, so check current versions.
  - Draft: A full disk image takes hours, so SOC teams usually start with targeted triage: tools such as KAPE, Velociraptor or an EDR's live-response console pull the key artefacts in minutes. On a live host, isolate it from the network rather than powering it off, so memory survives. Record the commands you run, because every action on a live system changes it a little.
- **digital-forensics-basics** (dfir-r3): Windows Event Logs as evidence. The room cites event log analysis as part of the narrative but never says where the logs are or which matter.
  - Draft: Event logs are stored as .evtx files in C:\Windows\System32\winevt\Logs and can be copied from an image. Start with Security 4624 and 4625 for logons, 4688 for process creation, 4698 for a scheduled task created, and System 7045 for a new service. Security 1102 means the log was cleared, which is itself a finding, so check off-host copies in the SIEM.
- **digital-forensics-basics** (dfir-r3): Registry and persistence artefacts. Finding how an attacker stayed on the host is central to disk forensics, and no reading covers it.
  - Draft: The registry is stored in hive files: SYSTEM, SOFTWARE and SAM in C:\Windows\System32\config, and NTUSER.DAT in each user's profile. Check the Run and RunOnce keys, services under SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services, and scheduled tasks in C:\Windows\System32\Tasks. A persistence entry pointing to a user folder or Temp, or created inside the incident window, deserves a closer look.
- **digital-forensics-basics** (dfir-r3): Timestomping. The room builds timelines on $MFT times without warning that attackers can change them.
  - Draft: Attackers can alter file times (Timestomp, T1070.006) to hide a file among old system files. The $MFT holds two sets of timestamps: $STANDARD_INFORMATION, which common tools can change, and $FILE_NAME, which is much harder to alter. If $STANDARD_INFORMATION shows a date earlier than $FILE_NAME, treat the times as suspect and confirm them with other artefacts such as Prefetch or the USN journal.
- **digital-forensics-basics** (dfir-r1): Encryption and SSD limits. These decide whether powering off destroys access to the evidence, and the room does not mention them.
  - Draft: Full-disk encryption changes the order of work: if BitLocker is on and the machine powers off, an image is unreadable without the recovery key.
