Secure the key, or take a logical copy while the system is running. On SSDs, TRIM can clear deleted data on its own, so deleted files may not be recoverable however quickly you act.
- **discovery-and-enumeration** (disc-r2): Seeing commands that write no Security event. The reading implies whoami and quser leave no Security event but never says where an analyst finds them.
  - Draft: whoami, quser and ipconfig usually write no 4798 or 4799 event, so you see them only through process-creation logging: Sysmon Event ID 1, EDR, or Windows Event ID 4688 with command-line auditing enabled. Look for several such commands from one parent within seconds. The parent matters: whoami under w3wp.exe or winword.exe is a very different story from an administrator's own shell.
- **discovery-and-enumeration** (disc-r2): net.exe hands off to net1.exe. Real logs usually show net1.exe as the 4799 caller, as vendor rules keyed on that name show, while the room shows net.exe; confirm on a lab host before changing the examples.
  - Draft: net.exe usually hands the work to a child process, net1.exe, so process logs show net.exe followed by net1.exe, and Event 4799 commonly records CallerProcessName as net1.exe. Search for both names, or a rule can miss the activity. Collectors such as SharpHound also produce 4799, often across many hosts in a short window.
- **discovery-and-enumeration** (disc-r2): Wider host reconnaissance. Real recon bursts mix these commands with the identity checks the room teaches, and analysts must recognise the whole set.
  - Draft: Attackers also ask what the host is and what protects it: systeminfo (System Information Discovery, T1082), ipconfig /all and route print (System Network Configuration Discovery, T1016), tasklist (Process Discovery, T1057), and checks for installed security products (Software Discovery, T1518). One of these alone means little. Several from the same parent within a minute, on a user workstation, is the pattern.
- **discovery-and-enumeration** (disc-r5): Denied calls in cloud enumeration. A stolen key testing its rights fails often, and the room only teaches successful read-only calls.
  - Draft: In cloud enumeration, failures are evidence too. A stolen key testing its rights produces bursts of AccessDenied or UnauthorizedOperation in CloudTrail's errorCode field across services it never normally touches, often after sts:GetCallerIdentity, the cloud equivalent of whoami. Successful calls show what the attacker learned, and denied calls show what they tried to reach.
- **discovery-and-enumeration** (disc-r7): Responding to confirmed discovery. The room ends at escalate, while discovery is the cheapest moment to stop an intrusion.
  - Draft: Confirmed hostile discovery means the attacker is active now, so act before Lateral Movement or Collection begins. Isolate the source host, or revoke sessions and rotate keys for a cloud identity, and preserve the process and API logs. Search for the same source touching other hosts or shares. Use what was enumerated to predict the next target, and watch those accounts and shares closely.
- **dlp-fundamentals** (dlp-r6): Responding when data really left. The room stops at the verdict, but an allowed event with real data needs action from the analyst.
  - Draft: When sensitive data has really left, the job moves from triage to response. Remove what can still be removed: revoke the share link, ask in writing for the external copy to be deleted, and rotate any exposed secret. Preserve the evidence, including the file, the user's recent activity and the device, before confronting anyone. Bring in legal or privacy early, because regulated data can start a breach-notification clock: GDPR gives a controller 72 hours to notify the authority after becoming aware.
- **dlp-fundamentals** (dlp-r4): What DLP cannot see. An analyst who trusts a quiet DLP console misses the common evasions.
  - Draft: DLP can only judge content it can read. Password-protected archives, encrypted files and often text inside images may pass unscanned unless a policy treats unscannable files as a risk. Splitting data into small files can also stay under a minimum-count threshold, so many small matches from one user deserve a look. The absence of an alert is therefore not proof that nothing left.
- **dlp-fundamentals** (dlp-r6): Tuning false positives safely. Cases like the procurement spreadsheet need a fix that cuts noise without opening a hole.
  - Draft: Closing a false positive should end in a tuning request, not just a closed ticket. Prefer narrow changes: add required supporting keywords, raise the minimum count or confidence for that rule, or exclude one named sender-and-recipient flow with an owner and an expiry date. Avoid switching the rule off or excluding a whole department, since that removes protection for real data too. After the change, confirm a known-bad test file still matches.
- **dlp-fundamentals** (dlp-r2): Working DLP alerts in the console. The room shows raw fields only, while an analyst works from the portal; portal menu names change often, so verify the labels before publishing.
  - Draft: In Microsoft Purview, DLP alerts are reviewed in the Data Loss Prevention alerts view, and Activity explorer shows the underlying matches with the file, user, policy and action. The same alerts also flow into the Microsoft Defender portal, where they can be investigated beside EDR and identity alerts as one incident. Viewing the matched content itself is restricted, so an analyst may need a specific role to open the file.
- **dns-deep-dive** (dns-r6): Responding to tunneling and DGA. The room is strong on detection but gives no response steps, so a trained analyst could identify the channel and not know how to act.
  - Draft: Once a tunnel or DGA is confirmed, use Sysmon to find the process and its parent, and block or sinkhole the domain at the resolver. Isolate the host and keep its evidence, because cutting the channel does not remove the implant. For a tunnel, estimate what left from query counts and sizes. For a DGA, the few domains that resolved are the live infrastructure, so search all hosts for them.
- **dns-deep-dive** (dns-r3): Benign lookalikes for tunneling. Security products generate long encoded queries all day, and an analyst who does not know this will raise false tunneling incidents.
  - Draft: Not all long, high-entropy, unique subdomains are tunnels. Antivirus and reputation services often send a file or URL hash as a DNS lookup, and mail servers do the same with DNS blocklists. These show long unique labels under one parent domain, which is the tunneling shape. Identify the parent's owner and the querying process before you escalate, then add a narrow allowlist entry for that parent domain.
- **dns-deep-dive** (dns-r6): Resolver logging sources. Many organisations have neither Zeek nor Sysmon everywhere, so analysts must know what the resolver itself can give them; check the event numbers against the Windows Server version in use.
  - Draft: If you run Windows DNS Server, query logging is not on by default. DNS analytical logging records queries and responses as events in the Microsoft-Windows-DNSServer Analytical channel, while the older debug log writes to a text file. A resolver log shows the client IP, name and record type but not the process. In the cloud, use your provider's resolver logs, such as Route 53 Resolver query logs or Azure DNS Analytics sources.
- **dns-deep-dive** (dns-r2): Finding unsanctioned encrypted DNS. The room explains the DoH blind spot but gives analysts little on how to find hosts using it; confirm browser behaviour against current Firefox and Chrome documentation.
  - Draft: To find DoH use, compare proxy or firewall logs with a list of public DoH provider domains and IPs, looking for long-lived sessions with small, steady requests. A host active on the web but almost absent from resolver logs is a strong sign. Firefox checks the canary domain use-application-dns.net before enabling DoH by default, so a network can signal it to stay off. Managed browsers should also get a policy that disables or pins encrypted DNS.
- **dns-deep-dive** (dns-r3): ATT&CK mapping for DNS abuse. Analysts must label these findings in tickets and reports, and the room never gives the technique IDs; verify each ID against ATT&CK v19 before publishing.
  - Draft: Map DNS findings precisely. C2 over DNS is T1071.004, Application Layer Protocol: DNS, and carrying other traffic inside DNS is T1572, Protocol Tunneling. Generated domains are T1568.002, Domain Generation Algorithms, and rotating IPs are T1568.001, Fast Flux DNS. Data stolen over a tunnel also maps to an Exfiltration technique, so a single incident can carry more than one ID.
- **dns-investigation** (dns-r3): Encrypted DNS blind spot. DNS over HTTPS hides queries from resolver logs, so an analyst who relies on those logs misses this traffic completely.
  - Draft: DNS over HTTPS (DoH) and DNS over TLS (DoT) send lookups inside encrypted traffic, usually on TCP 443 and 853, so your resolver never sees them. Look for workstations connecting to public DoH providers such as dns.google or cloudflare-dns.com, or to port 853. Browsers can use DoH legitimately, so check which process made the connection and whether policy allows it.
- **dns-investigation** (dns-r2): Benign look-alikes. Without these, an analyst will raise false DGA and tunnelling alerts, and telling them from malice is the core skill of the room.
  - Draft: Some normal software looks like an attack. Chromium browsers send random single-label lookups at startup to test for DNS hijacking, which produce NXDOMAIN. Security products and reputation services can send long, encoded-looking subdomains to their own vendor domains, and CDNs use random hostnames. Check the process, the parent domain's owner, and how many hosts behave the same way.
- **dns-investigation** (dns-r1): Sysmon Event 22 limits. The room says Event 22 records every DNS query, but a process that sends its own raw DNS packets to a resolver will not appear, so missing records prove nothing.
  - Draft: Sysmon Event 22 records lookups made through the Windows DNS client API. Malware that builds its own DNS packets and sends them directly to a resolver may not appear in it, so check network and firewall logs for port 53 traffic from unexpected processes or to external servers. QueryStatus 0 means success and 9003 means the name does not exist. What you get also depends on your Sysmon configuration.
- **dns-investigation** (dns-r3): Responding to tunnelling and fast flux. The workflow's containment step covers only DGA, while these two need different blocks.
  - Draft: For tunnelling, block or sinkhole the parent domain at the resolver, because the subdomains change with every query, and estimate what left by adding up the query volume and length. For fast flux, block the domain rather than the IPs, since the IPs change within minutes. In both cases isolate the host and find the process, then check whether other hosts queried the same domain.
- **edge-case-usecases** (edge-r4): OAuth consent grant response. An analyst who finds an illicit grant must remove the app's access correctly, and the steps appear only in a task explanation.
  - Draft: To respond, remove the app from Enterprise applications in Entra ID, which deletes its service principal and consent grants, then revoke the user's refresh tokens. Access tokens already issued can keep working until they expire, usually within 60 to 90 minutes. Scope the damage with service principal sign-ins, MailItemsAccessed records carrying the app's ID, and Graph activity logs. To prevent repeats, restrict user consent and route requests through the admin consent workflow.
- **edge-case-usecases** (edge-r1): Supply-chain package response. Detecting a malicious install is only half the job, because everything reachable from that developer machine or build runner must be treated as exposed.
  - Draft: After a malicious package runs, isolate the host and treat every secret the install could reach as stolen: npm tokens, SSH keys, cloud credentials and environment variables must be rotated. Search lockfiles, other developer machines and build servers for the same package name and version. To prevent a repeat, publish private packages under a reserved scope, map that scope to the internal registry, and pin versions with lockfiles.
- **edge-case-usecases** (edge-r3): Third-party compromise response. The room shows the tells for skimmers and MSP abuse but not the first moves, which differ from an internal incident because someone else owns the system.
  - Draft: For a suspected MSP compromise, suspend the RMM tool's access, contact the MSP through a known phone number and not the possibly compromised ticket channel, and list every endpoint and script the session touched. For a skimmer, get the web owner to remove or pin the script, keep the CSP reports as evidence, and bring in the payments and legal teams, because stolen card data carries notification duties. PCI DSS v4 also expects an inventory and integrity checks for payment page scripts.
- **edge-case-usecases** (edge-r5): Hunting LOLBins beyond regsvr32. The room names five binaries but shows only regsvr32 in detail, and an analyst needs the abusive command shapes for the others.
  - Draft: Each LOLBin has a typical abusive form. certutil with -urlcache downloads a file (T1105) and with -decode unpacks one (T1140); mshta given a URL runs remote script (T1218.005); rundll32 calling an unusual export or script (T1218.011) and bitsadmin /transfer (T1197) are the other common cases. Prioritise runs with an Office or browser parent, a user-writable path, or an outbound connection, and compare with what your admins normally do.
- **edge-case-usecases** (edge-r4): Finding shadow IT. The reading explains why shadow IT is risky but never how an analyst discovers it or what to do once found.
  - Draft: Shadow IT shows up in proxy, DNS and firewall logs as traffic to SaaS domains missing from the approved list, and a CASB such as Defender for Cloud Apps can report it as a discovered app. Sort by upload volume, because large uploads to an unknown service matter most. Then ask the owning team what data went there and route the tool to review, so it is either sanctioned and monitored or blocked.
- **edr-detection-investigation** (edr-r3): When containment cannot wait. The room teaches containment as the last of six steps, and an analyst who follows that literally during live credential theft loses the race.
  - Draft: The six steps describe the order of reasoning, not a rule that containment must wait. If the evidence shows an action still in progress, such as credential dumping that ran unblocked or hands-on-keyboard activity, network-contain the host as soon as the process tree confirms it, then continue scoping. Containment through the EDR console keeps the host reachable for investigation, so it costs little. Record why you contained early.
- **edr-detection-investigation** (edr-r4): Real process injection telemetry. The reading's 'process injection' section describes a handle request for credential dumping, so an analyst would not recognise actual code injection.
  - Draft: Injection means writing code into another process and running it there, and credential dumping only reads memory. Look for a thread started in a process by a different process: Sysmon Event ID 8 (CreateRemoteThread), the Falcon InjectedThread event, or CreateRemoteThreadApiCall in Defender's DeviceEvents table. A remote thread into a process such as explorer.exe or svchost.exe from an unsigned file in a user folder is a strong signal; some debuggers and security tools do this legitimately, so check the source image and its signer.
- **edr-detection-investigation** (edr-r3): Live response and eradication. The room ends at 'isolate and kill', but an analyst must also collect evidence and remove what the attacker left, such as the scheduled task from the first behavior.
  - Draft: After containment, use the console's remote shell (Real Time Response in Falcon, Live Response in Defender) to collect the binary, any dump file and the scheduled task definition before anything is deleted. Then remove each persistence item you found in the sibling behaviors, not only the headline process. Rotate the credentials used on that host, and decide with the incident lead whether to clean or rebuild it.
- **edr-detection-investigation** (edr-r6): Tuning after a benign true positive. Closing the same authorized alert every deployment night trains analysts to skim, and a broad exclusion blinds the detection.
  - Draft: When an alert is confirmed as authorized and repeats, request a narrow exclusion scoped to the exact account, host group, file path and command line, with an owner and a review date. Do not exclude a whole binary such as regsvr32.exe, because that removes the detection for attackers too. Record the change ticket that justifies it.
- **email-protocols-forensics** (email-r6): Responding to a malicious message. The room is all analysis; no reading says how to scope and clean up, which is what the analyst must do the moment the header review ends.
  - Draft: Once a message is judged malicious, trace it: search the gateway or message trace by sender, subject and sending IP to find every recipient. Remove all copies, then check who opened, clicked or replied, and treat anyone who entered credentials as a possible account compromise. Block the domain and sending IP, and tell the targeted team what the lure looked like. If money may have moved, bring in finance at once.
- **email-protocols-forensics** (email-r6): Mail from a real, compromised mailbox. The room's two cases are spoofing and a lookalike; when an attacker sends from a hijacked real account every check passes on the true domain, and an analyst trained only here has no next step.
  - Draft: A hijacked mailbox sends through the domain's own servers, so SPF, DKIM and DMARC all pass on the real domain, and headers show nothing wrong. Signs are in behaviour instead: an unusual request, a reply-to or payment change inside an existing thread, or a sender who never mails this person. Confirm by reviewing that account's sign-in logs, mailbox forwarding and inbox rules, because attackers often add a rule to hide replies.
- **email-protocols-forensics** (email-r6): How real gateways display these results. Analysts read vendor output, and Microsoft 365's verdicts include values not taught here; check current Microsoft documentation, as these header values can change.
  - Draft: Microsoft 365 adds extras to Authentication-Results. dmarc=bestguesspass means the domain has no DMARC record but the message would have passed one. action=oreject means override reject: the domain published p=reject, yet Microsoft delivered the message as spam rather than refusing it. compauth=pass or fail is Microsoft's own composite verdict, which can disagree with plain DMARC, so read it alongside the three standard results.
- **email-protocols-forensics** (email-r3): Message-ID and other origin clues. The log tasks display Message-ID but no reading says what it means, and it is a quick cross-check when From is forged.
  - Draft: The Message-ID header is set by the sending system and usually ends in that system's domain. A From address at one domain with a Message-ID from an unrelated one is a useful clue, though not proof, since third-party senders do this legitimately and attackers can write any value. Use it to search for other messages from the same sending system.
- **email-security** (email-sec-r2): Passing authentication does not mean safe. Learners can leave thinking spf=pass clears a message, while the room's own log shows a lookalike domain passing SPF.
  - Draft: SPF, DKIM and DMARC prove which domain sent a message, not that the sender is honest. An attacker who registers a lookalike domain can publish valid records and pass all three for that domain, and a message from a compromised real mailbox passes too. Always compare the authenticated domain with the domain the user believes they are dealing with, and check its registration age.
- **email-security** (email-sec-r3): Actions after a user clicks or enters credentials. Step 7 stops at investigating users who interacted, which is where the most urgent analyst work starts.
  - Draft: If a user entered credentials, revoke their sessions, reset the password and review their MFA methods, because a stolen session can outlive a password change unless it is revoked. If an attachment ran, isolate the endpoint and investigate it as a host compromise. Then review the mailbox for new forwarding or hiding rules and for mail sent from it.
- **email-security** (email-sec-r3): Detecting BEC from a compromised real mailbox. Header analysis shows nothing wrong in this case, and the room gives no other way to find it.
  - Draft: BEC from a taken-over account has no failing header, so the evidence sits in identity and mailbox logs. Look for a sign-in from a new location or device shortly before the message, new inbox rules that move or hide replies, and forwarding to an outside address. Confirm any payment change by phone on a known number, never by replying to the message.
- **email-security** (email-sec-r3): AiTM and QR code phishing. These are common current lures that get past the MFA and URL-scanning habits the room implies are enough.
  - Draft: Two techniques defeat the habits in this reading. Adversary-in-the-middle kits proxy the real login page, so the victim completes MFA and the attacker takes the session token. QR code phishing puts the link in an image, so URL scanners may miss it and the victim opens it on a personal phone. Look for a sign-in followed by the same session used from another IP, and treat any email asking a user to scan a code as suspicious.
- **email-security** (email-sec-r3): Scoping in mail platform logs. Step 6 says to query gateway logs but never shows what those records are or how link rewriting changes what you see.
  - Draft: Scope comes from your mail platform's records, not from one .eml file. In Microsoft Defender for Office 365, the Advanced Hunting tables EmailEvents, EmailUrlInfo, EmailAttachmentInfo and UrlClickEvents show who received a message, who clicked and what was attached; other gateways offer a message trace. Search by sender domain, subject, URL and attachment hash, because campaigns vary the sender address. Link-rewriting products wrap URLs, so decode the wrapper to see the real target.
- **encoding-encryption-hashing** (enc-r2): Decoding a real -enc blob. Analysts decode these daily, and the room's verdict task expects it without showing how.
  - Draft: To read a -enc value, Base64-decode it and then interpret the bytes as UTF-16LE. In CyberChef use From Base64 followed by Decode text with UTF-16LE. In PowerShell use [Text.Encoding]::Unicode.GetString([Convert]::FromBase64String($blob)). Attackers often stack layers, such as Gzip inside Base64, so if the output still looks like a jumble, decode again.
- **encoding-encryption-hashing** (enc-r5): Fast versus slow password hashes. How much a stolen hash database exposes depends on the algorithm, which the room never mentions.
  - Draft: Salting stops pre-computed tables but does not slow down guessing. General hashes such as SHA-256 are built for speed, so password storage uses deliberately slow functions such as bcrypt, scrypt or Argon2. Windows NT hashes are fast and unsalted, which is why two accounts with one password share a hash and why stolen NT hashes crack quickly. Ask which algorithm protected the data before judging a breach.
- **encoding-encryption-hashing** (enc-r7): Digital signatures. Analysts judge files, email and certificates by signatures every shift, and this is where hashing and asymmetric keys meet.
  - Draft: A digital signature combines hashing with a keypair: the signer hashes the data and signs that hash with a private key, and anyone can verify it with the matching public key. A valid signature shows who signed the data and that it has not changed since. It does not show the data is safe, because attackers steal signing certificates or abuse signed tools. This is how Windows code signing, DKIM and TLS certificates work.
- **endpoint-security-fundamentals** (ep-sec-r2): Persistence on an endpoint. An analyst who kills a process but misses its restart mechanism closes a case that comes straight back.
  - Draft: Persistence is how malware survives a reboot. The usual places on Windows are registry Run keys, scheduled tasks, services and the Startup folder. After you find a malicious process, check its host for all of these, because killing the process does not remove what restarts it. In EDR, look for registry and file events from the same process tree.
- **endpoint-security-fundamentals** (ep-sec-r2): After isolation: cleaning and release. The room stops at containment, but an analyst must know a host is not safe merely because the alert stopped.
  - Draft: Isolation stops the spread but does not clean the machine. Before you release a host, confirm how the attacker got in, remove the malware and its persistence, and reset any credentials used on it. If the attacker had administrator rights, reimaging is usually safer than trying to clean. Lift isolation only after the owner and your lead agree, then watch the host closely for repeat activity.
- **endpoint-security-fundamentals** (ep-sec-r2): Agent health as a signal. EDR sees nothing on a host where the agent is missing or stopped, and a beginner tends to read silence as safety.
  - Draft: No alerts from a host does not mean the host is safe. Check that the agent is installed, online and reporting recently. An agent that goes offline during an active incident may have been tampered with, while one that stopped when a laptop shut down is normal, so check whether the device is still active on the network. Devices with no agent at all are blind spots you should report.
- **entra-id** (entra-r3): Investigating and responding to a risky sign-in. The room lists detections but gives no steps from alert to verdict to containment.
  - Draft: To work a risky sign-in, filter the sign-in logs by the user and by the IP, then look for successes, which app was reached, and whether MFA was satisfied. Compare the device and location with the account's history, and confirm travel with the user through a trusted channel. If compromise is likely, revoke sessions, reset the password, remove unknown authentication methods, and mark the user as compromised in ID Protection. If it is explained, record why and dismiss the risk.
- **entra-id** (entra-r3): Token theft and AiTM in the logs. Adversary-in-the-middle phishing defeats ordinary MFA, and the room gives token theft one line. Check detection names against current ID Protection documentation.
  - Draft: An adversary-in-the-middle phishing site proxies the real login, so the user completes MFA and the attacker keeps the session cookie. The sign-in log then shows a success with MFA satisfied, from the proxy's IP, followed by activity under the same session ID from a different IP. ID Protection may raise Anomalous Token or Attacker in the Middle detections. Revoke sessions, because a password change alone may not end the stolen session right away, and prefer phishing-resistant methods such as FIDO2.
- **entra-id** (entra-r2): Other sign-in log types. Analysts who read only interactive sign-ins miss token replay and service-principal abuse.
  - Draft: The default view shows interactive sign-ins only. Non-interactive sign-ins, made by apps using tokens, are stored separately (AADNonInteractiveUserSignInLogs in Sentinel), and so are service principal and managed identity sign-ins (AADServicePrincipalSignInLogs and AADManagedIdentitySignInLogs). Always widen to these tables when a token or app is involved, since a replayed token appears there and not in SigninLogs.
- **entra-id** (entra-r2): Reading Conditional Access results. Knowing whether a policy actually applied is how an analyst finds coverage gaps and bypasses such as device code sign-in. Verify the authentication-flows control in the current admin center.
  - Draft: Each sign-in records ConditionalAccessStatus: success means policies passed, failure means a policy blocked it, and notApplied means no policy covered this sign-in, which is a gap to report. The per-policy results show which rule fired, and report-only policies are logged without being enforced. Check authenticationProtocol too, because attackers abuse device code sign-in, which Conditional Access can restrict.
- **entra-id** (entra-r3): Post-compromise persistence in the audit log. Attackers keep access after a password reset through apps, MFA methods and federation; the room's audit list names only a few, with no meaning attached. Check exact activity names against a current tenant.
  - Draft: After a compromise, check the audit log for what would outlive a reset: User registered security info for a new MFA method, Add service principal credentials for a new app secret or certificate (T1098.001), Consent to application for a risky grant, and Set domain authentication for a domain switched to federation (T1484.002). A new credential on an existing app from an unusual actor deserves immediate review, because service principals do not face user MFA.
- **escalation-procedures** (esc-read-2): Severity levels and response targets. Escalation hangs on P1 to P4, which this room uses without defining, so a learner cannot judge how fast to act.
  - Draft: Most SOCs rank incidents from P1 to P4 by impact and urgency, and each level has its own target for acknowledging and escalating. A P1 is active harm to critical systems or many hosts, and it usually means calling the on-call analyst now, often with a target of about 15 minutes. A P4 is low impact and waits for normal queue handling. Confirmed triggers mean escalate as soon as they are met; the playbook time limit applies only when you cannot reach a conclusion. Exact targets come from your own SOC's matrix and client contracts.
- **escalation-procedures** (esc-read-3): When nobody answers. Night-shift analysts face this directly, and the room lists contacts but gives no procedure for an unanswered page or for what Tier 1 may do meanwhile.
  - Draft: Treat an escalation as open until someone confirms they have it. If the on-call does not answer within your playbook's window, often 10 to 15 minutes for a P1, page the backup, then the SOC manager, and log each attempt with its time. While you wait, take only the containment steps your playbook pre-authorises, such as an EDR isolation rule, and record them. Do not stay silent because it is late, and do not act beyond your authority.
- **escalation-procedures** (esc-read-2): Out-of-band communication. In a serious compromise the attacker may read the same chat and email that responders use.
  - Draft: If an attacker may control accounts, mail or chat, do not coordinate over those systems. Move the bridge to phone numbers or a separate communication platform agreed before the incident, and share the details only with the people who need them. Avoid naming suspected accounts or planned containment steps in ordinary company channels. Keep a record of decisions, because the normal ticket trail may be unavailable.
- **escalation-procedures** (esc-read-2): Sensitive escalations such as insider cases. The room lists suspected insider threat as a trigger but does not say that it follows a different, restricted path.
  - Draft: Some cases must not follow the usual open path. For a suspected insider, escalate directly to the SOC manager, who brings in HR and legal, and keep the ticket restricted to those who need it. Do not contact the person or their manager, and do not discuss it in shared channels. Preserve the evidence quietly, because a tipped-off insider can delete data and a careless accusation can harm an innocent employee.
- **escalation-procedures** (esc-read-3): Who decides on notification and when the clock starts. Analysts must know these deadlines exist and that they feed legal, not decide themselves; the CISA reporting rule under CIRCIA may have changed, so check its current status rather than state a deadline for it.
  - Draft: Notification deadlines start at defined moments, so the time you first record awareness matters. Under GDPR it is 72 hours after becoming aware of a reportable personal data breach, for US-listed companies the SEC expects disclosure within four business days of deciding an incident is material, and HIPAA allows up to 60 days after discovery. Analysts do not decide whether to notify: legal and leadership do. Your job is to flag likely personal data or regulated systems early and keep accurate timestamps.
- **esxi-virtualization-security** (esxf-r5): Forwarding hypervisor logs off the host. The whole room depends on host logs that an attacker with root can delete, so without remote copies the evidence may be gone.
  - Draft: vobd.log, shell.log and auth.log sit on the host the attacker controls, and ransomware operators often delete logs around encryption. Configure every host to forward logs to a remote syslog collector through the Syslog.global.logHost setting, and forward vCenter logs too. Alert when a host stops sending logs or when that setting changes.
- **esxi-virtualization-security** (esxf-r8): Responding to a hypervisor compromise. The room stops at detection and ranking, and a wrong first move here can cost every VM on the cluster.
  - Draft: Treat suspected hypervisor compromise as an immediate escalation. Disable the abused accounts, turn SSH off and restrict the management network. Avoid rebooting affected hosts until logs are collected, because some ESXi logs may not survive a reboot on hosts without persistent storage. Do not power VMs back on until the datastore state is known, and restore only from backups held outside the compromised vSphere environment.
- **esxi-virtualization-security** (esxf-r6): Host login records: auth.log and hostd.log. shell.log shows commands, but an analyst also needs who logged in, from where, and what the host agent did.
  - Draft: shell.log shows commands, but the login itself is recorded in /var/log/auth.log on the host, with the account and source IP, so check it for root logins over SSH. hostd.log records VM operations handled by the host agent. Actions run directly on a host may reach vCenter only as a state change with no user named, which is why the host logs decide attribution.
- **esxi-virtualization-security** (esxf-r3): Reaching vSphere through Active Directory. Identity is a common route in and the room starts after an administrator session already exists; CVE-2024-37085 was fixed in ESXi 8.0 U3, so check the current Broadcom advisory for other versions.
  - Draft: Many intrusions reach vSphere through identity, not a vSphere flaw. If hosts or vCenter trust Active Directory, whoever controls AD can often grant themselves access. CVE-2024-37085 gave full admin rights on domain-joined ESXi hosts to members of an AD group named ESX Admins, and an attacker could simply create that group. Treat that group's creation and any change to vCenter identity sources as high priority, and keep vSphere administration on accounts separate from the corporate domain.
- **esxi-virtualization-security** (esxf-r6): Persistence through malicious VIBs. Attackers keep access across reboots this way, and nothing in the room would find it.
  - Draft: An attacker with host access can persist through a malicious vSphere Installation Bundle (VIB), MITRE T1505.006, which survives reboots. Review esxcli software vib list for unexpected vendors or CommunitySupported acceptance levels, and watch for the host acceptance level being lowered. The execInstalledOnly setting stops binaries that were not installed through a VIB from running, which also blocks many ESXi encryptors.
- **exchange-online-security** (exch-sec-r3): Responding to a compromised mailbox. The workflow ends at finding evidence, and the room has no response steps for BEC.
  - Draft: Once a mailbox is confirmed compromised, act in a set order. Revoke the user's sessions and reset the password, because a reset alone may leave existing tokens working, then review MFA methods. Remove malicious inbox rules and mailbox forwarding, check delegates and OAuth app consents, and review sent mail for further phishing. If money moved, tell finance at once: a bank recall has its best chance in the first hours, and the FBI's IC3 is the US reporting route.
- **exchange-online-security** (exch-sec-r3): How mailboxes actually get taken over. The room jumps from spoofed mail to a compromised mailbox without showing the takeover, and token theft defeats ordinary MFA.
  - Draft: Many mailbox takeovers now use adversary-in-the-middle phishing: a fake login page relays the real sign-in and steals the session cookie, so ordinary MFA does not stop it. The sign-in log shows a success with MFA satisfied, soon followed by a sign-in for that user from a new IP or device. Attackers often add inbox rules within minutes, so a new rule right after an unusual sign-in is a strong combination. OAuth consent phishing is a related path that needs no password: check the Entra audit log for Consent to application events.
- **exchange-online-security** (exch-sec-r3): Reading an inbox rule's content. The room says rule content is what makes a rule suspicious but never shows which parameters to read or what a benign rule looks like.
  - Draft: In the audit record, the rule's parameters carry the evidence: ForwardTo, RedirectTo and ForwardAsAttachmentTo send mail away, while DeleteMessage and MoveToFolder hide it. Conditions such as SubjectContainsWords with invoice, payment or password show what the attacker wants to see or hide, and rule names of a single dot or other junk characters are a common attacker habit. Legitimate rules usually file mail into the user's own folders or forward to a colleague inside the company. Admins can also forward mail with a transport rule, logged as New-TransportRule, so check that too.
- **exchange-online-security** (exch-sec-r3): Scoping and cleaning up a phishing campaign. One reported email is rarely alone, and the room names Threat Explorer without showing the hunt or the clean-up.
  - Draft: One reported phish is rarely alone. In Threat Explorer or advanced hunting (EmailEvents, EmailUrlInfo and UrlClickEvents), pivot on the sender, subject, URL or attachment hash to find every recipient and every click. Then remove the remaining copies from mailboxes through a remediation action instead of waiting for ZAP. Anyone who clicked or entered credentials becomes a mailbox-compromise check.
- **exchange-online-security** (exch-sec-r2): Composite authentication (compauth). Analysts see compauth in every Exchange Online header, and the reading's Authentication-Results breakdown leaves it out.
  - Draft: Exchange Online adds its own verdict to Authentication-Results, called compauth (composite authentication). It combines SPF, DKIM and DMARC with Microsoft's sender signals, so a message can be treated as spoofed even when the domain has no DMARC policy, and compauth=pass can appear despite a failed check. Read it with its reason code, for example compauth=fail reason=000. When the results disagree, compauth shows what Microsoft actually decided.
- **firewall-log-analysis** (fw-r3): Pivoting from firewall to process and domain. A firewall log cannot name the program behind a connection, and the pivot steps never send the analyst to EDR or DNS, though graded explanations assume it.
  - Draft: A firewall sees addresses and ports, not programs. Take the timestamp, source IP and source port to your EDR's network events, or to Sysmon Event ID 3, to find the process and its parent. Then check DNS or proxy logs for the domain behind the IP address, because one IP can host many sites and attackers replace addresses often. Blocking the domain as well as the IP makes the block last longer.
- **firewall-log-analysis** (fw-r3): NAT and identifying the real host. Behind NAT, the address in a log may not be the machine involved, and an analyst can pivot to the wrong host.
  - Draft: Network Address Translation (NAT) means the address outside the firewall differs from the internal one. Logs usually carry both: FortiGate uses transip for the translated source, Palo Alto uses natsrc and Check Point uses xlatesrc. When you start from an external report, match on the translated address, time and port to find the internal source. Then check DHCP for the lease at that exact time, because the same internal IP may belong to a different machine an hour later.
- **firewall-log-analysis** (fw-r2): Beacons with jitter. The room teaches exact timing, but common C2 frameworks randomise it, so a learner looking for perfect intervals will miss real beacons.
  - Draft: Real beacons are rarely perfectly regular. Frameworks such as Cobalt Strike add jitter, so a 60-second sleep with 20 percent jitter produces gaps of roughly 48 to 60 seconds. Group connections by source and destination, calculate the time between them and look for low variation over many connections rather than an exact number. Some beacons also sleep for hours, so widen the time window before you rule one out.
- **firewall-log-analysis** (fw-r2): Benign lookalikes. Scans and timers are normal in many networks, and an analyst who cannot rule them out will raise false alarms.
  - Draft: Vulnerability scanners and monitoring tools look exactly like port scans, and update, sync and endpoint agents look like beacons. Check the source against the approved scanner list and change records, and check that the destination belongs to software installed on that machine. A pattern that has run for months across many similar hosts is usually a product, while one that started recently on a single host needs more attention. Confirm with the process behind it before you close the alert.
- **firewall-log-analysis** (fw-r1): Reading a full vendor log line. The room promises FortiGate, Palo Alto and Check Point telemetry but only lists field names, so a learner has never walked through a raw line before the graded log.
  - Draft: Read every raw line in the same order: what happened, who, to where, how much, which rule. In type="traffic" subtype="forward" action="deny" srcip=10.10.22.87 dstip=185.220.101.47 dstport=443 sentbyte=0 rcvdbyte=0 duration=0 policyid=41, a FortiGate denied an internal host's attempt to reach an external server on port 443, no data moved and policy 41 made the decision. Palo Alto and Check Point logs carry the same facts under different names, so learn the mapping once. Zero bytes with a deny means the attempt was stopped, but the host that tried still needs attention.
- **firewall-masterclass** (fw-r10): Telling beacons and uploads from benign traffic. Updaters, telemetry agents and backup jobs also produce regular or large outbound traffic, and an analyst who cannot separate them escalates noise.
  - Draft: Regular timing alone does not prove C2: software updaters, cloud agents and monitoring tools also call home on a schedule. Before escalating, check who owns the destination, how many hosts talk to it (one workstation is more suspicious than the whole fleet), which process made the connection, and whether the destination is new for this host. Large uploads work the same way: a backup server sending gigabytes to its known cloud vendor is routine, while a workstation sending the same volume to a first-seen address is not.
- **firewall-masterclass** (fw-r10): Responding to a firewall finding. The room ends at reading the log, but an analyst must know what to do next and which actions belong to the network team.
  - Draft: When a firewall log shows likely C2 or exfiltration, contain the host first, usually through EDR isolation, then search the logs for every other internal host that contacted the same destination. Request a block for the destination through the change process rather than editing production rules yourself, and record the policy name that allowed the traffic so the rule gap can be reviewed. Keep the original log records, because the block changes what future logs will show.
- **firewall-masterclass** (fw-r10): Inbound scans in deny logs. Reconnaissance is usually the earliest firewall signal and the room's hunting list covers only outbound and lateral patterns.
  - Draft: A burst of denied sessions from one source to many ports on one host is a port scan, and the same source hitting one port across many hosts is a sweep. Look for very short sessions with little or no data and group them by source address. Internet-facing scanning is constant background noise, so raise priority when the source is internal, or when an accepted session from the same source follows the denies.
- **firewall-masterclass** (fw-r10): FortiGate UTM logs and session-end actions. Analysts search these fields daily, and the reading's data.type "threat" and three action values do not match what FortiOS normally writes; exact value lists vary by FortiOS version, so confirm against the current log reference before publishing.
  - Draft: FortiGate records security-profile hits under data.type="utm", with data.subtype naming the engine, such as ips, virus, webfilter or app-ctrl. Traffic logs written when a session ends often show data.action values such as close, timeout, client-rst or server-rst, so a session that was allowed may not say accept. Do not filter on accept alone when you want every permitted connection.
- **firewall-network-security** (fw-r3): NAT in firewall logs. Threat intel and abuse reports name the public address, so without NAT fields an analyst cannot find the real internal host.
  - Draft: Most firewalls translate addresses (NAT), so one connection has two identities: the private address of the internal host and the public address the outside world sees. A threat-intel hit or abuse report names the public address, and the firewall log is where you find the real host. Look for the translated-address fields, such as natsrc on Palo Alto or transip on FortiGate, and match on time and port, because many hosts share one public address.
- **firewall-network-security** (fw-r3): What to do after a scan alert. The room reads a scan but never says whether it matters or what comes next, which is the daily decision on this alert type.
  - Draft: One source hitting many ports on one host is a vertical scan, and one source hitting one port across many hosts is a horizontal scan. Internet scans are constant and rarely need escalation by themselves. The useful pivot is to filter the firewall logs on that source IP for ALLOW actions: a blocked scan is noise, while a scan followed by an allowed connection to the same service is a lead. Check whether any scanned port is actually open, then log it, or escalate if a risky service answered.
- **firewall-network-security** (fw-r3): Requesting a block safely. SOC analysts usually do not own the firewall, and a careless block can cut off a shared service or hide other rules.
  - Draft: A block is a change to a production device, so follow your SOC process: confirm the IP is not a shared CDN or a partner, record the ticket and set an expiry. Keep the rule narrow, because rules run top to bottom and a broad rule placed high can shadow others. A block only stops traffic at that boundary, so it does not clean an infected host.
- **firewall-network-security** (fw-r3): Same fields, different vendor names. Real logs do not use the generic names in this reading, and a beginner must find the same facts in any product.
  - Draft: The fields in this reading are generic, and each product names them differently. Palo Alto logs src, dst, dport, rule and session_end_reason; FortiGate logs srcip, dstip, dstport, policyid, sentbyte and rcvdbyte; Check Point logs src, dst, service, action and rule_name. Learn to find the same facts in any product: who, to where, which port, what the firewall did, which rule, and how much data.
- **gcp-security** (gcp-r4): Service-account impersonation. Attackers often mint tokens through impersonation instead of keys, so an analyst who only watches CreateServiceAccountKey misses the escalation.
  - Draft: An identity holding roles/iam.serviceAccountTokenCreator can mint short-lived tokens for another service account without any key. The call is GenerateAccessToken on iamcredentials.googleapis.com, and it is a Data Access event, so it stays invisible unless Data Access logging is enabled for that API. When the impersonated account then acts, the audit entry names the real caller under authenticationInfo.serviceAccountDelegationInfo, so always read that field as well as principal_email.
- **gcp-security** (gcp-r7): Stopping a stolen token, not only a key. The room's containment starts from a key, but the first credential in its own chain is a metadata-server token that disabling a key does not touch. Not fully sure how quickly tokens are rejected after disabling an account, so check Google's current documentation.
  - Draft: Disabling a key does nothing against a stolen access token, such as one taken from the metadata server, which works until it expires, by default about one hour. To cut that identity off, disable the service account itself with gcloud iam service-accounts disable SA_EMAIL and remove its bindings. Check first which workloads depend on it, because they stop too.
- **gcp-security** (gcp-r6): Logging tampering. An attacker with Owner can blind the SOC, and an analyst must recognise that before trusting an empty SIEM.
  - Draft: An attacker with Owner may try to stop the evidence. Watch Admin Activity for google.logging.v2.ConfigServiceV2.DeleteSink and UpdateSink, which stop logs reaching the SIEM, and for SetIamPolicy calls that change auditConfigs, which turn Data Access logging off. Admin Activity logs cannot be disabled, so these calls remain in Cloud Logging even after the SIEM feed goes quiet.
- **gcp-security** (gcp-r4): Compute metadata persistence. Writing SSH keys or startup scripts is a common GCP foothold and survives credential cleanup.
  - Draft: Anyone who can write Compute Engine metadata can add SSH keys or a startup script: v1.compute.instances.setMetadata changes one VM and v1.compute.projects.setCommonInstanceMetadata changes every VM in the project. A startup script runs as root at each boot, so it can bring an attacker back after keys are revoked. Treat these calls from an unexpected identity as high severity, and review the metadata during eradication.
- **google-workspace-security** (gwsf-r6): How to revoke and find other victims. The room insists on revoking but never says where or how, or that one malicious client_id may hold grants from several users. Console labels change often, so check current Google Admin Help before publishing the menu names.
  - Draft: Revoke a grant from the user's record in the Admin console, under Security and connected applications, or with the Admin SDK Directory API tokens call. To stop an app across the organisation, set it to Blocked under API controls and app access control. Before closing the case, search the token audit for the same client_id across all users, because a phishing lure rarely reaches just one person.
- **google-workspace-security** (gwsf-r2): The login audit. r2 lists the login category and nothing teaches it, yet account takeover in Workspace starts there and it is the first place an analyst looks.
  - Draft: The login category records sign-in outcomes. login_success and login_failure are the everyday events, suspicious_login is Google's own risk flag, and 2sv_disable shows 2-Step Verification being turned off, a common step after takeover. gov_attack_warning means Google suspects a government-backed attacker. Read these against the actor's usual location and device, as you would any sign-in log.
- **google-workspace-security** (gwsf-r5): Drive sharing events as they appear in logs. The room calls visibility changes one of the highest-value alerts but shows no such event; exact parameter names should be checked against Google's current Drive audit reference.
  - Draft: Sharing changes have their own events. change_document_visibility records a file moving to a wider or narrower audience, and change_user_access records a specific person being added or removed. Compare the old and new visibility values, and treat a move to anyone with the link on a sensitive file as the alert. Benign cases are common, such as an agreed external partner share, so confirm with the file owner before escalating.
- **google-workspace-security** (gwsf-r6): Gmail forwarding and delegation. Mailbox forwarding is standard persistence after a Workspace takeover and the room does not cover it; the specific audit event names should be confirmed against Google's reference before use.
  - Draft: After taking over an account, attackers often set Gmail to forward mail outside the organisation or add a delegate, so they keep reading mail after a password change. Check the user's forwarding and delegation settings, and search the audit logs for those changes around the time of compromise. Admins can restrict automatic external forwarding by policy.
- **google-workspace-security** (gwsf-r2): Audit log delay and retention. An analyst who assumes Workspace logs are instant may wrongly conclude nothing happened; Google publishes lag and retention per log, and those figures change, so cite the current table instead of fixed numbers.
  - Draft: Workspace audit data does not arrive instantly, and the delay differs by log, so a search that comes back empty minutes after an event proves little. Retention is limited too, so long investigations rely on logs already exported to your SIEM. Check Google's current data retention and lag times page when you plan a search window.
- **html-smuggling-and-lolbins** (hsl-r9): Responding to a confirmed LOLBin chain. The room ends at detection, so an analyst holding a confirmed case has no taught next actions.
  - Draft: Once a LOLBin chain is confirmed, isolate the host through EDR rather than powering it off, so memory and network state survive. Capture the full command line, retrieve the remote scriptlet or MSI in a sandbox, and record its hash. Hunt the URL, IP and file name across every host, then look for anything the proxied code dropped or scheduled, because removing the first file does not remove persistence. Reset the user's credentials if the payload could have reached them.
- **html-smuggling-and-lolbins** (hsl-r8): File-creation telemetry for the smuggled file. Network tools see only HTML, so the first hard evidence of smuggling is the browser writing a container to disk.
  - Draft: The browser process writes a smuggled file, so look for chrome.exe, msedge.exe or firefox.exe creating an .iso, .img or .zip in Downloads (Sysmon Event ID 11, FileCreate, when your configuration collects it). Sysmon Event ID 15, FileCreateStreamHash, can record the Zone.Identifier stream being written. A mounted ISO then appears as a new drive letter, and a process starting from that drive is a strong follow-on signal.
- **html-smuggling-and-lolbins** (hsl-r1): Inspecting a suspect HTML attachment. Analysts triage reported attachments, and the room never shows what smuggling code looks like in the file.
  - Draft: Open a suspect HTML attachment as text in a plain editor, never in a browser. Signs of smuggling are a very long encoded string, atob(, new Blob(, URL.createObjectURL, msSaveOrOpenBlob, and a hidden link with a download attribute that the script clicks. Legitimate web apps use these calls too, so the signal is the combination inside a standalone emailed HTML file.
- **html-smuggling-and-lolbins** (hsl-r9): Preventive controls and their logs. Blocks from these controls are early signals, and an analyst must know which ones exist to read them.
  - Draft: Windows can stop parts of this chain before it runs. Attack Surface Reduction rules can block Office apps from creating child processes, App Control for Business (formerly WDAC) can deny binaries a site does not need, such as mshta.exe, and policy can make .hta and .js files open in Notepad instead of running. Learn which of these are deployed, because their block events are high-value early signals.
- **identity-basics** (idbasics-r5): What revocation really does and how long it takes. idbasics-r5 says revocation immediately invalidates every session and token, which overstates it: Microsoft's emergency revocation page says access tokens stay valid until they expire and apps control their own session cookies.
  - Draft: Revocation is not always instant. In Microsoft Entra ID, Revoke sessions stops new tokens from being issued, but an access token already issued keeps working until it expires, one hour by default, unless the app supports Continuous Access Evaluation. An application's own session cookie is controlled by that application. Keep watching the account's activity after you revoke, and for on-premises Active Directory, Microsoft recommends resetting the password twice.
- **identity-basics** (idbasics-r4): Seeing token theft in sign-in logs. Token theft is called the most dangerous attack in the room but the only log taught is Windows, where it does not appear.
  - Draft: Token replay shows up in the identity provider's sign-in log, not in Windows events. In Entra ID, look for a sign-in where MFA is listed as satisfied by a claim in the token, from an IP address, network or device that does not match the user's earlier sign-in, sometimes with the same session ID. Microsoft Entra ID Protection raises risk detections such as Anomalous token for this pattern. A new location with no fresh MFA prompt is the point to check.
- **identity-basics** (idbasics-r8): More of the Windows logon evidence. An analyst cannot triage 4625 without the failure code, and the room stops at three logon types and two event IDs.
  - Draft: A 4625 carries a Status and SubStatus code that says why it failed: 0xC000006A is a wrong password, 0xC0000064 is a username that does not exist, 0xC0000234 is a locked account and 0xC0000072 is a disabled account. LogonType 4 is a batch job, 5 is a service and 7 is a workstation unlock. On a domain controller, 4740 records an account lockout, 4776 an NTLM credential check, and 4768 and 4769 Kerberos ticket requests.
- **identity-basics** (idbasics-r8): Password spraying and account lockout. A graded distractor relies on spraying and lockout, but no reading explains either, and spraying is one of the most common identity attacks.
  - Draft: Brute force tries many passwords against one account (T1110.001). Password spraying tries one or two common passwords across many accounts (T1110.003) slowly enough to stay under the lockout limit, the number of failures that makes a system lock an account. In logs, spraying is one source with many different usernames, each failing once or twice. A low failure count per account is therefore not reassuring when the same source touches hundreds of accounts.
- **identity-basics** (idbasics-r8): Benign failure bursts. The room teaches the burst pattern as an attack shape with no way to rule out everyday causes.
  - Draft: Not every burst of 4625 events is an attack. A phone still holding an old password, a mapped drive or a service left with an expired password will fail again and again from the same internal device. Those failures usually come at a steady rhythm from a known machine, and no success follows until the password is updated. Check the source device, its owner and any recent password change before you treat the burst as guessing.
- **incident-report-writing** (doc-r4): Verifying a finding before reporting it. One false finding undermines the whole report, and the room grades this skill without teaching the check.
  - Draft: Before a suspicious event enters the report, verify it. Check it against change tickets, the account owner, and your baseline for that host and hour. If it proves authorized, record it as verified activity with the ticket number, so that the next reviewer does not have to repeat the work. Looking related is not the same as being related.
- **incident-report-writing** (doc-r4): Separating facts from assessments. Reviewers and legal readers must be able to tell what was observed from what the analyst concluded.
  - Draft: Keep observed facts separate from your conclusions, and label each conclusion with a confidence level. 'The task was created at 23:14 UTC' is a fact. 'We assess with moderate confidence that this was the attacker's only foothold' is a judgement that a reader can weigh. When new evidence changes a conclusion, correct it openly and do not silently rewrite it.
- **incident-report-writing** (doc-r3): Defanging and handling the report. Reports travel by email and chat, so live indicators can be clicked or trigger scanners, and the contents may be sensitive.
  - Draft: Keep exact IOC values for search tools, but defang them when they go into email, tickets or chat. For example, write hxxp://evil[.]example so that nothing is clickable or auto-scanned. State which form you used, so readers do not paste a defanged value into a hunt. Mark the report's sharing level, such as TLP:AMBER+STRICT, and limit distribution to those who need it.
- **incident-report-writing** (doc-r3): Normalising time across sources. A timeline built from sources in different time zones can reverse the order of events and produce wrong conclusions.
  - Draft: Log sources do not all record time the same way: some use UTC and others local time, and device clocks can drift. Convert every timestamp to UTC before building the timeline, and state in the report that you did. Keep the original value in your evidence notes. If two sources disagree by a fixed offset, measure it from an event seen in both and record the correction.
- **incident-report-writing** (doc-r4): Interim updates and version control. Leadership needs reports while the incident is still running, and untracked revisions create the drift this room warns about.
  - Draft: Incidents are usually reported before they are finished, so issue dated interim updates that state what is known, what is not known and when the next update is due. Keep a revision history that lists each change, who made it and why. When a fact changes, update the Executive Summary and the technical sections together. The final report then records how understanding developed, without hiding earlier statements.
- **incident-response-methodology** (ir-method-r2): Timing containment and account actions. Containing one host at a time can warn the attacker, and the room treats containment as always immediate.
  - Draft: During active spread, contain at once. If an attacker is present but quiet and many footholds are known, IR leads may scope first and then act on all of them together, because removing one foothold warns the attacker to use another. For identities, containment means disabling the account and revoking sessions and refresh tokens, not only isolating a host. The IR Lead owns this timing decision.
- **incident-response-methodology** (ir-method-r3): Cloud and identity persistence. The eradication checklist is endpoint-centred, while many current incidents leave persistence in identity systems.
  - Draft: Eradication also covers identity and cloud footholds. Check for rogue MFA methods or registered devices, mailbox forwarding and inbox rules, OAuth app consents, new service principal credentials and added admin roles. These survive a password reset and a host rebuild, so each has to be found and removed on its own.
- **incident-response-methodology** (ir-method-r2): Out-of-band communication. If the attacker can read mail or chat, the response plan leaks, and the room's communication section does not cover this.
  - Draft: Assume a compromised environment may expose your own conversations. Run the incident on a channel the attacker cannot reach, such as a separate tenant, a phone bridge or a pre-arranged messaging service, and agree this during Preparation. Keep indicators and containment plans out of corporate mail and chat until the scope is known.
- **incident-response-methodology** (ir-method-r2): Notification clocks. Analysts' timestamps set legal deadlines, and the room gives only a passing mention; legal counsel decides what applies in each case.
  - Draft: Regulatory clocks often start when the organisation becomes aware of an incident, so record when each fact was learned. GDPR requires notifying the supervisory authority within 72 hours of becoming aware of a reportable personal data breach, and SEC rules require listed companies to disclose a material incident within four business days of determining it is material. Other national rules differ, so legal decides what applies.
- **incident-response-methodology** (ir-method-r3): Response metrics. The post-incident report asks for a timeline, but the room never names the measures teams use to show improvement.
  - Draft: Turn the timeline into numbers: dwell time is first compromise to detection, MTTD is the mean time to detect, and MTTR is the mean time to respond or recover (define which one your team reports). Tracking these across incidents shows whether action items worked, and dwell time also tells you how far back a clean backup must reach.
- **intro-cybersecurity** (intro-cyber-r2b): Threat, vulnerability and risk. These words appear in every alert, report and meeting, and the room never separates them.
  - Draft: A vulnerability is a weakness, such as an unpatched server or a reused password. A threat is someone or something that could take advantage of it, and an exploit is the actual method used. Risk combines how likely that is with how much damage it would do. An unlocked door is a vulnerability, a burglar is a threat, and the risk is higher if valuables are inside.
- **intro-cybersecurity** (intro-cyber-r2b): Main types of malware. The first log task shows a Trojan, and the word malware is used throughout but never broken down.
  - Draft: Malware is any software built to cause harm. A virus attaches to a file and spreads when that file is run, a worm spreads by itself across a network, and a Trojan pretends to be something useful, like the fake invoice in this room's alert. Ransomware locks files for payment, and spyware quietly collects information. Security tools often put the type in the detection name, as in Trojan:Win32.
- **intro-cybersecurity** (intro-cyber-r2): How defenders protect, detect and respond. A beginner needs to see the three jobs of defence, and the room names layers without saying what they are.
  - Draft: Defence has three jobs. Prevention stops attacks with controls such as strong passwords, multi-factor authentication, encryption, patching and giving people only the access they need. Detection spots what got through, using logs and alerts. Response contains the damage and restores service, using tools such as backups.
- **intro-cybersecurity** (intro-cyber-r1): What an alert is and what triage means. The room puts learners in front of an alert without saying what they are supposed to decide.
  - Draft: A log is a record of activity, and an alert is a tool saying that some activity looks worth checking. Triage means deciding quickly whether the alert is real and how urgent it is. A true positive is a real problem, while a false positive is an alert on something harmless. Many alerts turn out harmless, so the analyst's skill lies in checking the evidence and not trusting the alert title.
- **investigate-alert-workflow** (inv7-r3): When to contain before collecting. Read as an absolute, collect-before-act would leave a spreading intrusion running while evidence is gathered.
  - Draft: Collecting first is the default, not an absolute rule. If you see active encryption, data leaving or a privileged account in live use, containing now can outweigh the evidence you might lose, and that risk decision follows your runbook and shift lead. Prefer actions that keep evidence: network isolation through the EDR keeps memory and processes alive, while a reboot or reimage destroys them. Write down what you did, when and why, because containment changes the state later readers will see.
- **investigate-alert-workflow** (inv7-r1): Decoding an encoded command. Every encoded PowerShell alert needs the command decoded before the analyst can say what it did, and the room's case never does this.
  - Draft: The value after -enc is base64 of UTF-16LE text, so decode it with those two settings, for example in CyberChef with From Base64 then Decode text UTF-16LE. The prefix SQBFAFgA decodes to IEX, the PowerShell alias that runs text as code. Then look up any destination or hash found in the decoded command, and check in your EDR how many other hosts ran the same thing. The hash of powershell.exe tells you nothing, so judge the command line and not the binary.
- **investigate-alert-workflow** (inv7-r1): Closing an alert as benign. The case is malicious throughout, yet most alerts close as benign and the analyst must prove that with evidence.
  - Draft: Validation can end in three ways: a duplicate or artefact, a benign true positive, or a real finding that moves to scope. A benign close needs named evidence such as a change ticket, a known admin account on its usual host at the expected time, or a script hash matching the approved version. A familiar look is not evidence, and neither is a user saying it was them, because that account may be the compromised one. Record what you checked and the reason for closing.
- **investigate-alert-workflow** (inv7-r2): Cloud identity in scope. The room says identity drives most investigations but pivots only on Windows logons, leaving cloud sessions unexamined.
  - Draft: Scope also covers cloud sign-ins. In Entra ID, pivot the sign-in logs on IP address, device and user agent to find other accounts using the same source, and check for new MFA methods, mailbox rules and OAuth consents. A password reset alone does not end a stolen session, so also revoke the user's sessions and confirm in the logs that the old session stopped.
- **investigate-alert-workflow** (inv7-r3): Collecting from the EDR console. An analyst needs to know how Step 3 is done in practice from their own tools, not only the order.
  - Draft: In Microsoft Defender for Endpoint, the Collect investigation package action pulls a ZIP of system information, and Live Response lets you run commands and fetch specific files from the host. Neither replaces a full memory image, which needs a dedicated tool and usually a forensic team, so ask for one early if memory may matter. Record the hash and the time of each item you pull.
- **investigation-methodology** (inv-method-r1): Testing hypotheses and avoiding bias. An analyst who only seeks supporting evidence closes cases wrongly, and the room teaches collecting more than reasoning.
  - Draft: Treat your first explanation as a hypothesis, write down at least one innocent alternative, and ask what evidence would prove each wrong. Then search for that evidence, not only for what supports your favourite. Keep observed facts apart from your inferences in the notes, and record gaps: missing logs mean you do not know, not that nothing happened.
- **investigation-methodology** (inv-method-r2): Process ancestry pivot. The parent chain is how an analyst walks from a suspicious process back to the entry point, and the four pivots skip it.
  - Draft: In EDR, follow a suspicious process up through its parents as well as out to its network connections. A chain such as Outlook starting Word starting PowerShell points to the delivery route, and its child processes show what the attacker did next. Each parent gives you a new time and file to add to the timeline.
- **investigation-methodology** (inv-method-r3): Real pivot queries. The room describes pivots in words but never shows one in a SIEM console.
  - Draft: A user pivot in Microsoft Sentinel can be as short as: SecurityEvent | where EventID == 4624 and TargetUserName == "svc-backup" | summarize count() by Computer, IpAddress, LogonType. It lists where the account logged on and from which address. Save the query link in the ticket so that others can reproduce the result.
- **investigation-methodology** (inv-method-r3): When to stop and escalate. The lifecycle runs to a written conclusion, while an active attack needs handing to IR during the investigation.
  - Draft: Do not finish the investigation before raising the alarm. Hand over to incident response as soon as you see confirmed active compromise, lateral movement or data leaving, and pass on what you know with your open questions. Containment then runs alongside your analysis, so check with IR before any action that could alert the attacker or destroy evidence.
- **ioc-analysis** (ioc-r2): Interpreting clean or weak lookups. Analysts must not close a case on 0 detections, and the room only teaches that a higher score means worse.
  - Draft: A clean lookup is not proof of safety. A new or targeted sample can show 0 detections on VirusTotal and a fresh domain has no reputation yet, while one or two vendors flagging a popular file can be a false alarm. Weigh each result by its age and the number of independent sources, then record a confidence level rather than a yes or no. Your own telemetry decides the case: what process connected, and what happened on the host next.
- **ioc-analysis** (ioc-r3): Hunting the IOC internally and responding. Enrichment only describes the indicator, and the room shows one hash search but no general method for scoping and acting on hits.
  - Draft: Enrichment tells you about the indicator; the investigation question is whether it touched you. Search proxy, DNS, firewall, EDR and mail logs for the period it was active, and note whether each hit was allowed or blocked. An allowed connection means triaging that host: find the process, isolate it if malicious, then block the indicator and record what you did. A blocked attempt still shows a host tried to reach it and needs a look.
- **ioc-analysis** (ioc-r3): Pivoting on certificates and reused attributes. The room lists certificates as IOCs but pivots only on IPs and domains, which stop working once infrastructure moves.
  - Draft: IP and domain pivots fail when infrastructure moves, so pivot on what the attacker reuses. TLS certificate fingerprints and subject names, registrant details and name servers can link servers that share no IP. Certificate transparency search (crt.sh) and internet scanners (Shodan, Censys) support this. Treat a match as a lead, because shared hosting and default certificates create false links.
- **ioc-analysis** (ioc-r3): Handling and sharing indicators safely. Analysts paste IOCs into tickets and share them daily, and the room covers sharing formats but not safe handling.
  - Draft: Write indicators in defanged form in tickets and chat, for example hxxp://203.0.113[.]45/login, so nobody clicks them and tools do not fetch them. Mark shared intelligence with the Traffic Light Protocol (TLP 2.0: CLEAR, GREEN, AMBER, AMBER+STRICT, RED), which tells recipients how far it may travel. Do not browse to a suspect address from a corporate host, because that contact tells the attacker someone is looking.
- **kerberos-authentication** (krb-r6): Responding to forged tickets. The room teaches detection of Golden and Silver tickets but never what ends them, so an analyst could not direct containment.
  - Draft: A Golden Ticket stays valid until the krbtgt key changes, so reset the krbtgt password twice, letting replication finish between resets, because the KDC also accepts the previous key. Waiting at least the maximum ticket lifetime between the two resets, 10 hours by default, avoids breaking real sessions. A Silver Ticket depends on one service or computer account's key, so reset that account's password. Either way, find how the hash was obtained, or the attacker can repeat it.
- **kerberos-authentication** (krb-r6): Pass the Ticket. Stealing and reusing a real ticket is a core Kerberos technique that the room's list of attacks omits.
  - Draft: Pass the Ticket (T1550.003) reuses a real ticket exported from LSASS memory on a compromised host instead of forging one. Because the ticket is genuine, a stolen TGT needs no new 4768 on the DC. The tell is that the 4768 that issued the TGT shows one IpAddress while later 4769 events for the same account come from another host. Compare those addresses, and check whether the account has a logon on that second host.
- **kerberos-authentication** (krb-r6): Limits of the missing-4768 check. An advanced analyst must not treat one rule as proof. Not fully sure of behaviour on patched DCs: PAC hardening from KB5008380 may reject forged tickets for accounts that do not exist, so verify the reading's 'including one that doesn't exist' claim.
  - Draft: A 4769 with no matching 4768 is a lead, not proof: first check every DC and the full TGT lifetime, since the TGT may have been issued elsewhere or earlier. It can also miss the attack, because a Diamond Ticket edits a real TGT from a normal AS exchange, so a matching 4768 exists. Also look at the ticket itself where tooling allows, since a lifetime far above domain policy is a sign of forgery.
- **kerberos-authentication** (krb-r3): Kerberoasting without RC4, and decoy accounts. Detections built on 0x17 fail as RC4 disappears. Microsoft has announced moves to retire RC4 from Kerberos defaults; check the current state for the Windows versions in use.
  - Draft: Kerberoasting does not require RC4: tools can take AES tickets too, which only makes cracking slower. In a domain without RC4, detect the behaviour instead, meaning one account requesting many distinct SPNs in a short window. A strong addition is a decoy service account with an SPN no real client uses, so any 4769 for it is worth an alert.
- **kerberos-authentication** (krb-r2): Kerberos failure codes. Status values reveal username enumeration and password spraying, which often come before roasting.
  - Draft: Failure events carry a Status code that tells you why the request failed. On a 4768, 0x6 means the username does not exist, so many of these from one source suggest username enumeration. On a 4771, 0x18 means a wrong password, so one source failing across many accounts suggests password spraying over Kerberos. A 0x25 means clock skew and is usually a time-sync problem, not an attack.
- **kubernetes-container-security** (k8s-r3): Audit log availability and runtime blind spots. The room assumes audit logs exist, yet EKS control plane logs are off by default and audit logs never show what runs inside a container.
  - Draft: Audit logs exist only if someone turns them on and ships them. On Amazon EKS each control plane log type, including audit, is off by default and must be enabled to reach CloudWatch Logs, and self-managed clusters need an audit policy on the API server. Audit logs also stop at the API: what runs inside a container after exec or escape needs runtime telemetry such as Falco or an EDR sensor on the node. Confirm both sources exist before treating a quiet cluster as a clean one.
- **kubernetes-container-security** (k8s-r4): Reaching IMDS without an escape, and detecting credential use. Step 3 reads as if node access is required, which understates the risk, and the room gives no cloud-side detection for stolen node credentials.
  - Draft: A pod does not always need to escape to reach cloud credentials.
