Unless the node requires IMDSv2 with a hop limit of 1, or network policy blocks 169.254.169.254, an ordinary pod can often query the metadata service directly. Workload identity (IRSA or EKS Pod Identity on AWS, Workload Identity on GKE) gives pods their own narrow role instead of the node's. In CloudTrail, watch for the node role being used from an IP outside your environment; GuardDuty raises InstanceCredentialExfiltration findings for this.
- **kubernetes-container-security** (k8s-r4): Containing a compromised pod or node. The room says to treat this as one incident but never says how to contain it without destroying evidence.
  - Draft: Contain in an order that keeps evidence. Capture the pod spec and logs, isolate the pod with a deny-all network policy and cordon the node so nothing new lands there, and only then delete anything. Rotate the ServiceAccount's credentials and remove excess bindings. If the node was reached, treat its cloud role as stolen: revoke its sessions and replace the node rather than cleaning it.
- **kubernetes-container-security** (k8s-r2): Telling legitimate privileged pods from hostile ones. CNI agents and security sensors use these settings every day, and the benign case appears only in a task explanation.
  - Draft: Real infrastructure does use these settings: CNI agents such as Cilium and Calico and sensors such as Falco often run privileged as DaemonSets, one managed copy per node. To judge a pod, check metadata.ownerReferences for a DaemonSet or other controller, whether the image comes from your approved registry and whether the creator is the normal deployment identity. A bare pod with no owner, created by a CI token from an unfamiliar image, is the shape to escalate.
- **kubernetes-container-security** (k8s-r3): Admission control and other high-value audit events. Blocked pod creations and RBAC or secret access are key signals, and the room covers only pod creation and exec.
  - Draft: Pod Security Admission, the built-in replacement for PodSecurityPolicy, can enforce the baseline or restricted profile per namespace and rejects privileged and host-namespace pods with a 403, so a denied create can be the first sign of an attempt. Also alert on new clusterrolebindings that grant cluster-admin, and on get or list against secrets by identities that do not normally read them. Those give an attacker lasting access without any privileged pod.
- **lateral-movement-practice** (lat-r1): Source-side and share-access artefacts. The room says one host is never enough, but names no record that shows the outbound side or the IPC$/svcctl access it keeps referring to.
  - Draft: The chain leaves more records than a 4624 and a 7045. On the source host, 4648 records a logon with explicit credentials and process creation shows the tool. On the target, 5145 records share access, with IPC$ as the share and svcctl as the relative target, if Detailed File Share auditing is enabled. Sysmon adds Event ID 3 for the network connection and Event IDs 17 and 18 for named pipes, and a pipe named PSEXESVC on the target is a strong sign of PsExec.
- **lateral-movement-practice** (lat-r2): Scoping and containment of a confirmed chain. A trained analyst needs a response order, and the room gives none beyond investigate the workstation.
  - Draft: Once a chain is confirmed, scope before you touch the account: list every host where it logged on in the window, and check each for new services and credential access. Then isolate the affected hosts and rotate the account in one coordinated step, so the attacker cannot simply switch paths. After the incident, narrow the GPO grant so a support account cannot administer every host, and use unique local admin passwords through Windows LAPS.
- **lateral-movement-practice** (lat-r1): The Kerberos variant of the same movement. NTLM is only one case, so an analyst or query built on NtLmSsp alone misses the same movement when the target is addressed by name.
  - Draft: When the target is addressed by name inside a domain, the same movement normally uses Kerberos rather than NTLM. The target 4624 then shows AuthenticationPackageName Kerberos, WorkstationName is often blank, and the domain controller logs a 4769 ticket request for a service such as cifs/SRV-FIL02 from the source IP. Build correlations on LogonType 3 plus the following service install, and treat NTLM as one variant.
- **lateral-movement-practice** (lat-r2): Tool variants beyond default PsExec. Operator frameworks commonly randomise service names, so an analyst who hunts the default name alone misses them; exact tool defaults change between versions.
  - Draft: Impacket's psexec.py and Cobalt Strike's service-based jump commands use the same mechanism, usually with a random service name. Hunt on shape rather than name: a new service with a short random name, an ImagePath in an unusual location or containing cmd.exe /c or powershell, started seconds after a network logon.
- **lateral-movement-practice** (lat-r1): Where the credential was stolen. A valid account at a strange source means the secret was exposed earlier, and the analyst has to find that origin to scope the incident.
  - Draft: A valid account at an unusual source means the credential was exposed somewhere first. Look for LSASS access (T1003.001), such as Sysmon Event ID 10 with TargetImage lsass.exe, on hosts where that account recently logged on, since a privileged interactive logon leaves usable secrets in memory. Pass-the-hash (T1550.002) gives an ordinary-looking NTLM 4624 on the target, so the target record alone will not reveal it.
- **linux-fundamentals** (linux-fund-r3): Reading auth.log lines. The room tells analysts to confirm sudo and SSH outcomes in auth.log but never shows a line, so a trainee cannot do the check the log task sends them to.
  - Draft: Authentication events read like sentences. 'Failed password for root from 203.0.113.9 port 51122 ssh2' is a failed SSH guess, and 'Accepted publickey for alice from 10.0.0.5' is a successful key login. A sudo line such as 'alice : TTY=pts/0 ; PWD=/home/alice ; USER=root ; COMMAND=/bin/bash' shows a command that was allowed, while 'www-data : user NOT in sudoers' shows a refusal. Debian and Ubuntu write these to /var/log/auth.log and RHEL family systems to /var/log/secure; some newer installs keep them only in the journal, so use journalctl if the file is missing.
- **linux-fundamentals** (linux-fund-r3): systemd services and timers. On modern Linux, attacker persistence through systemd (ATT&CK T1543.002 and T1053.006) is at least as common as cron, and the room lists only processes, cron and SSH keys.
  - Draft: Most current distributions start background programs through systemd, so a malicious unit file survives reboots just as a cron job does. Look in /etc/systemd/system/ and ~/.config/systemd/user/ for recently changed .service and .timer files, and read each ExecStart line for paths in /tmp, /dev/shm or a home folder. Run systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled and systemctl list-timers to see what will run. Also check shell startup files such as ~/.bashrc, which run a command at every login.
- **linux-fundamentals** (linux-fund-r3): Telling real system activity from fakes. The room warns about a fake kworker but gives no way to confirm a real one or a clean binary, so a trainee will raise false alarms on normal kernel threads.
  - Draft: Real kernel threads appear in ps output in square brackets, such as [kworker/0:1], their parent is PID 2 (kthreadd), and /proc/PID/exe has no target. A kworker without brackets, or with a path on disk, is an impostor. To check whether a system binary was changed, run dpkg -V on Debian or rpm -V on RHEL, and use dpkg -S or rpm -qf to learn which package owns a file. A file no package owns in /usr/bin deserves a closer look.
- **linux-fundamentals** (linux-fund-r3): Preserving evidence before cleanup. The room lists kill -9 with no warning, and a trainee could destroy the running binary and its memory before anyone has examined them.
  - Draft: Do not kill a suspicious process first. Note its PID, parent, owner, command line and open network connections, then copy /proc/PID/exe to safe storage and hash it: this recovers the binary even when the file on disk was deleted. Copy any cron entries, unit files and authorized_keys lines you plan to remove. Killing the process ends its memory contents, so isolate the host from the network first when you can.
- **linux-log-analysis** (linux-logs-r3): Web attacks in Linux logs. The room's description promises web application attacks, but the readings give only a log format and one bullet.
  - Draft: In access.log, look for encoded payloads in the URL, such as UNION SELECT, ../ or %2e%2e%2f, and for one source producing many 404s and then a 200. A request to a new .php file that only one IP ever calls may be a web shell. Confirm on the host: an auditd execve record with uid=33 (www-data on Debian) running /bin/sh means the web server launched a shell, which it should not do.
- **linux-log-analysis** (linux-logs-r2): Login records: wtmp, btmp and lastlog. These are standard login history sources and they can survive when auth.log is edited, yet the room never mentions them.
  - Draft: Linux also keeps binary login records. /var/log/wtmp holds logins and reboots (read with last), /var/log/btmp holds failed logins (read with lastb), and lastlog shows each account's most recent login. Because they are binary, an attacker using sed on auth.log does not touch them, so compare them with auth.log when you suspect tampering; utmpdump prints them as text.
- **linux-log-analysis** (linux-logs-r3): Cron persistence in real log lines. Persistence via cron is a stated subject, but the room shows only a bullet and one CRON line, so an analyst could not tell an edit from a run.
  - Draft: Two different lines matter. A run looks like CRON[12345]: (root) CMD (/tmp/.cache), and an edit through crontab -e looks like crontab[2201]: (www-data) REPLACE (www-data). A service account replacing its crontab is suspicious, and a job running from /tmp or a hidden file is worse. Direct edits to /etc/cron.d or /etc/crontab write no such line, so rely on the auditd cron rules for them.
- **linux-log-analysis** (linux-logs-r2): Separating benign activity. The room has no benign case, so a graduate may escalate every root session and sudo shell.
  - Draft: Before escalating, ask whether the activity is explained. Automation such as Ansible logs in by publickey from a fixed management host at set times, and an admin's sudo use should match a ticket or change window. Check the source IP against known jump hosts, the key fingerprint against the account's usual key, and the time against a change record. The same login from a new source or outside those patterns is the one to work.
- **linux-log-analysis** (linux-logs-r1): Differences on current distributions. Analysts will meet these on current systems. Exact behaviour varies by release, so confirm on the versions in your estate.
  - Draft: Do not assume every file exists. Minimal Debian 12 and later installs may have no rsyslog and no auth.log, so use journalctl there, and on Debian and Ubuntu the SSH unit is usually ssh, not sshd. Recent OpenSSH versions log sessions under sshd-session, so a search for the program name sshd alone can miss them. Journald also keeps logs in /run/log/journal only until reboot unless /var/log/journal is in use.
- **log-entry-anatomy** (loganat-r1): Timestamp formats and time zones. Timelines are built by comparing logs, and the room's own Zeek record uses an epoch time no reading explains.
  - Draft: Timestamps come in several forms. A trailing Z, as in 2026-06-24T14:32:19.000Z, means UTC, and an ending like +03:00 gives the offset from UTC. Epoch time, as in Zeek's ts field, counts seconds since 1 January 1970 UTC and needs converting. Before comparing two logs, put both in UTC.
- **log-entry-anatomy** (loganat-r1): Actor versus target. WHO often has two answers, and mixing them up sends an investigation to the wrong account.
  - Draft: Many logs name two identities: the account that acted and the account acted on. In Windows events SubjectUserName is usually the actor and TargetUserName the affected account, so in a user-creation event the subject is the admin and the target is the new user. Field names do not always follow this pattern, so check the documentation for that event. Always ask who did it and who it was done to.
- **log-entry-anatomy** (loganat-r3): Reading outcome values. The outcome is often a code, not a word, and an analyst must decode it before deciding what happened.
  - Draft: Outcomes vary by product. Firewalls use words such as accept, deny and drop, web logs use status codes such as 200 and 403, and Windows may use a separate event ID plus a hex status, where 0xC000006A means wrong password and 0xC0000234 means a locked account. When a value is unfamiliar, look it up in the vendor's documentation instead of guessing.
- **log-entry-anatomy** (loganat-r8): Correlating records across sources. No single log tells the whole story, and the room asks for correlation without showing how it is done.
  - Draft: To join two logs, use a shared value such as an IP address, a username or a session ID, and a time window. Allow a few seconds or more of difference, because clocks drift and systems log at different moments. Remember each log shows only its own viewpoint: a device behind NAT or a proxy may appear under another address. Treat a match as a lead, then confirm it with a third fact where you can.
- **log-management** (log-mgmt-r2): Time zones and clock sync. An investigation is a timeline, and mismatched clocks can reverse the apparent order of events.
  - Draft: Logs from different systems only line up if their clocks agree, which is why servers and network devices synchronise with NTP. Sources also record time differently, some in UTC and others in local time, so normalisation should convert everything to UTC. A clock that is off by a few minutes can reverse the apparent order of events. When building a timeline, check each source's time zone first.
- **log-management** (log-mgmt-r2): Logging gaps and silent sources. An analyst must know that no result can mean no logging, not no activity.
  - Draft: A log only exists if the system was set to write it. Some useful Windows events, such as process creation (Event ID 4688), are off by default and must be enabled through audit policy. SOC teams also monitor collection health, because a source that stops sending looks the same as a quiet system. Set an alert when an important source goes silent, and treat a sudden stop as suspicious until it is explained.
- **log-management** (log-mgmt-r3): Other ways to stop logging. Clearing is the loud version of tampering, and a benign clear must also be told apart from a malicious one.
  - Draft: Clearing a log is the loud way to hide; attackers can also stop events being written at all. Windows records Event ID 1100 when the event log service shuts down and 4719 when audit policy changes, though an attacker who kills logging quietly may leave neither, so a gap in a busy log is evidence too. On Linux, look for a stopped rsyslog or journald service and files that are suddenly empty. Legitimate clears do happen during approved maintenance, so look for a change ticket before calling one routine.
- **log-management** (log-mgmt-r1): Which log answers which question. The room lists sources but not how a beginner picks one during an investigation.
  - Draft: Start an investigation by asking which log can answer your question. Who logged in: Windows Security events 4624 (success) and 4625 (failure), or /var/log/auth.log on Debian and Ubuntu Linux. What ran: process creation events such as 4688, or EDR telemetry. Where traffic went: firewall, proxy and DNS logs. What changed in the cloud: CloudTrail or the Azure Activity Log.
- **log-sources-integration** (log-src-r3): Treating a silent source as a security event. Attackers stop logging to hide, and the room frames gaps only as technical faults; the exact v19 technique ID for disabling logs was not verified, so the draft names only the tactic.
  - Draft: A silent source can be a security event, not just a technical fault. Attackers stop agents and turn off logging to hide, which ATT&CK v19 places under Defense Impairment (TA0112), and Windows writes Event ID 1102 when the Security log is cleared. Track the last-seen time of each source and alert when it stays quiet longer than its normal gap. Treat an unexplained stop on a monitored host as suspicious until you know the cause.
- **log-sources-integration** (log-src-r1): Sources only log what is enabled. An analyst must know a missing event may never have been generated, and the room assumes every source records everything.
  - Draft: An agent can ship only what the source records. On Windows, process creation auditing and its command-line field (Event ID 4688) must be enabled, PowerShell script block logging (Event ID 4104) must be turned on, and Sysmon must be installed and configured. Cloud services vary too: AWS CloudTrail does not record S3 object-level data events unless you enable them. Before trusting a detection, check that the source generates the events it depends on.
- **log-sources-integration** (log-src-r1): Securing transport and time. The room tests that Syslog on 514 is not encrypted without teaching it, and wrong clocks break timelines regardless of parsing.
  - Draft: Classic Syslog on port 514 is plain text, so anyone on the path can read or alter it. Syslog over TLS (RFC 5425, usually TCP 6514) encrypts the stream and verifies the collector. Use it for anything crossing untrusted networks, and make sure devices buffer logs while the collector is unreachable. Every source also needs NTP time sync, because a wrong clock produces timestamps no parser can fix.
- **log-sources-integration** (log-src-r3): Event time versus ingestion time. Late logs make rules miss real activity, and analysts misread timelines without this distinction.
  - Draft: Each event has two times: when it happened and when the SIEM received it. Elastic can store these as @timestamp and event.ingested, and Sentinel exposes TimeGenerated and ingestion_time(). If a rule searches the last 5 minutes by event time and logs arrive 10 minutes late, it misses them entirely. Measure the delay per source and allow for it in rule windows.
- **log-sources-integration** (log-src-r3): Spotting parser failures. Event counts can look healthy while field extraction is broken, so rules go blind with no visible outage.
  - Draft: Parsers fail quietly. Logstash tags such events _grokparsefailure, and a vendor format change after an upgrade can break extraction while the event count looks normal. Rules then miss activity because the field is empty, not because the activity stopped. After any device upgrade, recheck a sample event, and monitor the rate of unparsed events per source.
- **macos-security-fundamentals** (macf-r3): How the quarantine check is bypassed. The most common real Gatekeeper bypasses rely on the quarantine mark never being set or being removed, and an analyst who assumes every download carries it will misread the chain. T1553.001 is confirmed live on attack.mitre.org under Defense Impairment.
  - Draft: The quarantine mark is set by the app doing the download, so a tool that does not take part leaves it off: a file fetched with curl in Terminal normally has no com.apple.quarantine attribute and Gatekeeper never checks it. Attackers use this by getting users to paste a curl one-liner into Terminal, or they strip the mark with xattr -d com.apple.quarantine. ATT&CK tracks this as T1553.001, Gatekeeper Bypass. Since macOS 15 Sequoia, Control-click no longer overrides Gatekeeper, so the user must approve the app in System Settings under Privacy & Security, and lure pages now walk victims through those steps.
- **macos-security-fundamentals** (macf-r8): Responding to a macOS compromise. The room ends at finding the persistence item, and an analyst must know how to contain and clean up on a Mac.
  - Draft: Contain the Mac through the EDR first, then collect the plist and its target binary and hash them before removing anything. Unload the item with launchctl bootout, then delete the plist and the binary it starts. If a password was captured, change the login password, rotate everything saved in the Keychain and browsers, and revoke web sessions, because the attacker may already hold those secrets. If anything ran as root, check SIP with csrutil status and plan a rebuild, since a clean-up alone cannot prove the machine is clean.
- **macos-security-fundamentals** (macf-r4): Checking the facts on a live Mac. EDR fields are not always available, and an analyst needs the commands that show signature, Gatekeeper and download origin directly.
  - Draft: On the Mac itself, codesign -dv --verbose=4 followed by the app path shows the signer, and spctl -a -vv shows what Gatekeeper would decide and whether the app is notarized. xattr -l shows whether a file carries com.apple.quarantine. The QuarantineEventsV2 database in the user's Library/Preferences folder records download events, often with the source URL. launchctl list shows what is loaded for the current user.
- **macos-security-fundamentals** (macf-r6): The full stealer chain and its exfiltration. The room stops at reading the Keychain, but commodity macOS stealers such as Atomic (AMOS) go on to send the loot out, and that network step is often the alert an analyst sees.
  - Draft: Commodity macOS stealers such as Atomic Stealer (AMOS) follow a short chain: a fake password prompt, then a sweep of the Keychain, browser stores, wallet files and documents into a temporary folder, then an archive sent to the attacker. The last step often appears as curl posting a zip file to an unfamiliar host from the same process tree. ATT&CK tracks the two credential steps as T1555.001, Keychain, and T1555.003, Credentials from Web Browsers. Treat osascript, security and an outbound curl within minutes of each other as one incident, not three alerts.
- **macos-security-fundamentals** (macf-r8): Persistence outside launchd plists. Login Items appear as a graded distractor but no reading explains them, and an analyst who checks only the two launchd folders will miss other common persistence. T1547.015 is confirmed live on attack.mitre.org.
  - Draft: LaunchAgents (T1543.001) and LaunchDaemons (T1543.004) are the main routes but not the only ones. Login Items (T1547.015) start an app when the user logs in, and since macOS 13 Ventura the system notifies the user when a background item is added, while sudo sfltool dumpbtm lists them all. Shell startup files such as ~/.zshrc (T1546.004) run commands whenever a shell opens. Check these too before you state that a Mac has no persistence.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals** (malware-r3): Sandbox evasion. An analyst who trusts a quiet sandbox report will clear live malware, and the room never says a sandbox can be fooled.
  - Draft: A quiet sandbox report does not clear a file. Malware can sleep past the 60 to 180 second run, check for signs of a virtual machine, wait for mouse movement or stay idle unless the machine belongs to the target's domain or language. ATT&CK tracks this as T1497, Virtualization/Sandbox Evasion. If a suspicious file does nothing, look for long sleep calls and VM checks, then rerun it for longer or interactively before you conclude anything.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals** (malware-r2): Samples that are not .exe files. The static section covers only PE files, while much of what reaches a SOC is a document, script or shortcut, and the room itself grades a macro case.
  - Draft: Much of what arrives in a SOC is not an .exe. Office files with macros can be examined without opening them using olevba from the oletools set, which extracts the macro code and flags suspicious keywords. Scripts such as PowerShell, JavaScript or VBS are plain text, so read them in a text editor and decode any Base64 step by step. A .lnk shortcut hides its real command in its target field. Never double-click these to find out what they do.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals** (malware-r2): Recognising packed samples. Packing is mentioned once in passing, yet a packed file makes static results nearly empty and an analyst must recognise that rather than conclude nothing is there.
  - Draft: A packer compresses or encrypts a program so that its real code only appears in memory at run time (T1027.002). Signs include very few readable strings, a very short import list often limited to LoadLibrary and GetProcAddress, and section entropy above about 7 on a scale of 8. Common packers such as UPX can sometimes be reversed with upx -d. When a sample looks packed, static results say little, so move on to dynamic analysis.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals** (malware-r3): Handling samples safely. An analyst passing or running samples without these habits can infect a colleague or tip off the attacker.
  - Draft: Store and pass samples in a password-protected archive, by convention using the password infected, so that nobody runs one by accident and security tools do not remove it. Write indicators in a safe form, such as hxxp://bad[.]example, so they cannot be clicked. In a local lab, take a clean snapshot, cut the VM off from real networks and use a fake internet service such as INetSim or FakeNet-NG. Remember that a public upload can be seen by the attacker, while a hash lookup reveals far less.
- **malware-analysis-fundamentals** (malware-r1): Turning findings into action. The room ends at extracting indicators and never says how to rank them or what to do with them, which is the point of analysis in a SOC.
  - Draft: Indicators age at different speeds: changing one byte changes a hash, and IP addresses and domains are cheap to replace, while behaviour is much harder for an attacker to change. Use each finding right away: block it, search all endpoints for it, note the earliest sighting to set the incident's scope, and record it in the ticket. Then write a detection for the behaviour, not only the hash. Import hashes and fuzzy hashes such as ssdeep can also find close variants that an exact hash misses.
- **malware-types** (malware-r5): First moves on active ransomware. The room says when to worry but not what to do, and the first minutes decide how many systems are lost.
  - Draft: When you see the pre-encryption sequence, escalate at once and isolate the affected hosts through EDR or the network rather than powering them off, so memory evidence survives. Disable the account doing the mass file access and protect the backups, because attackers go after them next. Keep any ransom note and samples for the incident team.
- **malware-types** (malware-r1): How malware arrives. The room's own promise is 'how does it arrive', yet delivery is only mentioned in passing, and the delivery path tells an analyst where to look for other victims.
  - Draft: Most malware reaches a company by one of a few routes: an email attachment or link, a download from a fake or compromised site, a malicious ad, a USB drive, or an exposed service with an unpatched flaw or weak password. Knowing the route tells you where to scope. An email route means searching the mail gateway for the same message, and a web route means searching proxy logs for the same URL.
- **malware-types** (malware-r6): Cryptominer telemetry. The room says to investigate miners thoroughly but gives no way to recognise one.
  - Draft: A miner shows up as sustained high CPU or GPU use by one process, often with a tool name such as xmrig or a renamed copy of it. Command lines often carry a pool address and a wallet string, and network logs show long-lived connections to mining pools, frequently on unusual ports. Finding these answers what ran; your next question is how it got in.
- **malware-types** (malware-r7): Handling a suspected rootkit. The room says a clean scan proves nothing, then stops, leaving the analyst with no next step.
  - Draft: If you suspect a rootkit, stop trusting what the host reports about itself. Compare it with evidence from outside the host, such as firewall, proxy and DNS logs, and escalate for memory or offline disk analysis. A host with confirmed kernel-level or boot-level compromise is rebuilt, not cleaned.
- **memory-disk-forensics** (mdf-r4): Disk artifacts that prove execution. The room's disk side is almost entirely NTFS metadata, and an analyst must be able to show that a program actually ran, which $MFT and $UsnJrnl cannot do.
  - Draft: NTFS metadata shows a file existed, not that it ran. Prefetch files in C:\Windows\Prefetch record a program's run count and recent run times, but Prefetch is off by default on Windows Server. Amcache and ShimCache record that a binary was present on the system, and on modern Windows neither alone proves execution. SRUM (SRUDB.dat) keeps per-application network byte counts for weeks, which helps to size an exfiltration.
- **memory-disk-forensics** (mdf-r2): A practical Volatility triage sequence. The room says what memory can hold but never how to work through an image, so an analyst handed a dump has no order of operations. Plugin paths have been reorganised in newer Volatility 3 releases, so confirm exact names against the installed version.
  - Draft: A workable first pass on a Windows image runs windows.pstree for parent-child oddities, then windows.psscan compared with windows.pslist to find hidden processes. windows.cmdline shows how each process was launched, windows.netscan ties connections to processes, and windows.malfind flags executable memory with no backing file. malfind output needs review, because some legitimate software such as browsers and security agents also creates executable private memory.
- **memory-disk-forensics** (mdf-r6): BitLocker and the power-off decision. Powering off an encrypted host can lock the analyst out of the disk entirely, and the room's power-off discussion weighs only RAM loss.
  - Draft: On a BitLocker-protected host the volume is readable while the system runs and sealed once it powers off. Before any shutdown, confirm that the recovery key is available from Entra ID or Active Directory. If it is not, take a live logical image first, because a dead-box image without the key is unreadable.
- **memory-disk-forensics** (mdf-r2): Memory evidence on disk after power-off. When RAM is already gone, the analyst needs to know that some of it may survive in two files.
  - Draft: If a host is already off, memory is not always a total loss. hiberfil.sys holds a compressed copy of memory from the last hibernation or Fast Startup, and pagefile.sys holds fragments paged out of RAM. Collect both from the disk image. Convert the hibernation file with a dedicated tool before analysis, and search the page file with strings or YARA, since it is not a complete image.
- **mfa-session-token-attacks** (mfast-r5): Containing session theft. Only Golden SAML gets containment steps, and session replay is the case an analyst will meet most often.
  - Draft: Once replay is confirmed, the cookie is the problem, so a password reset alone is not enough. Revoke the user's sign-in sessions and refresh tokens, reset the password, and review registered authentication methods, inbox rules and OAuth grants for anything the attacker added. An already-issued access token can stay valid until it expires, commonly about an hour, unless the resource supports Continuous Access Evaluation. Then find the phishing message and check who else clicked.
- **mfa-session-token-attacks** (mfast-r4): Cookie theft by infostealer. The room presents T1539 only through AiTM, so an analyst finding replay with no lure would have no explanation.
  - Draft: T1539 does not need a proxy. Infostealer malware running on the user's device copies cookies straight out of the browser profile, so the sign-in log shows the same replay pattern with no phishing page behind it. If you cannot find a lure or look-alike domain, check the user's device in EDR for a stealer and treat every browser session on it as stolen.
- **mfa-session-token-attacks** (mfast-r5): Finding the AiTM proxy upstream. Replay detection starts after the theft, while the earlier signs find other victims; Entra detection names and licensing change, so confirm them against current Microsoft documentation.
  - Draft: Look upstream of the replay too: the first sign-in in an AiTM attack comes from the proxy's address, not the user's usual network, even though the user is at their desk. Mail and web-proxy logs show the click on the look-alike domain, which lets you find other recipients. Entra ID Protection detections such as Anomalous Token and Attacker in the Middle can fire here, but they depend on licensing and may arrive late, so treat them as a bonus rather than a control.
- **mfa-session-token-attacks** (mfast-r6): Responding to illicit consent. T1528 detection is taught, but not how to remove the access, and a password reset does not remove it.
  - Draft: To contain a bad consent grant, remove the app's delegated permission grants and delete or disable its service principal in the tenant, then revoke the user's refresh tokens. A password reset alone does not remove a grant. Search the audit log for the same app across other users, since one malicious app often collects several consents, and restrict user consent to verified publishers or admin approval afterwards.
- **mfa-session-token-attacks** (mfast-r5): Session pivot in Okta. The room says both identity providers matter but teaches replay only in Entra fields; confirm the field against current Okta System Log documentation.
  - Draft: Okta has its own session pivot: the System Log field authenticationContext.externalSessionId ties events to one session. The same value appearing with a different client IP, ASN or user agent is the Okta counterpart of a repeated Entra sessionId.
- **microsoft-365-security** (m365-sec-r3): Reading Entra ID sign-in logs. Most M365 compromises are confirmed in the sign-in log, which the room barely mentions; retention depends on licensing and should be verified per tenant.
  - Draft: Entra ID sign-in logs show who signed in, from where and how, separately from the audit log. Key fields are ResultType (0 means success, 50126 means bad credentials), IPAddress, AppDisplayName, ConditionalAccessStatus and AuthenticationRequirement. Successful MFA does not clear a sign-in, because adversary-in-the-middle phishing steals the session after MFA is done, so a new location or device right after a phishing click still matters. Check non-interactive sign-ins too, since token reuse appears there.
- **microsoft-365-security** (m365-sec-r3): BEC response end to end. The room lists remediation in one line, and a real BEC case also has mailbox scoping and a financial side.
  - Draft: First export the malicious rule as evidence, then revoke sessions, reset the password, and remove rules and forwarding. Check for added MFA methods and consented apps, or the attacker may walk back in. Scope with message trace and MailItemsAccessed, remembering that Bind entries are individual items while Sync entries cover a whole folder. Tell finance quickly, because a fraudulent payment may still be stoppable.
- **microsoft-365-security** (m365-sec-r3): Persistence beyond inbox rules. Attackers who lose the password keep access through changes an analyst must know to check.
  - Draft: After an account takeover, look for persistence other than inbox rules. Audit operations worth checking include User registered security info (a new MFA method), Add service principal credentials, Add-MailboxPermission (delegate access to a mailbox), New-TransportRule (an org-wide mail rule) and Update conditional access policy. Compare each with the time of the compromise and confirm any change with its owner.
- **microsoft-365-security** (m365-sec-r2): Email authentication and headers. The room covers spoof protection without SPF, DKIM or DMARC, which are the first things read in a phishing header.
  - Draft: SPF checks whether the sending server may send for a domain, DKIM checks a signature over the message, and DMARC checks that one of them passed and aligns with the visible From domain. The Authentication-Results header shows the outcome, including Microsoft's compauth result. A pass does not prove a mail is safe, because an attacker can pass all three on a domain they own. A fail on your own domain is a strong spoofing sign.
- **microsoft-365-security** (m365-sec-r2): Hunting and removing delivered mail. Once one phish is found, an analyst must find every recipient and clean up, and the room stops at the quarantine.
  - Draft: Advanced hunting tables such as EmailEvents, EmailUrlInfo and UrlClickEvents show who received a message and who clicked. For example: EmailEvents | where SenderFromDomain == "corp-company.com" | project Timestamp, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction. Delivered mail can then be removed from Threat Explorer or the hunting results, while Zero-hour auto purge removes some mail automatically after a verdict changes. Check who clicked before closing the case.
- **mitre-attack** (mitre-r3): How to map an alert to a technique. Analysts tag alerts daily and wrong tags mislead reports; the room shows a finished mapping but not the method, which CISA's ATT&CK mapping guide covers.
  - Draft: To map an alert, start from what actually happened, not from a keyword: write the behaviour down in one sentence. Ask what the attacker gained from it to find the tactic, then find the technique and, only if the evidence supports it, the sub-technique. Check the technique's description and procedure examples to confirm the fit. If the evidence only supports the parent technique, tag the parent rather than guessing.
- **mitre-attack** (mitre-r3): A technique is not a verdict. A new analyst who treats every tagged event as an attack will over-escalate normal admin work.
  - Draft: An ATT&CK tag describes a behaviour, not proof of an attack. Many techniques are also normal work: admins run PowerShell (T1059.001), schedule tasks and log in remotely every day. Before calling an alert malicious, check who did it, from where, whether it is normal for them, and whether a change ticket explains it. The tag tells you what to check next, not what to conclude.
- **mitre-attack** (mitre-r2): Tactics are not a fixed sequence. The reading lists tactics in order of attack progression, and a learner may wrongly expect every attack to follow that path.
  - Draft: The numbered tactic order is a common pattern, not a fixed path. Attackers skip tactics, repeat them and return to earlier ones: after Lateral Movement they often run Discovery and Credential Access again on the new host. One technique can also sit under several tactics, such as T1078 Valid Accounts. So use an alert's tactic to ask what may have come before and after it, and do not assume the attack must follow the list.
- **mitre-attack** (mitre-r3): Limits of a coverage map. The room sells Navigator colouring as showing coverage, and a false sense of coverage is the classic misuse.
  - Draft: A coloured cell in Navigator does not mean a technique is covered. One rule usually catches only certain ways of performing a technique, so an attacker can use another procedure and stay invisible. Rate coverage honestly, for example none, partial or tested, and test rules by simulating the behaviour. Also check that the log source each rule needs is actually being collected.
- **nac-masterclass** (nac-r10): Responding to a NAC finding. An analyst who confirms a spoofed or rogue device must locate it and cut it off, and no reading covers any response step.
  - Draft: When NAC logs point to a rogue or spoofed device, find it first: the RADIUS accounting record gives the switch, the port, the endpoint MAC and the session times. Save the evidence before you act, including the ISE session details, the profiler history and the switch's MAC table. In Cisco ISE, Adaptive Network Control (ANC) lets an operator apply a quarantine, shut-down or port-bounce policy to an endpoint, and the switch receives that change on the live session. If a hub or implant is suspected, send someone to the physical port.
- **nac-masterclass** (nac-r7): Reading authentication outcomes in ISE and ClearPass. Analysts work from RADIUS Live Logs and Access Tracker every day, and the room shows only one posture event and raw RADIUS packets.
  - Draft: ISE's RADIUS Live Logs show one row per attempt with the status, the identity, the endpoint MAC, the switch and port, the authorization profile and a failure reason. Message code 5200 means authentication succeeded, 5400 means it failed, and 5411 means the supplicant stopped responding, which often points to a client or certificate problem. Group failures by MAC and by port before you judge them: one MAC trying many usernames is very different from one user with a bad password. ClearPass shows the same data in Access Tracker.
- **nac-masterclass** (nac-r10): Telling NAC noise from attacks. Most NAC failures and profile changes are ordinary, and without this an analyst either escalates every one or dismisses the real one.
  - Draft: Most NAC alerts are not attacks. An expired machine certificate, a password changed while the old one is cached in the supplicant, a re-imaged laptop, a new docking station with its own MAC address or a new device model the profiler does not know all cause failures or profile changes. Check who owns the MAC in the asset inventory, whether an IT ticket explains it and whether the behaviour fits that device. A profile change on a MAB entry, one MAC trying many usernames, or a new MAC on a meeting-room port at night deserves a closer look.
- **nac-masterclass** (nac-r5): Change of Authorization (CoA). The room says access is set by the Access-Accept but never explains how a live session changes after posture, which is also how SOC-driven quarantine works.
  - Draft: RADIUS normally answers only when the switch asks, so the server needs another way to change a session that is already running. Change of Authorization (CoA, RFC 5176) lets ISE push a message to the switch, which then re-authenticates the port, bounces it or applies new access rules. This is how a device moves from limited access to full access once its agent reports Compliant, and how a quarantine takes effect without a reconnect. ISE logs a successful CoA as Dynamic Authorization succeeded, message code 5205.
- **nac-masterclass** (nac-r2): Deployment modes and fail-open behaviour. The room says an unauthorized port passes only EAPOL, but monitor mode and critical authentication change that, so a log can show a failure while the device still has access.
  - Draft: A failed authentication does not always mean a blocked device. In monitor mode the port stays open and failures are only logged, which is how most rollouts begin; low-impact mode allows a small set of traffic before authentication; closed mode is the strict behaviour described here. If a switch cannot reach any RADIUS server, critical authentication can place ports in a chosen VLAN so people can keep working. Always check which mode a port runs in before you decide what a NAC log line means.
- **networking-fundamentals** (net-fund-r3): How DNS resolution works. DNS logs are among the first evidence an analyst uses, yet the google.com walkthrough skips name lookup entirely.
  - Draft: Before your laptop can reach google.com it must turn the name into an IP address, which is the job of DNS. It asks its configured resolver, which checks its cache or asks other name servers, then returns records such as A (IPv4 address), AAAA (IPv6), MX (mail server) or CNAME (alias). DNS logs show which host asked for which name and when, so they often reveal malware contacting its server before any other alert. Look for newly seen domains, random-looking names, and unusually long or frequent queries.
- **networking-fundamentals** (net-fund-r3): What a firewall does. The room's only log is a firewall log, but no reading explains firewalls, rules or allow versus deny.
  - Draft: A firewall decides which traffic may pass by checking rules against source, destination, port and protocol, and it records each decision. A stateful firewall remembers open connections, so replies to an allowed outbound request return without a separate rule. Rules are usually read top to bottom, and the last rule normally denies whatever is left, so a default-allow rule on outbound traffic is a weakness. An IDS only alerts on suspicious traffic, while an IPS sits in the path and can block it.
- **networking-fundamentals** (net-fund-r3): Reading a connection log. The description promises this skill, but no reading walks through a log's fields.
  - Draft: Read a connection log in a fixed order: who (source IP and host), to where (destination IP and country), on what (port and protocol), what the firewall did (action and matched rule), and how much moved. Compare bytes sent and received: a large upload suggests data leaving, while repeated small connections at regular times suggest malware checking in. An ESTABLISHED state with a long duration means the two sides really talked. An action of allow only means the firewall let it pass, not that the traffic was safe.
- **networking-fundamentals** (net-fund-r3): What to do with a suspicious connection. The room flags the connection but never says how to confirm it or respond.
  - Draft: A suspicious connection is a starting point, not a verdict. Find which process made it on the host, for example with EDR or netstat -ano on Windows, and check the destination IP's reputation. Search for the same destination across other hosts to see how far it spread. If the process is unknown and the destination is bad, escalate and ask for the host to be isolated; if it is a known business tool with an owner, document that and close the alert.
- **networking-fundamentals** (net-fund-r3): Basic network tools. Analysts verify network facts with these daily, and the room's Toolkit section names concepts but almost no tools.
  - Draft: A few simple tools answer most network questions. ping checks whether a host replies, traceroute shows the routers along the path, and nslookup shows what a name resolves to. netstat lists a machine's current connections and listening ports, and Wireshark or tcpdump capture the packets themselves. Flow and firewall logs tell you that two hosts talked, while a packet capture shows what they said.
- **networking-protocols** (net-prot-r3): Reading a connection record. A beginner must read firewall logs on day one and the room never explains source versus destination ports or bytes.
  - Draft: A client picks a random high source port and connects to the service's well-known destination port, so the destination port usually names the service. A firewall record gives source IP, source port, destination IP, destination port, protocol, action and bytes. Compare bytes sent with bytes received: a workstation sending far more than it receives on port 443 can mean an upload. Action allow means the connection passed, not that it was safe.
- **networking-protocols** (net-prot-r2): Kerberos and LDAP. Active Directory attacks run over these two protocols, and the room never teaches them.
  - Draft: Kerberos (port 88) is how Windows domain logons get tickets from a domain controller, and LDAP (port 389, or 636 with TLS) is how clients query the directory for users and groups. Workstations talking to domain controllers on these ports is normal. One workstation making thousands of LDAP queries in minutes can mean an attacker mapping the domain with a tool such as SharpHound, and Kerberos traffic to anything other than a domain controller is unusual.
- **networking-protocols** (net-prot-r1): HTTP in logs. The room promises how attackers exploit each protocol, yet HTTP gets status codes only, with no attack or log example.
  - Draft: A web server or proxy record shows the client IP, method, URL, status code, bytes and User-Agent. Many 404s from one IP usually mean someone is probing paths, and a 200 on a new .php or .aspx file can mean a web shell. A large POST to an unknown site can be data leaving. The User-Agent is set by the client, so it is a clue and never proof.
- **networking-protocols** (net-prot-r1): Investigating HTTPS without decryption. The reading calls encrypted C2 a detection challenge and then gives no way to work on it.
  - Draft: Without decryption you still have the destination IP, usually the hostname in the SNI field of the handshake, plus timing and bytes. Check how new the domain is, whether other hosts visit it, and whether connections repeat at regular intervals. HTTP/3 runs over QUIC on UDP port 443, so look at UDP 443 as well as TCP.
- **networking-protocols** (net-prot-r3): First response steps. The room stops at calling something an incident, and a junior analyst must know what comes next.
  - Draft: Match the response to what happened. After a successful brute force, find the account and session, reset the password, end the session, and check what it touched. For workstation-to-workstation SMB or DNS tunneling, follow your runbook to isolate the source host and look for other hosts showing the same pattern. Blocking an IP alone is never the finish, because the attacker may already be inside.
- **okta-identity-fundamentals** (oktaf-r8): Containing a compromised Okta account. The room never says how to act, yet containment is graded twice.
  - Draft: Containing a compromised Okta account takes several separate actions, because a password reset alone does not end an open session. In the Admin Console, clear the user's sessions (logged as user.session.clear), then reset the password and review enrolled factors, removing any the real user does not recognise. Revert group and role changes, revoke API tokens the account created, and confirm each change in the System Log.
- **okta-identity-fundamentals** (oktaf-r5): Factor lifecycle events. An attacker-enrolled factor is a top persistence move, and the reading names no event for it.
  - Draft: Factor changes have their own eventType values: user.mfa.factor.activate records a factor being enrolled, user.mfa.factor.deactivate removes one, and user.mfa.factor.reset_all clears them all. Compare the time and client.ipAddress of a new enrolment against the user's normal network and any helpdesk ticket. A new factor added shortly after a suspicious sign-in, or from a hosting network, should be treated as attacker persistence until proven otherwise.
- **okta-identity-fundamentals** (oktaf-r8): Scoping what a session reached. After a compromise the analyst must list which applications the attacker entered, and the room stops at Okta itself.
  - Draft: After a session opens, each application the user enters through single sign-on writes user.authentication.sso, with the application named in the target. Filter on the account and the suspect window to list every app reached, then continue the investigation in those apps' own logs. Also compare client.ipAddress across events sharing one externalSessionId, because a session that jumps to a new network can mean a stolen session cookie, which no sign-in event would show.
- **okta-identity-fundamentals** (oktaf-r8): Direct admin grants and identity provider changes. Admin rights can be granted without any group change, so watching only group membership misses them; check these names against Okta's current event catalog before publishing.
  - Draft: Group membership is not the only route to admin rights. user.account.privilege.grant records an admin role given directly to a user, and group.privilege.grant records one given to a group. system.idp.lifecycle.create records a new identity provider, which attackers holding super admin rights have used to sign in as other users through a trusted external login. Treat all three as high priority when no change ticket exists.
- **okta-identity-fundamentals** (oktaf-r4): Benign failure bursts. Failed sign-ins are constant, and the room gives little for closing a harmless one.
  - Draft: Most failed sign-ins are harmless: a mistyped password, or a phone or app retrying an old saved password after a change. Those usually come from the user's normal ISP and device, stop soon, and may end in a normal success. A burst from hosting infrastructure, or an unusual client, is different, and many accounts failing from one source points to password spraying. Confirm a benign case against the user's recent password change and usual network before closing.
- **osint-fundamentals** (osint-r2): Interpreting reputation results. The room shows how to look something up but not how to judge the answer, which is where most wrong verdicts come from.
  - Draft: A lookup result is evidence, not a verdict. A low detection count on VirusTotal can simply mean a new indicator, and one or two vendor flags on a shared hosting or CDN IP may refer to a different customer on that address. Check how recent the report is, whether the IP belongs to a CDN or cloud provider, and whether a source such as GreyNoise shows it as internet-wide background scanning. Then combine the result with what your own logs show.
- **osint-fundamentals** (osint-r2): Strong and weak pivots. Pivoting on a shared attribute can tie innocent sites to an attacker, and the room presents every pivot as equally reliable.
  - Draft: Not all pivots are equal. A shared IP on a CDN or shared host, or a free certificate authority, links many unrelated sites, so it proves little. A certificate's SHA-256 fingerprint, a reused unusual banner, a favicon hash, or a JARM TLS fingerprint is far more specific, and a certificate common name sits between the two. Confirm each new indicator with a second independent signal before you block it or link it to an actor.
- **osint-fundamentals** (osint-r2): Certificate transparency and passive DNS in practice. osint-r1 lists both as sources, but no tool or use is taught, and they are the standard way to find lookalike domains and an actor's history.
  - Draft: Every publicly trusted certificate is recorded in certificate transparency logs, so crt.sh shows certificates issued for a domain, including subdomains and lookalikes. Searching your own brand there can reveal a phishing site before the first email goes out. Passive DNS services show which IPs a domain resolved to over time, and which domains shared an IP, without touching the target. This is how you find infrastructure an actor has since moved away from.
- **osint-fundamentals** (osint-r3): Recording and sharing findings. Enrichment only has value when it reaches a ticket or a block list safely, and the room never covers handling indicators.
  - Draft: Record each finding with its source, the time you checked it, and your confidence, because infrastructure changes and a result can go stale within days. Defang indicators when writing them, for example hxxp://bad[.]example, so nobody clicks them by accident. Mark sharing limits with the Traffic Light Protocol (CLEAR, GREEN, AMBER, AMBER+STRICT, RED), and give blocks an expiry so that reused addresses are not blocked forever.
- **osint-fundamentals** (osint-r1): Checking your own exposure. osint-r1 names attacker reconnaissance awareness as one of the three purposes but gives no way to do it.
  - Draft: To see what attackers see, search Shodan for your own organisation with filters such as org: and ssl.cert.subject.cn:, and review any exposed remote access, databases or admin panels. Check breach notification services for your domain's leaked accounts, and search public code repositories for your domain name to catch committed secrets. Report what you find to the asset owner rather than probing it further.
- **ot-ics-security** (ot-r6): Telemetry at Purdue Levels 2 and 3. OT intrusions are often visible first on Windows hosts and boundary devices where a SOC does have logs, and the room reads as if only the wire exists.
  - Draft: The no-agent rule applies to Levels 0 and 1. Engineering workstations, HMIs, historians and jump hosts at Levels 2 and 3 are usually Windows machines that produce Windows Event Logs, and some can run a vendor-approved agent. Collect those logs along with firewall, VPN and remote-access logs from the Level 3.5 boundary, and correlate them with the sensor feed. An unusual logon on the engineering workstation minutes before a write is often the evidence that connects an IT intrusion to a plant event.
- **ot-ics-security** (ot-r5): Recognising logic, firmware and mode changes. Sign 5 calls this the more serious finding but gives no way to recognise it.
  - Draft: A project download, a PLC stop or a controller switched to program mode outranks a single register write. ATT&CK for ICS tracks these as T0843 Program Download, T0889 Modify Program, T0839 Module Firmware and T0858 Change Operating Mode. On the wire they appear as vendor-specific functions, such as S7comm stop and download requests on port 102, so learn which functions your sensor decodes. Outside a ticketed change window, escalate any of them to plant engineering at once.
- **ot-ics-security** (ot-r4): Recent attacks on Modbus and water systems. An analyst must know this is not only historic nation-state work: plain Modbus commands and exposed devices have recently caused real harm.
  - Draft: In January 2024 FrostyGoop, reported by Dragos, sent Modbus/TCP commands over port 502 to heating controllers in Lviv, Ukraine, and residents of more than 600 apartment buildings lost heating for about two days. In late 2023 the Iran-linked CyberAv3ngers defaced internet-exposed Unitronics PLCs at US water utilities, including Aliquippa, Pennsylvania, by way of default passwords, as described in CISA advisory AA23-335A. Both fit this room directly: no protocol authentication, exposed devices and default credentials (T0883, T0812).
- **ot-ics-security** (ot-r5): The operator view can be falsified. Stuxnet shows readings can be faked, so an analyst must not close a case because the HMI looks normal.
  - Draft: An attacker who can write to a controller may also be able to lie to the screen, so a normal HMI value does not prove the process is normal. ATT&CK for ICS names this T0856 Spoof Reporting Message and T0832 Manipulation of View. After a suspicious write, ask engineers to check an independent source such as a local gauge, a lab sample or historian trends before anyone concludes that nothing changed.
- **persistence-mechanisms** (persist-r5): Cloud persistence in the logs. persist-r5 explains why cloud persistence survives a reset but names no log source or event, so an analyst cannot actually run the check it demands. Activity names can shift between Microsoft releases, so confirm them in the tenant.
  - Draft: In Microsoft Entra ID these actions appear in the audit log: look for Consent to application, Add service principal credentials, new role assignments, and User registered security info, which shows an attacker adding their own MFA method. Mailbox changes appear in the Unified Audit Log as New-InboxRule, Set-InboxRule or Set-Mailbox with a forwarding address. Attackers also add a secret to an existing app registration, which gives them a login that no user password controls, so review credentials on existing apps as well as new consents.
- **persistence-mechanisms** (persist-r2): Logging prerequisites. The room presents Event 4698 and Sysmon as simply available, and an analyst who finds no 4698 could wrongly conclude no task was created.
  - Draft: Event 4698 is written only when the Audit Other Object Access Events subcategory is enabled, and it is off in a default configuration. Sysmon events exist only where Sysmon is deployed. Before treating an empty search as a clean result, confirm the log source is collected on that host, and where it is not, inspect the task files in C:\Windows\System32\Tasks directly.
- **persistence-mechanisms** (persist-r2): Linux persistence beyond cron. Linux servers are common footholds, and an analyst who checks only cron would miss the mechanisms attackers use most often there.
  - Draft: Cron is not the only option on Linux. A systemd service or timer under /etc/systemd/system, or under ~/.config/systemd/user for one user, runs at boot or on a schedule. An attacker's public key added to ~/.ssh/authorized_keys gives login that survives a password change, and a line added to ~/.bashrc runs at every interactive shell. A Linux sweep should check all of these, and auditd file watches on those paths catch new writes.
- **persistence-mechanisms** (persist-r4): Other Windows autostart locations and a full sweep tool. The room's sweep list ends at BITS and WMI, so a hunt could miss Winlogon, IFEO and COM hijacks, and the learner is never told how to enumerate autostarts in practice.
  - Draft: Several less obvious Windows locations also launch code: the Winlogon Userinit and Shell values, Image File Execution Options Debugger values, and COM object hijacks under HKCU\Software\Classes. Sysinternals Autoruns lists these and most other autostart locations in one view, and it can hide signed Microsoft entries so unusual ones stand out. Run it against a known-good baseline and investigate the differences.
- **phishing-analysis** (phishing-r2): Device code and OAuth consent phishing. These lures steal access with no fake login page and no password, so URL and header checks can come back clean.
  - Draft: Some phishing steals access without a fake page. In device code phishing the attacker sends a real sign-in code and the victim enters it on the genuine Microsoft device login page, which hands the attacker tokens; in Entra sign-in logs look for authenticationProtocol deviceCode on a user who has no such device. In consent phishing the victim approves a malicious app, so look for a consent entry in the Entra audit log, and remove the app, because a password reset does not cancel its grant.
- **phishing-analysis** (phishing-r3): From click to account compromise. After a click, the question that sets severity is whether the account was taken over, and the room stops at UrlClickEvents.
  - Draft: When someone clicked, line up the click time with that user's sign-in logs. A successful sign-in from an unfamiliar IP or device within minutes is evidence of stolen credentials or a stolen session, even when MFA shows as satisfied. Then check for new inbox rules, forwarding, new MFA methods and OAuth grants, since these are what attackers set up first.
- **phishing-analysis** (phishing-r1): Reading a raw authentication header. The log analysis tests SPF passing with DMARC failing, and the room never shows a real header or what the DMARC policy does.
  - Draft: A real result reads: Authentication-Results: mx.contoso.com; spf=pass smtp.mailfrom=relay.example; dkim=none; dmarc=fail action=none header.from=partner.example. Compare smtp.mailfrom with header.from: SPF passed, but for a different domain, so it does not align and DMARC fails. The action shows the policy applied, and p=none means the domain owner asked for no enforcement, which is why a failing message can still reach the inbox.
- **phishing-analysis** (phishing-r3): Clearing benign reports. Most reports are not phishing, and the room gives little on closing them or on the organisation's own simulations.
  - Draft: Before starting containment, check for the common benign causes: a phishing simulation run by your own awareness team, a known marketing sender, or a real vendor notice from a new platform. Simulations are usually listed with your email security team or in the mail platform's simulation settings, and they must not be purged or blocked by hand. Close benign reports with the reason, and thank the reporter so people keep reporting.
- **playbook-execution-and-escalation** (pbk-r1): What a password reset leaves behind. The room asks the learner to defend collection on this basis, yet no reading says what persists or where to find it.
  - Draft: A password reset does not remove what an intruder already set up. An inbox rule that forwards or hides mail and an OAuth app the user consented to both keep working after the reset, and refresh tokens can keep working until they are revoked. That is why Step 2 collects these items and why containment revokes sessions as well as changing the password. In Microsoft 365 audit logs, look for operations such as New-InboxRule, Set-InboxRule and Consent to application.
- **playbook-execution-and-escalation** (pbk-r1): Benign causes of impossible travel. Most of these tickets are not this incident, and an analyst must be able to close the common benign case correctly at Step 1.
  - Draft: Not every impossible-travel alert is an intruder. Corporate VPN egress, mobile carrier gateways and cloud proxies can all place one user in two locations. Compare the IP and ASN with known company egress, then check whether the device is managed and whether MFA was satisfied. A match to known egress on a managed device supports closing as benign, with that evidence recorded in the ticket.
- **playbook-execution-and-escalation** (pbk-r3): When to page and what to do if no one answers. The room's decision turns on paging at 03:52, but no reading gives a rule for urgency or for an unanswered page.
  - Draft: Escalation needs a clock as well as a route. Live unauthorised access, more than one affected account or a privileged account justifies paging the on-call lead now rather than waiting for the next shift. If the lead does not answer within the time your process sets, use the next name in the escalation path and record each attempt in the ticket. Do not start containment on your own unless the playbook gives you that authority.
- **powershell-for-soc-analyst** (ps-soc-r3): PowerShell's own logs. The room is the defender's view of PowerShell yet never mentions the log that shows what a script actually ran.
  - Draft: PowerShell keeps its own log, Microsoft-Windows-PowerShell/Operational. Event 4104 (script block logging) records the code that ran, after decoding, and 4103 records module activity. Turn script block logging on by policy; without it, PowerShell 5 and later still logs blocks it considers suspicious. Query it like any log: Get-WinEvent -FilterHashtable @{LogName='Microsoft-Windows-PowerShell/Operational'; Id=4104}.
- **powershell-for-soc-analyst** (ps-soc-r3): Parent process and command line. In Windows PowerShell 5.1 Get-Process shows neither of the two fields triage depends on, so the room's process cmdlet cannot show lineage.
  - Draft: Get-CimInstance Win32_Process | Select-Object ProcessId, ParentProcessId, Name, CommandLine returns both. Look up a parent with Get-Process -Id, remembering that a parent that has exited may leave an ID that no longer exists or has been reused. Run elevated, because reading other users' command lines needs it.
- **powershell-for-soc-analyst** (ps-soc-r3): Querying remote hosts. Analysts rarely sit at the suspect machine, and the room only shows local queries.
  - Draft: Invoke-Command -ComputerName WKS-01 -ScriptBlock { Get-NetTCPConnection -State Established } runs a query on a remote host over WinRM and returns objects to your session, with PSComputerName naming the source. WinRM must be enabled and allowed by policy; it is on by default on Windows Server but not on client Windows. Remote sessions use a network logon, so they generally avoid leaving reusable credentials on the suspect host, unlike an interactive RDP session. Your own query also appears in that host's logs, so note the time in the ticket.
- **powershell-for-soc-analyst** (ps-soc-r3): Reading what a scheduled task runs. Author is free text an attacker can set, so a filter on it alone misses a task that claims to be from Microsoft.
  - Draft: The Author field is plain text set by whoever registers the task, and some built-in tasks show an empty or resource-string author, so treat that filter as a rough first pass. Read what the task runs: Get-ScheduledTask | Select-Object TaskPath, TaskName, @{n='Command';e={$_.Actions.Execute}}, @{n='Args';e={$_.Actions.Arguments}}. Use Get-ScheduledTaskInfo for LastRunTime to learn whether it already fired.
- **powershell-for-soc-analyst** (ps-soc-r2): Making an export defensible. The room promises evidence, but a bare CSV cannot be shown to be unaltered or tied to a host and time.
  - Draft: An export counts as evidence only if someone can later show what it is. Record the host, the UTC time, your account and the exact command in the ticket, then run Get-FileHash on the CSV and note the result. Save it to a case share and not to the suspect host's disk, where it could overwrite deleted data. Live commands change a host slightly, so log what you ran.
- **privileged-access-monitoring** (priv-r2): Benign LSASS access. The reading says only Windows processes touch LSASS, so a trainee will raise false alarms on every security product.
  - Draft: LSASS is also opened by antivirus, EDR agents and Windows Error Reporting, so a Sysmon Event 10 hit is not proof on its own. Build an allowlist of expected SourceImage paths and signers for your estate, then review what remains, weighting unusual tools and wide access masks. LSA protection (RunAsPPL) and Credential Guard make dumping harder, so check whether they are on, and treat attempts to turn them off as suspicious.
- **privileged-access-monitoring** (priv-r2): Detecting PAM bypass. The reading says to watch for access outside the vault but never says how, and that is the main value of PAM logs.
  - Draft: To catch use of a vaulted account outside the vault, compare its Windows logons (4624) with vault checkout records. A logon with no matching checkout, or from a host that is not the PAM session gateway, means someone holds the password or a hash outside the control. Also alert on repeated checkouts of many accounts by one user, reasons that are blank or copied, and checkouts of accounts unrelated to that person's job.
- **privileged-access-monitoring** (priv-r1): Tier 0 logon boundaries. Admin logons on workstations are what leave Domain Admin hashes in LSASS, and a logon there is a simple high-value detection.
  - Draft: Tier 0 covers Domain Controllers and the accounts that control them. Tier 0 accounts should log on only to Tier 0 systems or dedicated privileged access workstations, because any other logon leaves credentials in that machine's memory. So a 4624 or 4672 for a Domain Admin on an ordinary workstation or server is worth an alert even with no attack visible. The Protected Users group strengthens this by blocking NTLM and credential caching for its members.
- **privileged-access-monitoring** (priv-r3): Containing domain-level compromise. The room stops at detection, and resetting one account does not evict an attacker who has DCSync output.
  - Draft: If DCSync or NTDS theft is confirmed, every domain password hash must be treated as stolen. Disable the attacker's accounts, remove unexpected group members and rotate privileged and service credentials. Reset the krbtgt password twice, allowing replication to finish between resets, so forged golden tickets stop working. Do this as a planned incident response action, because a rushed double reset can break authentication.
- **privileged-access-monitoring** (priv-r2): Cloud privileged roles. Most organisations also hold privileged identities in Entra ID, and a trainee here would not know where those changes appear. Confirm exact activity names against current Entra audit logs before publishing.
  - Draft: Privilege also lives in the cloud. In Entra ID, Global Administrator and similar roles are changed through role assignments recorded in the audit log, and Privileged Identity Management provides just-in-time activation with a reason and a time limit. Apply the same test as on-premises: a direct permanent role assignment, or an activation with no ticket, needs an owner who can explain it.
- **protocols-masterclass** (proto-r10): Benign look-alikes for each protocol abuse. An analyst who knows only the attack signature will escalate normal traffic; security agents, CDNs and update checkers all produce long DNS labels or regular beacons.
  - Draft: Long, odd-looking DNS labels are not always tunneling: antivirus and email-security products often encode a file hash or IP into a DNS lookup to their vendor's reputation service, and CDNs use machine-generated hostnames. Regular outbound check-ins also come from update agents, chat clients and EDR sensors. Before calling either malicious, identify the process making the traffic, check the parent domain's owner and age, and ask whether many hosts in the fleet show the same pattern, because managed software usually does and a single implant usually does not.
- **protocols-masterclass** (proto-r3): Reading real network logs. The room shows one Sysmon event; a working analyst reads firewall, proxy and Zeek records daily, and connection states are how half-open scans actually appear.
  - Draft: In Zeek, every connection gets a line in conn.log with a conn_state value: S0 means a SYN was seen with no reply, REJ means the attempt was refused, and SF means a normal start and finish. Many S0 or REJ lines from one source to many ports or hosts is a scan seen in logs. Zeek's dns.log gives query, qtype_name and rcode_name for each lookup, and http.log gives host, uri, user_agent and status_code, so the patterns in this room can be searched field by field.
- **protocols-masterclass** (proto-r7): What TLS 1.3 hides from the network. The room teaches certificate checks and JA3 from the TLS 1.2 flow; on TLS 1.3 traffic those checks fail without a decrypting proxy, so an analyst will wrongly expect the data.
  - Draft: In TLS 1.3 the server's certificate travels after the encryption starts, so a passive sensor cannot read its issuer or dates: self-signed checks need a decrypting proxy or the endpoint. SNI stays visible unless Encrypted Client Hello is in use. Browsers now shuffle TLS extension order, which makes a browser's JA3 value change between connections, so JA3 is best used to match fixed malware profiles, and the newer JA4 fingerprint sorts extensions to stay stable.
- **protocols-masterclass** (proto-r5): Finding DoH use. The room says DNS over HTTPS blinds DNS logging but gives no way to detect it, leaving a named blind spot with no action.
  - Draft: Because DoH looks like ordinary port 443 traffic, spot it by destination: alert on connections to well-known public DoH resolvers from hosts that should only use the corporate resolver. Block those destinations at the proxy and disable DoH through browser policy. A process other than a browser talking to a DoH resolver deserves a closer look, since DoH-based C2 tools work this way.
- **ransomware-full-lifecycle** (rw-r5): Fleet-wide push via Group Policy and RMM tools. rw-r4 promises these are covered in Lateral Movement but rw-r5 never does, and an analyst who watches only PsExec, WMI and RDP misses the two most scalable deployment routes.
  - Draft: Affiliates with domain admin rights often skip host-by-host movement and push the encryptor through Group Policy, for example an immediate scheduled task defined in a GPO, which appears as a new ScheduledTasks.xml under SYSVOL and, with directory-change auditing on, as Event 5136 on a Domain Controller. Others install or abuse a remote management tool such as AnyDesk or ScreenConnect, which is signed and looks like normal IT software. Treat a GPO edit outside a change window, or an RMM agent your organization does not use, as a fleet-level emergency.
- **ransomware-full-lifecycle** (rw-r8): Hypervisor encryption (ESXi). Many current families encrypt VMware ESXi hosts directly, where no EDR agent runs, so an analyst trained only on Windows telemetry would not see the largest single blast radius.
  - Draft: Many ransomware families include a Linux build aimed at VMware ESXi, because encrypting one hypervisor's datastore takes down every virtual machine on it at once. ESXi normally runs no EDR agent, so detection depends on forwarded vCenter and ESXi logs. Watch for SSH being enabled on a host, new local accounts, and commands such as esxcli vm process kill powering off guests just before files with a new extension appear on the datastore.
- **ransomware-full-lifecycle** (rw-r6): Targeting the backup platform. The room covers shadow copies but not the attack on real backup systems, which decides whether recovery is possible at all.
  - Draft: Shadow copies are only the local safety net; affiliates also go after the backup platform itself. They look for the backup console during discovery, log in with stolen admin credentials, and delete repositories or restore points before Impact. Logins to the backup console from an unexpected host, and deletion of jobs or repositories outside a change window, deserve the same urgency as vssadmin delete shadows.
- **ransomware-full-lifecycle** (rw-r6): Bring Your Own Vulnerable Driver. This is the common way affiliates defeat tamper-protected EDR, and rw-r6 implies tamper protection mostly holds.
  - Draft: When tamper protection blocks a normal stop, affiliates load a legitimately signed but vulnerable kernel driver and use it to kill the EDR process from kernel level, a technique called Bring Your Own Vulnerable Driver. Look for a new kernel-driver service in Event 7045 and for Sysmon Event 6 (driver loaded) naming a driver from a temp or user folder, then a host whose EDR sensor stops reporting. A sensor going silent on a server during an active intrusion is itself an alert.
- **remote-email-collection** (remc-r4): BEC response sequence. Responding in the wrong order lets a live attacker recreate rules or keep reading mail, and the room grades this without teaching it.
  - Draft: Cut access first. Block sign-in, then reset the password and revoke all sessions back-to-back, and force MFA re-registration. A password reset alone can leave an issued token working until it expires. Next, export and remove malicious rules and any mailbox-level forwarding, scope what the session read or sent, warn exposed finance contacts and vendors, and document the timeline.
- **remote-email-collection** (remc-r3): Rules that do not log as New-InboxRule. A hunt limited to New-InboxRule misses rules created from an Outlook client; confirm the current operation names against Microsoft's audit documentation.
  - Draft: New-InboxRule is logged for rules created through Exchange PowerShell and often Outlook on the web. A rule created in the Outlook desktop client can instead appear as UpdateInboxRules. Hunt on New-InboxRule, Set-InboxRule and UpdateInboxRules together, and also list the mailbox's current rules with Get-InboxRule. A hunt on one operation name alone leaves a blind spot.
- **remote-email-collection** (remc-r5): Malicious OAuth apps. An app granted mail access keeps reading after password resets and session revocation, and this is a common form of remote collection.
  - Draft: An attacker can trick a user into granting an app access such as Mail.Read, and the app then reads mail through Microsoft Graph with its own tokens. Look for 'Consent to application' in the Entra ID audit log, and check which app made the access in the MailItemsAccessed record. Compare the app with your approved list, especially if it has few users or an unknown publisher. Containment means removing the consent and the app's service principal, because a password reset does not stop it.
