- **remote-email-collection** (remc-r2): MailItemsAccessed limits. Treating this log as complete can produce a false 'nothing was read'; licensing and throttling behaviour change, so confirm against current Microsoft documentation.
  - Draft: MailItemsAccessed is not a perfect record. Bind operations are aggregated in short time windows, so one record can list several messages and you must count the items inside it. Microsoft documents throttling when a mailbox generates very many records, and during throttling some access goes unlogged. When you scope a compromise and find no records, say that the log shows no evidence of access, not that no mail was read.
- **remote-email-collection** (remc-r4): Scoping what the attacker sent and whether mail left. BEC harm comes from fraudulent messages and forwarded data, and read events alone do not show either.
  - Draft: Reading is only half of the scope. Search the same session for Send, SendAs, SendOnBehalf, SoftDelete and HardDelete to see what was sent or hidden. Use Message Trace to confirm whether forwarded mail reached the external address or bounced. Any fraudulent payment instruction you find must go to finance at once, by phone or another channel outside the compromised mailbox.
- **reporting-documentation** (rep-read-1): How severity is assigned. The ticket lists P1 to P4 but nothing says how to choose one, and that choice drives SLAs and who gets woken up.
  - Draft: Severity comes from two questions: how much damage is this causing or could it cause (impact), and how fast is it getting worse (urgency). A confirmed attack spreading across many systems or critical servers is usually P1, while a single contained low-value host is often P3. Use your SOC's own matrix rather than your instinct, and record why you chose the level. Update the severity as the facts change, and note the change in the timeline.
- **reporting-documentation** (rep-read-1): Separating facts from assumptions. Tickets can end up as legal evidence, and a guess written as fact misleads the next analyst and damages the record.
  - Draft: Write only what the evidence shows, and label anything else. 'The host connected to 203.0.113.5 at 09:14 UTC' is a fact; 'this is probably the attacker's server' is a hypothesis and should be marked that way, with what would confirm it. Use UTC timestamps throughout so that logs from different systems line up. Correct earlier notes by adding a new dated entry instead of rewriting them.
- **reporting-documentation** (rep-read-1): Closure codes. Metrics such as the false positive rate come straight from closure labels, so inconsistent labels make every KPI wrong.
  - Draft: Resolution needs a closure code, not only free text. True positive means a real attack, false positive means the rule misfired, and benign true positive means the rule was right but the activity was authorized. Cite the evidence for the code in the ticket. Metrics such as the false positive rate are calculated from these labels, so a wrong code quietly distorts the report and the tuning decisions based on it.
- **reporting-documentation** (rep-read-3): Sharing reports safely. Reports carry sensitive indicators and personal data, and an analyst needs to know who may receive them and in what form.
  - Draft: Mark a report with who may see it. The Traffic Light Protocol (TLP) uses CLEAR, GREEN, AMBER, AMBER+STRICT and RED, from public down to named recipients only. Write indicators in a safe form such as hxxp://bad[.]example so nobody clicks them by accident. Include personal data only where the report needs it, and keep usernames out of summaries sent to wide audiences.
- **reporting-documentation** (rep-read-4): Reading metrics honestly. Managers act on these numbers, and a mean across all severities can hide a serious problem.
  - Draft: A mean can be pulled far off by one long incident, so also report the median, and split every metric by severity so that fast P4 closures do not hide a slow P1. Watch for numbers improving for the wrong reason: rushing closures lowers MTTR but may raise the number of missed attacks or reopened tickets. Read speed metrics alongside a quality measure, such as how many tickets were reopened or failed review.
- **risk-fundamentals** (riskfund-r1): Risk treatment options. Rating a risk is only half the job; an analyst must know what can be done with it and who decides.
  - Draft: Once a risk is rated, the organisation chooses a response: mitigate it (patch or add a control), transfer it (insurance or contract), avoid it (stop the activity or retire the system), or accept it. Acceptance is a recorded decision by the risk owner with a review date, not something an analyst decides alone. If a patch is not possible, a compensating control such as a WAF rule or segmentation lowers the risk, and what remains is the residual risk.
- **risk-fundamentals** (riskfund-r1): Exposure as part of likelihood. Internet exposure decides the graded tasks, yet the model names no place for it.
  - Draft: Exposure changes likelihood. The same flaw on an internet-facing system can be reached by anyone scanning the internet, while on a segmented internal server an attacker must first be inside. Ask what exploitation requires: remote or local access, a login or none, a user action or none. Exposure is not one of the three words in the shorthand, so count it under likelihood when you rate a finding.
- **risk-fundamentals** (riskfund-r1): EPSS and SSVC beside CVSS and KEV. Vulnerability tools show these beside CVSS, and most flaws are not in KEV; EPSS model versions change, so check FIRST for the current one.
  - Draft: CVSS measures severity, and KEV confirms exploitation has happened. EPSS, published by FIRST, estimates the probability that a CVE will be exploited in the next 30 days as a score from 0 to 1, which helps with the many flaws not in KEV. SSVC, from Carnegie Mellon and adopted by CISA in its own form, replaces a score with a decision tree ending in an action such as Track or Act. None of these knows the impact on your asset, so use them alongside it.
- **rmm-abuse-remote-access-tools** (rmm-r6): Attacker-owned tenant of the sanctioned product. An allowlist by product alone passes an attacker running the same vendor's tool on their own account, a commonly reported phishing outcome. Check installer argument formats against current vendor versions.
  - Draft: An allowlist must pin your tenant, not only the product, because attackers sign up for their own trial of the same tool. Compare the instance the client connects to with your own: a ScreenConnect client's command line and relay host name the instance it belongs to, and an Atera agent's install arguments carry the account email it enrolls under. The sanctioned product talking to a stranger's instance is an unsanctioned session.
- **rmm-abuse-remote-access-tools** (rmm-r7): Endpoint evidence of a session. An analyst needs to prove who connected and when; the room stops at process and proxy records. Verify log paths for the versions in use.
  - Draft: RMM tools keep their own records on the host. AnyDesk writes %ProgramData%\AnyDesk\connection_trace.txt and ad.trace files, TeamViewer writes Connections_incoming.txt in its install folder, and ScreenConnect logs to the Windows Application log. An installed client also creates a service, recorded as Event ID 7045 in the System log. Collect these early, because they show the remote ID and session times that network logs cannot.
- **rmm-abuse-remote-access-tools** (rmm-r5): Detecting Quick Assist. The install-path and service signals do not fire for a built-in tool, so the room's own case study falls outside its detection model. Verify the domain against Microsoft's current documentation.
  - Draft: Quick Assist breaks the install signals, because it ships with Windows and needs no service or download. Look instead for QuickAssist.exe starting on a host where IT does not use it, and for connections to remoteassistance.support.services.microsoft.com, especially shortly after an inbox flood or an unexpected call. If the organization does not use it, block or remove it and alert on any launch.
- **rmm-abuse-remote-access-tools** (rmm-r4): Detecting console exploitation. The CVE-2024-1709 case study explains the flaw but not what its exploitation looks like on the server an analyst has to check.
  - Draft: On a self-hosted ScreenConnect server, exploitation of CVE-2024-1709 shows as requests to SetupWizard.aspx with extra path text after it, on a server that was already configured. Check the web logs for these, look for new accounts in the User.xml file under App_Data, and review child processes of the ScreenConnect service. Treat any hit as a compromise of every managed endpoint, not of one server.
- **security-products-behaviour** (secprod-r2): Modern AV is not file-only. An analyst who believes AV never sees scripts or memory will misread Defender detections on fileless activity.
  - Draft: Classic antivirus scans files, but current products such as Microsoft Defender Antivirus also use cloud lookups, behavior monitoring and the Antimalware Scan Interface (AMSI), which lets the engine inspect PowerShell and other scripts after they are decoded in memory. So a fileless script can still raise an AV detection, and an AMSI-sourced alert may have no file on disk to quarantine. AV still has less depth than EDR: it gives a verdict on one item, not the full process history around it.
- **security-products-behaviour** (secprod-r7): NDR and DLP log lines. The room promises a real log line per product, and an analyst who has never seen these records cannot say which product raised an alert.
  - Draft: An NDR or Zeek connection record shows metadata only: source and destination, destination port, duration, bytes each way and connection state, with no content. Beaconing appears as many rows to one destination at near-equal intervals and sizes. A DLP record looks different: Microsoft Purview writes a DlpRuleMatch audit event naming the policy, the rule, the sensitive information matched, the location and the action taken, so you can read whether it blocked or only notified.
- **security-products-behaviour** (secprod-r6): Secure web gateway log line. Proxy logs are a main source in phishing and C2 triage, and the reading shows an email record but no web record.
  - Draft: A web proxy record usually holds the time, user, source host, the full URL when TLS is inspected or only the hostname when it is not, URL category, action, HTTP status, bytes and user agent. Read the action first to learn whether the user reached the site, then the category, which is a vendor's opinion rather than proof. No record for a host you know visited the site means that traffic bypassed the proxy.
- **security-products-behaviour** (secprod-r3): What TLS still exposes. Most traffic is encrypted, so analysts need to know what stays readable without decryption and that this is shrinking.
  - Draft: Even without TLS inspection the handshake usually shows the requested hostname in the SNI field, and fingerprints such as JA3 or JA4 describe the client software. TLS 1.3 encrypts the server certificate, and Encrypted Client Hello can hide the SNI where both sides support it. QUIC traffic on UDP 443 is a separate flow, and many organizations block it so browsers fall back to TCP, where inspection works.
- **sentinel-fundamentals** (sentinel-r3): Working and closing an incident. The room's subject includes incident management but it stops at listing incident fields, so an analyst cannot take an incident to closure. Microsoft has been moving Sentinel into the Defender portal, so screens and exact labels may differ; I am not certain of the current cut-over date.
  - Draft: To work an incident, assign yourself as owner, set the status to Active, and use the entities and timeline to see which users, hosts and IPs are involved, adding findings as comments. Close it with a classification: True Positive (suspicious activity), Benign Positive (suspicious but expected), False Positive (incorrect alert logic or incorrect data), or Undetermined. These classifications drive tuning and reporting, so a repeating Benign Positive should lead to a narrow exception rather than a weaker rule.
- **sentinel-fundamentals** (sentinel-r1): Reading SigninLogs. SigninLogs is the room's key cloud table, but only SecurityEvent fields are taught, so an analyst cannot triage a cloud sign-in.
  - Draft: The columns that matter in SigninLogs are UserPrincipalName, IPAddress, Location, AppDisplayName, ConditionalAccessStatus and ResultType. ResultType is a string: 0 means success, 50126 a wrong password, 50053 a locked account, and 50074 means strong authentication was required. Sign-ins made by apps on a user's behalf sit in a separate table, AADNonInteractiveUserSignInLogs, so include it when scoping a compromised account.
- **sentinel-fundamentals** (sentinel-r2): Time bucketing and threshold logic in KQL. The room's rule examples use 'more than 20 in 5 minutes', but no reading teaches the operator that expresses a time window, so a learner cannot write them.
  - Draft: A plain summarize counts across the whole query window. To count per time slice, add bin(): summarize FailureCount = count() by IpAddress, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m) gives one row per IP per 5 minutes, and a following where FailureCount > 20 keeps only the bursts. This is the normal shape of a brute-force or password-spray rule. Keep the lookback longer than the bucket so a burst that straddles two runs is still seen.
- **sentinel-fundamentals** (sentinel-r3): Automation rules and watchlists. An analyst needs the safe way to handle known-good sources such as scanners, and the room's only example hardcodes IPs in a query.
  - Draft: A watchlist is a table you maintain, read in KQL with _GetWatchlist('alias'), and it holds items such as approved scanners or VIP accounts, so exclusions live in one owned place rather than inside each rule. Automation rules run when an incident is created or updated: they can assign an owner, change severity, close known-benign patterns for a set time, or trigger a playbook. Give every exception an owner and an expiry date.
- **sentinel-fundamentals** (sentinel-r1): Current data connector methods. sentinel-r1 presents the HTTP Data Collector API as current, while Microsoft has replaced it and announced its retirement; I recall September 2026 but the date should be confirmed.
  - Draft: Current connectors for Windows, Syslog and CEF use the Azure Monitor Agent with a data collection rule, which defines what is collected and can filter it before ingestion. Custom applications send logs through the Logs Ingestion API, the replacement for the older HTTP Data Collector API. When a table goes quiet, check the connector and the data collection rule, and use the Heartbeat table to see whether the agent is still reporting.
- **sentinelone** (s1-r2): Triage and closing a threat. The room shows what the console displays but never how to reach and record a verdict, which is the analyst's daily job.
  - Draft: Each threat needs an analyst verdict and an incident status. Check the process path, signer, command line and Storyline: a Suspicious item from a signed internal tool with an expected parent is often benign, while a Malicious item needs scoping. Mark the threat as true positive, false positive or suspicious, and record what you did. If it is a false positive, add a narrow exclusion by hash or path, never a whole folder, because a broad exclusion is a blind spot attackers can use.
- **sentinelone** (s1-r2): Reading the Storyline tree. Storyline is taught as a concept, but the room never walks through finding root cause and scope in one.
  - Draft: Open the threat's Storyline and start at the root of the process tree to find how the attack began, such as an Office file, a browser download or a remote logon. Walk down each branch and note child processes, files written, registry changes and network connections, and collect the indicators. Then search those indicators across the fleet, because one Storyline covers one chain on one endpoint. The source process Storyline ID also lets you pull all related events in a telemetry search.
- **sentinelone** (s1-r2): Deep Visibility queries and retention. The room gives plain-English examples only, so a trainee could not write a search. The stated 365-day retention looks license-dependent and should be confirmed against current SentinelOne documentation, as should query syntax, which differs between console versions.
  - Draft: Telemetry searches filter on named fields, such as src.process.name, src.process.cmdline, tgt.file.path and dst.ip.address, for example to find powershell.exe with an encoded command line. Narrow the time range first, then widen it, and pivot on the Storyline ID when you find a hit. How far back you can search depends on your license and data retention, so check it before reporting that something never ran.
- **sentinelone** (s1-r2): Limits of Rollback. Analysts who promise recovery from Rollback alone will mislead the business. Confirm platform support against current SentinelOne documentation.
  - Draft: Rollback is not a backup. It depends on Windows Volume Shadow Copy snapshots, so treat it as a Windows capability, and it can only restore what those snapshots hold. Confirm it worked by checking the restored files, and keep tested backups as the real recovery plan. Rollback also does not remove stolen data, so a ransomware case still needs an exfiltration check.
- **sharepoint-teams-monitoring** (spt-teams-r3): Proving data left after a bulk download. A SharePoint download only shows files reaching a device, so an analyst cannot call it exfiltration or scope the loss without endpoint and mail evidence.
  - Draft: After a bulk download, identify the device from the ClientIP and check what happened to the files next. Look for copies to removable media and uploads to personal cloud storage in EDR or Purview Endpoint DLP telemetry, and for large outbound mail in a message trace. Until one of these shows the files leaving, report the activity as collection, not confirmed exfiltration.
- **sharepoint-teams-monitoring** (spt-teams-r3): Containing sharing and guest abuse. The room detects abuse but never says how to stop it, so a trained analyst would not know how to close the channel without losing evidence.
  - Draft: Record the link, its target file and its audit entries first, then delete the Anyone link or specific-people link so the URL stops working. For a malicious guest, remove the account from the team or site, block its sign-in and revoke its sessions in Entra ID. For an insider case, agree the steps with HR and Legal and place the user's content on an eDiscovery hold before anything is changed.
- **sharepoint-teams-monitoring** (spt-teams-r2): Investigating a Teams phishing chat. The room names Teams phishing as a main vector but gives no steps for investigating one; confirm the ParticipantInfo field names against the current Microsoft audit schema before publishing.
  - Draft: Search the Unified Audit Log for ChatCreated and MessageSent records involving the targeted user, and check the ParticipantInfo details for foreign-tenant participants and their tenant IDs. Find every employee the same external tenant contacted, then check those users for sign-ins, app installs or remote-support tool launches after the chat. To close the path, block that tenant and limit External Access to an allowlist of trusted domains.
- **sharepoint-teams-monitoring** (spt-teams-r2): Detecting guest additions. Guest accounts are a named threat, yet the room only shows what they look like after they download files, not how to catch the invitation.
  - Draft: A new guest leaves two traces: MemberAdded in the Teams audit records, with the member's role shown as Guest, and an external-user invitation in the Entra ID audit log. Ask who invited the guest, whether the team holds sensitive files, and whether a business owner approved it. A guest added by a recently compromised account, or to a Finance or Legal team, deserves immediate review.
- **sharepoint-teams-monitoring** (spt-teams-r3): Audit log retention and delay. An analyst must know how far back the log reaches before stating that nothing happened; retention depends on licensing, so confirm the tenant's plan.
  - Draft: Audit (Standard) keeps Unified Audit Log records for 180 days, and Audit (Premium) keeps Exchange, SharePoint, OneDrive and Entra ID records for one year by default. Records can also appear some time after the activity, so a very recent event may not be searchable yet. Check both limits before you write that a search found nothing, and export the results you rely on.
- **siem-fundamentals** (siem-fund-r3): A triage workflow. The room promises how analysts triage detections but never gives the steps, so a trainee can classify an outcome yet has no method for reaching it.
  - Draft: Work every alert the same way. First read the rule and open the matched raw events to confirm what actually happened. Next enrich the entities: who is the user, how critical is the host, what is known about the source. Then scope it by searching for related activity before and after, including any success that followed a failure. Finally record a verdict with your evidence, and escalate anything you cannot rule out, noting what you checked so the next analyst does not repeat it.
- **siem-fundamentals** (siem-fund-r4): A correlation rule as a real query. The room describes rule types in words and teaches ad hoc search, but never shows the two joined, so a trainee cannot read the logic behind an alert.
  - Draft: A scheduled detection rule is usually a search that runs on a timer and alerts when it returns rows. This KQL finds a spray shape: SecurityEvent | where EventID == 4625 | where TimeGenerated > ago(1h) | summarize Fails = count(), Accounts = dcount(TargetAccount) by IpAddress | where Accounts > 10. The dcount counts distinct accounts, which catches many accounts tried from one source even when each account sees few failures. In Sentinel this would run as an analytics rule with a schedule and a lookback window.
- **siem-fundamentals** (siem-fund-r3): Silent failure of log sources. A SIEM cannot alert on logs it never received, so an analyst who reads no alerts as no attacks misses the most common cause of false negatives.
  - Draft: A quiet SIEM does not prove a quiet network. If an agent stops sending, a parser breaks after an update, or a field is renamed, rules can stop matching with no error shown. Mature SOCs watch log source health: an alert when a source goes silent or its volume drops sharply, and a periodic test that fires a harmless known event to prove the rule still works.
- **sigma-yara-rule-authoring** (sigyara-r4): Testing a Sigma rule before deployment. The room teaches corpus testing only for YARA, so a rule author has no method for proving a Sigma rule fires, or that its field mapping is right, before it reaches production.
  - Draft: A compiled query is untested until it has run against real data. Trigger the technique in a lab, for example with the matching Atomic Red Team test, and confirm the rule fires, then run the same query across about 30 days of production logs to count how many legitimate matches it would have raised. A query that returns nothing even in the lab usually means a field-mapping problem, since a wrong column name fails silently rather than with an error.
- **sigma-yara-rule-authoring** (sigyara-r2): Threshold detections with Sigma correlation rules. Brute force, password spray and beaconing cannot be expressed as a single-event selection, and the room gives no way to write a count-over-time rule.
  - Draft: A selection judges one event at a time, so it cannot express 'more than 20 failures in 5 minutes'. Sigma's correlation rules reference a base rule and add a type such as event_count or value_count, a group-by field, a timespan and a condition such as gte: 20. Backend support varies, so confirm that your pySigma backend converts them before relying on one.
- **sigma-yara-rule-authoring** (sigyara-r2): Rules that survive renaming and flag variants. Image|endswith, the room's main anchor, is defeated by copying a binary under a new name, and an analyst needs the counter.
  - Draft: Image|endswith trusts the file name, so an attacker who copies vssadmin.exe to a new name walks past it. Sysmon's OriginalFileName field comes from the binary's own version resource and survives a rename, so a selection can test Image or OriginalFileName together. The windash modifier also matches a flag written with a dash, a slash or similar dash characters, so -enc and /enc are both caught.
- **sigma-yara-rule-authoring** (sigyara-r5): The standard YARA file-type check. Published YARA rules almost all use integer-read checks, so an analyst who knows only $mz at 0 cannot read or tune them.
  - Draft: Most real YARA rules test the file type with uint16(0) == 0x5A4D rather than a string at offset 0. uint16 reads two bytes at the given offset, little-endian, so 0x5A4D is the bytes MZ. It needs no strings entry and is evaluated cheaply, which makes it a good first clause alongside filesize.
- **soar-automation** (soar-r2): Interpreting enrichment results. Analysts act on enrichment constantly, and treating an empty result as clean is a classic triage error.
  - Draft: Enrichment gives evidence, not a verdict. A score of 0 engines means nobody has flagged the indicator yet, not that it is safe, which matters for new attacker infrastructure. A cloud provider address can be shared by unrelated customers, so its reputation may say little about today's traffic. Check how fresh the result is, and weigh internal context such as the account's normal sources above an outside score.
- **soar-automation** (soar-r2): When a playbook fails. A failed integration can silently leave an alert unenriched or uncontained, so an analyst must know to check the run.
  - Draft: Playbooks break: an API key expires, a tool hits a rate limit, or a lookup times out. Every run has a step-by-step log, so when an alert looks thin, open the run and confirm which steps finished. A good playbook routes a failed step to a human instead of closing the alert. Treat a blank enrichment field as not checked, never as a clean result.
- **soar-automation** (soar-r3): Reversing containment. Automated containment will sometimes be wrong, and the analyst has to undo it quickly and safely.
  - Draft: Plan the undo with the action. Give IP blocks an expiry where the tooling allows it, record what the playbook changed, and name who can release an isolated host or re-enable an account. Before releasing anything, confirm why the action fired was wrong and record it in the case. Feed that result back into the playbook so the same mistake does not repeat.
- **soar-automation** (soar-r4): Quality checks on auto-closed alerts. Auto-triage hides its own mistakes unless someone checks the alerts nobody looked at.
  - Draft: MTTR and hours saved show speed, not accuracy, so also check what the automation closed. Sample a set of auto-closed alerts every week, have an analyst review them, and track how many were wrongly closed. If that rate rises, tighten the closure condition. Allow-list entries behind auto-closure also need an owner and a review date, because a scanner address can change hands.
- **soc-structure** (soc-struct-r3): Raw log versus correlation versus enrichment. The log analysis calls this the most important thing to understand about a SIEM and promises a next reading that does not exist.
  - Draft: A single log records one event and knows nothing else. The SIEM adds two things: correlation, which counts or joins many events over a time window, and enrichment, which adds outside context such as an address's reputation or location. So a figure like '47 failures' or a label like 'known malicious IP' comes from the SIEM, not the log. Always check which is which, because a rule or feed can be wrong while the raw event is simply a fact.
- **soc-structure** (soc-struct-r2): Triage outcomes and severity. A T1 must choose an outcome and a priority on every alert, and the room offers only true or false positive.
  - Draft: Triage has more than two outcomes. A true positive is real malicious activity, a false positive is a rule that misfired, and a benign true positive is a real event that turns out to be authorised, such as an approved admin task. If you cannot decide within your time limit, escalate rather than guess. Severity sets the order of work: most SOCs set a response target for each level, so a critical alert is picked up within minutes.
- **soc-structure** (soc-struct-r2): Writing an escalation. A T2 acts on what the T1 wrote, and a weak escalation costs time during a live incident.
  - Draft: A good escalation lets the next analyst start without asking you anything. State what fired, the affected host and user, the times, the evidence you checked with real values, what you ruled out, why you are escalating and what you have already done. Keep facts apart from guesses, and put it all in the ticket so it stays on record.
- **soc-structure** (soc-struct-r1): MDR as a SOC model. Many organisations and entry-level jobs now use MDR, and the list of SOC models leaves it out.
  - Draft: MDR (Managed Detection and Response) is an outsourced service that goes further than classic monitoring: the provider supplies the detection technology and analysts who investigate and often take response actions on the customer's behalf, around the clock. An MSSP has traditionally focused on monitoring and managing security tools and passing alerts on to the customer. In practice the labels overlap, so check what the contract says the provider will actually do.
- **tcpip-deep-dive** (tcpip-r3): Responding to a confirmed scan. A working analyst must decide whether a scan needs action, and the room stops at naming the technique.
  - Draft: Internet-facing hosts receive background scans all day, so a scan from outside that gets no further than the edge is usually logged and closed unless a completed session or exploit attempt follows it. An internal scan is different: first check the source against the approved scanner list and schedule, and if it is not listed, treat the host as possibly compromised and investigate the process behind the traffic. An approved vulnerability scanner is closed as authorised activity and handled with a narrow, owned exclusion for that source, never a broad rule change.
- **tcpip-deep-dive** (tcpip-r3): SYN flood versus scan. Both fill flow logs with S0 records, and telling them apart decides whether this is reconnaissance or an availability attack.
  - Draft: A SYN flood aims at availability, not discovery: many sources, often spoofed, send SYNs to one port so half-open connections fill the server's backlog in SYN_RECEIVED. In conn.log this looks like many S0 records with many source addresses and one destination port, the mirror image of a scan's one source and many ports. SYN cookies let a server keep answering without holding state for each half-open connection.
- **tcpip-deep-dive** (tcpip-r3): UDP and ICMP scan signs. Flag logic only covers TCP, so an analyst trained here would miss scans on the other two protocols of the stack.
  - Draft: UDP has no handshake, so a UDP scan (nmap -sU) learns from replies differently: a closed port usually answers with ICMP port unreachable, while an open or filtered port often stays silent. A burst of ICMP port unreachable messages from one host toward one source is therefore a sign of a UDP scan. One-sided UDP records usually appear in Zeek as S0, and ICMP echo sweeps across many hosts show the same breadth pattern on a different protocol.
- **threat-hunting-fundamentals** (threat-hunt-r3): A worked hunt query. This is an advanced hunting room and it never shows one real query, so a trainee could not run the stacking it tests.
  - Draft: Stacking is one short query. In Defender XDR advanced hunting: DeviceProcessEvents | where Timestamp > ago(30d) | where FileName =~ "powershell.exe" | summarize Hosts = dcount(DeviceName) by InitiatingProcessFileName | sort by Hosts asc. Rare parents rise to the top, so check those hosts first. The same shape works in SPL with stats dc(host) by parent, and for any field you can count.
- **threat-hunting-fundamentals** (threat-hunt-r3): Filtering LSASS hunts. The listed signs would flood a real estate with security-product hits, and a literal search for MiniDump misses a common variant.
  - Draft: LSASS hunts need a baseline, because antivirus, EDR agents and Windows Error Reporting open LSASS routinely, so exclude known signed paths and review the rest. Do not rely on one string: attackers call the same comsvcs.dll export by number, as #24, so search for rundll32.exe with comsvcs.dll, and treat lsass.dmp or any large new .dmp file as a separate lead. Confirm the file was not produced by an approved support case before raising an incident.
- **threat-hunting-fundamentals** (threat-hunt-r2): No data versus no finding. A hunt that returns nothing is only worth reporting if the data was there, and that check is the first thing a senior would ask about.
  - Draft: Before running a hunt, confirm that the data exists and covers the hosts and dates you care about. Process command lines need Sysmon, EDR or 4688 with command-line logging, and PowerShell content needs Script Block Logging. Test that the query can match by running it against a known example. Report a coverage percentage with every negative result, because 'nothing found on 40% of hosts' is a different statement from 'nothing found'.
- **threat-hunting-fundamentals** (threat-hunt-r3): Measuring beacons and clearing benign ones. Regular traffic is common from legitimate software, and real beacons add jitter, so looking at timing by eye fails both ways.
  - Draft: To test for beaconing, group connections by source and destination, calculate the gaps between them and look for a low spread relative to the average, which still shows through jitter of 10 to 30 percent. Tools such as RITA score this for you. Updaters, telemetry agents and time sync also call home on a schedule, so check the process, the signer and the domain's history before treating a hit as C2.
- **threat-intelligence** (threat-intel-r3): Triaging an IOC hit. An IOC match is only a lead, and analysts need a repeatable check before they call it an incident or dismiss it.
  - Draft: An IOC hit tells you a match occurred, not that a compromise happened, so test it against your own logs. Check the direction of the traffic, whether it was allowed or blocked, how often it repeats, how much data moved, and which host and process were involved. Repeated allowed outbound sessions at a regular interval suggest beaconing, meaning malware checking in with its controller. A single blocked inbound connection from the same IP is usually background scanning.
- **threat-intelligence** (threat-intel-r3): Responding to a confirmed IOC hit. The room grades this decision but never teaches it, and a wrong first move can leave the attacker connected or tip them off.
  - Draft: Once your own logs confirm the hit, contain the active channel first and escalate to incident response. Blocking one IP is not the end, because attackers change addresses, so search for the same indicator across all hosts and look for what the actor did before the block. Eradication steps wait until scoping is done. On a domain controller, coordinate with the AD owner, because isolating it can break authentication for the domain.
- **threat-intelligence** (threat-intel-r3): IOC false positives and ageing. Feeds go stale and shared infrastructure causes constant noise, so an analyst must be able to tell a bad match from a real one.
  - Draft: An IP on a feed may be a shared host, a CDN edge, a Tor exit or a cloud address that has since been reassigned to someone innocent. Check the indicator's first-seen and last-seen dates, how many unrelated sites share the address, and whether the source is still confident. Give indicators an expiry, because an old IP match with no supporting activity usually means nothing. Report bad indicators back to the producer, which is the Feedback phase of the lifecycle.
- **threat-intelligence** (threat-intel-r3): Pyramid of Pain. This standard model explains which intelligence is worth hunting on, and the room states the idea without giving analysts the framework.
  - Draft: The Pyramid of Pain ranks indicators by how much it hurts an attacker when you detect them. Hashes and IP addresses sit at the bottom because they are trivial to change, followed by domains, then network and host artefacts, then tools, with TTPs at the top. A detection built on behaviour forces the attacker to change how they work, not just swap an address. Use cheap indicators for quick blocking and spend engineering time on the upper levels.
- **threat-intelligence** (threat-intel-r3): Rating source reliability. The room says confidence matters but gives no method, so analysts cannot explain why they trust one report over another.
  - Draft: Rate the source and the information separately. The Admiralty Code grades source reliability from A to F and information credibility from 1 to 6, so B2 means a usually reliable source with probably true information. A trusted source can still pass on an unconfirmed claim, which is why the two ratings stay apart. Record the rating with the indicator so that later analysts know how much weight to give it.
- **timestamps-and-timelines** (time-r2): Event time vs ingest time. Every SIEM holds more than one timestamp per event, and sorting on the wrong one reorders the timeline without any visible error.
  - Draft: A SIEM record usually carries two times: when the event happened on the source, and when the SIEM received it. Elastic has @timestamp and event.ingested, Splunk has _time and _indextime, and Microsoft Sentinel has TimeGenerated and ingestion_time(). Build the timeline from the event time. A large gap between the two shows delayed delivery, which explains an alert that fired long after the action.
- **timestamps-and-timelines** (time-r1): Practical conversion. The room teaches how to recognise formats but never how to convert one, and an analyst must do that many times a shift.
  - Draft: You do not convert by hand. In PowerShell, [DateTimeOffset]::FromUnixTimeSeconds(1781218034) reads epoch seconds, FromUnixTimeMilliseconds reads milliseconds, and [DateTime]::FromFileTimeUtc(value) reads FILETIME. In KQL, unixtime_seconds_todatetime() and unixtime_milliseconds_todatetime() do the same job. To reach local time, add the offset to UTC: 03:50 UTC plus 5:30 is 09:20 at UTC+5:30.
- **timestamps-and-timelines** (time-r5): Detecting timestomping. The room names timestomping but gives no way to spot it, so the analyst would simply trust the forged date.
  - Draft: Timestomping (T1070.006) changes the timestamps a file shows, but it often misses others. On NTFS the $STANDARD_INFORMATION times are easy to alter, while the $FILE_NAME times usually keep the real value, so a mismatch is a warning sign. Sysmon Event ID 2 records a process changing a file's creation time. Also compare the file date with outside evidence, such as when EDR first saw the file.
- **timestamps-and-timelines** (time-r4): What a timeline row holds. A timeline is handed to other people, and without a standard row it cannot be checked or defended.
  - Draft: Each row should hold the time in UTC, the source log, the host, the account, what happened, and a pointer to the raw evidence such as an event ID or record link. Mark any skew correction you applied and how sure you are of the entry. This lets a teammate re-check any row without repeating your search.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic** (tls-r4): From metadata case to response. The room stops at the point where an analyst has to act on a suspected C2 session.
  - Draft: Once several metadata signals agree, escalate and move to the host: EDR shows which process owns the sessions, its parent and its hash. Search the fleet for the same JA3 or JA4, destination and certificate to find other infected hosts. Then isolate the host and block the destination together, because a block alone leaves the implant free to fall back to another server.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic** (tls-r2): Valid certificates and infrastructure pivots. Mature C2 uses free publicly trusted certificates, so an analyst who only looks for self-signed ones misses it and needs other pivots.
  - Draft: Many operators now use free, publicly trusted certificates, so a valid chain proves only that someone controlled the domain when the certificate was issued. Publicly trusted certificates are recorded in Certificate Transparency logs, searchable on services such as crt.sh, which show when a domain first got a certificate and which names share it. Zeek x509 logs and internet scanners such as Censys or Shodan let you pivot on subject, serial or certificate hash to find related servers. A certificate first logged hours before first contact is a useful sign even when the chain validates.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic** (tls-r2): Domain fronting: SNI is only a claim. Analysts treat SNI as the true destination although the client writes it freely; several large CDNs have blocked fronting, but support varies by provider, so check current behaviour before relying on this.
  - Draft: SNI is whatever the client chooses to send, so it is a claim and not proof of destination. In domain fronting (T1090.004) the SNI names a reputable site on a CDN while the encrypted HTTP Host header names a different site on the same CDN, so the proxy logs the reputable name. Without TLS inspection the two values cannot be compared, which is one more reason to weigh timing, byte shape and process context rather than the name alone.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic** (tls-r1): QUIC and HTTP/3 blind spot. Browsers and some tools run TLS 1.3 inside QUIC on UDP 443, which TCP-only sensors and many inspection proxies do not parse.
  - Draft: HTTP/3 runs over QUIC, which carries a TLS 1.3 handshake inside UDP, usually on port 443. A sensor that decodes only TCP TLS will miss these sessions or show bare UDP flows, and many inspection proxies cannot decrypt them. Many organisations block outbound UDP 443 so browsers fall back to TCP, where inspection works. If yours does not, confirm that your sensors decode QUIC, for example with Zeek.
- **tls-encrypted-traffic** (tls-r4): ATT&CK mapping for encrypted C2. Case notes and detections are tagged by technique, and this room names none.
  - Draft: Encrypted C2 maps to T1573 Encrypted Channel under Command and Control (TA0011), with T1573.002 Asymmetric Cryptography covering TLS-style key exchange, and HTTPS beacons also map to T1071.001 Web Protocols. Certificates an attacker creates map to T1587.003 and certificates they obtain from a CA to T1588.004, both named Digital Certificates, under Resource Development (TA0042).
- **tunneling-c2-channels** (tunnel-r6): Containing a live tunnel. An analyst must act on a confirmed tunnel without destroying evidence, and the room ends at the word escalate.
  - Draft: Once a tunnel has no legitimate explanation, treat it as a hands-on intrusion and escalate at once. Isolate the host through EDR, which keeps memory and disk intact, block the destination at the firewall or proxy, and capture the command line, binary hash and parent process before anything is cleaned up. Search other hosts for the same destination, hash and command-line pattern, because a pivot host usually means the attacker is already further inside. Rotate any credential visible on the command line, such as a plink -pw value.
- **tunneling-c2-channels** (tunnel-r5): Native Windows pivots: ssh.exe and netsh portproxy. Attackers often need to drop nothing, so hunting only for plink, Chisel and ngrok misses tunnels built from what Windows provides.
  - Draft: Windows 10 and 11 include an OpenSSH client at C:\Windows\System32\OpenSSH\ssh.exe, so -L, -R and -D can run with no dropped binary at all. The command netsh interface portproxy add v4tov4 builds a port forward with no SSH involved, and its rules persist in the registry under HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\PortProxy\v4tov4\tcp. Hunt process-creation logs for ssh.exe with forwarding flags and netsh with portproxy, and check that registry key on suspect hosts. Both are common on admin systems, so ask the same host, account and ticket questions as for any dual-use tool.
- **tunneling-c2-channels** (tunnel-r6): ATT&CK mapping for tunnels and proxies. Case notes and detections are tagged by technique, and this room names none.
  - Draft: Under Command and Control (TA0011), tunneling one protocol inside another, such as forwarding through SSH, maps to T1572 Protocol Tunneling. SOCKS and relay pivots map to T1090 Proxy, with T1090.001 Internal Proxy and T1090.003 Multi-hop Proxy for chained hops. HTTPS beacons map to T1071.001 Web Protocols, DNS C2 to T1071.004 DNS, and raw ICMP C2 to T1095 Non-Application Layer Protocol. Tag the technique you have evidence for, not the tool name.
- **tunneling-c2-channels** (tunnel-r5): ngrok agent traffic versus ngrok endpoint traffic. The room treats a ngrok public-domain SNI as the fingerprint of a host running ngrok, but the agent normally connects to separate agent hosts, so an analyst can misread who ran what; I am not certain the hostnames below are current, so verify them against ngrok documentation.
  - Draft: Two different things reach ngrok. A host running the ngrok agent keeps an outbound connection to the agent service, at hostnames such as connect.ngrok-agent.com, and that host is the one exposing a service. A host connecting to a public URL such as x7f2a9.ngrok-free.app is a client of a tunnel, which is how a beacon can reach attacker infrastructure hidden behind ngrok. Ask the same host-role questions for both, but read the SNI and the owning process together, because they tell different stories.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice** (uc-r5): Revoking stolen role sessions. The room's only response is rotation, which leaves stolen temporary credentials working until they expire.
  - Draft: Temporary role credentials keep working until they expire, even after you rotate the secrets they read. In AWS, use Revoke active sessions on the role, which adds an inline deny policy (AWSRevokeOlderSessions) for sessions issued before that moment. Then rotate every secret the identity read, and search CloudTrail by the access key ID for everything else those credentials did.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice** (uc-r4): Windows and browser password stores. Most SOC estates are Windows, yet the room details macOS Keychain and gives Windows one line; confirm Chrome's current encryption behaviour for your version, as it has been changing.
  - Draft: On Windows, Credential Manager (T1555.004) keeps saved logons, which an attacker lists with cmdkey /list or vaultcmd /list. Windows logs event 5379 when Credential Manager credentials are read, but it is noisy, so judge it by process and timing. Chrome and Edge passwords (T1555.003) sit in the Login Data file, so a process other than the browser opening it is the tell in EDR file events. Recent Chrome versions add app-bound encryption on Windows, which pushes attackers toward injecting into the browser process.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice** (uc-r2): Seeing an attacker search hosts and SYSVOL. Detection for T1552.001 and .006 covers scanning for exposures, not the telemetry of someone hunting for them.
  - Draft: Scanning finds exposures, but you also need to see an attacker hunting. In process creation records (Sysmon event 1, or Windows 4688 with command line) look for findstr, grep or Select-String searching for password, or for tools such as Get-GPPPassword. For SYSVOL, event 5145 on the domain controller can show which account read Groups.xml, though this detailed share auditing must be enabled and is noisy. A user workstation reading many policy XML files in a short burst stands apart from normal Group Policy refresh.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice** (uc-r3): Detecting use of a stolen key. Copying a key file is often invisible, so the login with it may be the only signal, and the room covers leak and file read only.
  - Draft: Theft of a key is often invisible, so also watch for its use. On Linux, sshd writes a line such as Accepted publickey for deploy from 203.0.113.9 port 51422 ssh2: RSA SHA256:<fingerprint>, giving the account, source and key fingerprint. Baseline which key logs in from where, and treat a known key from a new source as a lead. Response means removing the public key from every authorized_keys file and issuing a new pair, not only deleting the leaked copy.
- **unsecured-credentials-practice** (uc-r5): Metadata theft outside AWS. T1552.005 is taught only on AWS, while many SOCs monitor Azure or Google Cloud.
  - Draft: Azure and Google Cloud expose the same idea. Azure IMDS also answers at 169.254.169.254 and requires the header Metadata: true, while Google Cloud uses metadata.google.internal with Metadata-Flavor: Google. Those required headers block many simple SSRF attempts, the same goal as the session token in AWS IMDSv2. Detect misuse by watching what the instance's identity does afterward, because the metadata call itself is usually not logged.
- **use-case-development** (ucd-read-2): Designing for evasion. A rule built on one exact string is easy to bypass, and the room's own sample rule has this weakness.
  - Draft: Before deploying, ask how an attacker would avoid the rule. PowerShell accepts shortened parameters such as -e and -ec, so matching only -enc misses them, and pwsh.exe or a renamed copy of powershell.exe escapes an Image match. Match OriginalFileName where the data source has it, cover the short forms, and add Script Block Logging (Event ID 4104), which records the decoded script. Prefer behaviour attackers cannot easily change.
- **use-case-development** (ucd-read-2): Threshold and correlation rules. Many use cases, such as spraying and beaconing, cannot be caught by one event, and the room shows only a single-event rule.
  - Draft: Some attacks only appear across many events, so the rule must count over a window and per entity. For password spraying in Sentinel: SigninLogs | where ResultType == "50126" | summarize Accounts = dcount(UserPrincipalName) by IPAddress, bin(TimeGenerated, 10m) | where Accounts > 50. Choose the entity and window from what normal looks like in your data. Sigma also has correlation rules for this pattern, so such logic can stay vendor-neutral.
- **use-case-development** (ucd-read-1): Validating data sources. A rule on a field that is not logged never fires and looks the same as a quiet network.
  - Draft: Confirm the data exists before writing logic: check that the field is populated and parsed in the SIEM, not just documented. For example, Windows 4688 records the command line only if that audit setting is turned on. After deployment, watch for a log source going silent, and run a harmless test of the behaviour on a schedule so a broken pipeline shows up as a missed detection.
- **use-case-development** (ucd-read-3): Exception hygiene. Every suppression is a blind spot attackers can use, and the room never says how to manage one.
  - Draft: Write each exception as narrowly as the known-good activity allows, using several fields such as the parent process, account and host group. Record the reason, an owner and a review or expiry date in the registry. Avoid excluding on a process name or a user-writable path alone, because an attacker can copy either one. Review old exceptions in the quarterly review.
- **use-case-development** (ucd-read-3): Measuring detection coverage and quality. A SOC must show which techniques it can detect and how good those rules are; the room tracks only false positive rate.
  - Draft: Track coverage by marking tested rules on an ATT&CK Navigator layer, which shows gaps against the techniques that matter to you. Measure quality as precision, the share of alerts that were true positives, and record the last date each rule was tested against a simulated attack. A technique counts as covered only when a test made the rule fire. An untested rule is a gap that looks like coverage.
- **vishing-helpdesk-social-engineering** (vish-r7): Responding to a confirmed takeover. The room ends at detection and hardening, so a trained analyst has no steps for the account itself once a vishing case is confirmed.
  - Draft: When a vishing takeover is confirmed, revoke the user's sessions and refresh tokens, reset the password through a verified channel, and remove every authentication method added since the reset. A password reset alone is not enough, because issued access tokens stay valid until they expire and a newly registered method survives it. Then review the audit log for what the attacker changed, such as MFA methods, mailbox rules, app consents and role assignments, and scope their activity from the sign-in log.
- **vishing-helpdesk-social-engineering** (vish-r4): Logs for a new MFA registration. Registering a new method is one of the room's three attacker asks, yet no operation name is given for it. Check the exact activity names against a current tenant.
  - Draft: A new authentication method has its own audit record: look for User registered security info, or Admin registered security info when someone else added it, in the UserManagement category with loggedByService set to Authentication Methods. A registration soon after a reset, from a device or country new to the account, is as strong as the sign-in signal. Compare the record with the account's normal method list before deciding.
- **vishing-helpdesk-social-engineering** (vish-r4): Reading an MFA-push flood in sign-in logs. The room says this flavour lives in authentication logs and tests it, but gives no fields to find it with.
  - Draft: For vishing aimed at the account owner there is no admin record to find, so read the sign-in log instead. Look for repeated interrupted attempts for one user, with result codes such as 50074 (strong authentication required) or 500121 (failed during strong authentication), from one unfamiliar IP, followed by a success from that same IP. Check authenticationDetails to see the method used, and ask the user through a trusted channel whether they approved it.
- **vishing-helpdesk-social-engineering** (vish-r4): The same fingerprints in Okta. The room's lead case ran through Okta but only Entra fields are taught, and many SOCs run Okta. Check event names against current Okta System Log documentation.
  - Draft: In Okta the same story appears in the System Log. Admin resets show as user.account.reset_password and user.mfa.factor.reset_all, a new factor as user.mfa.factor.activate, and the follow-up login as user.session.start. Correlate them the same way: an admin action on someone else's account, then a session from a new device or location within hours.
- **vishing-helpdesk-social-engineering** (vish-r0): Vishing over collaboration tools. The room defines voice broadly but treats only phone calls, while external Microsoft Teams calls posing as IT are a reported current delivery path. Verify current audit event names before using specific ones.
  - Draft: The call does not have to use a phone. Attackers also message or call employees through Microsoft Teams from an outside tenant, with a display name such as Help Desk, which gives the same pretext and sometimes leaves more evidence than a phone call. Review the organization's external-access settings, and check Teams audit events for first-contact external chats shortly before a remote-support tool starts or an MFA change appears.
- **vpn-monitoring** (vpn-r2): Exploitation of the VPN appliance itself. The room treats the VPN as a door attacked with passwords, but appliance exploits are a leading ransomware entry point and produce no failed-logon pattern at all.
  - Draft: Attackers also exploit the VPN appliance itself through published vulnerabilities, as with Citrix Bleed and the Ivanti Connect Secure chains. A successful exploit can give a session with no password attempt at all, so authentication logs may look clean or show one normal login. Track the appliance's patch level against vendor advisories, watch for exploit patterns in its web logs, and after patching invalidate existing sessions, because stolen session tokens survive a patch.
- **vpn-monitoring** (vpn-r2): Real log lines from each vendor. The room promises analysis of AnyConnect and GlobalProtect telemetry but shows no real line, so an analyst would not recognise them. The GlobalProtect location in vpn-r2 reflects older PAN-OS behaviour; exact formats vary by version.
  - Draft: On Cisco ASA, a login reads like %ASA-6-113039: Group <VPN-USERS> User <jsmith> IP <203.0.113.5> AnyConnect parent session started. The matching end event, 113019, gives the duration, bytes transmitted and received, and the disconnect reason, so that is where you read session size. Since PAN-OS 9.1, GlobalProtect events have their own log type, separate from the system log, with stages such as login and gateway-tunnel-latency. Pull the public IP, assigned private IP and machine name from these records.
- **vpn-monitoring** (vpn-r3): Pivoting on the assigned internal IP. The room tells analysts to search by the assigned IP, but those addresses are reused from a pool, so a search with no time bound blames the wrong user.
  - Draft: The internal IP a gateway assigns belongs to a session, not a person, and the pool reuses addresses after disconnect. When you pivot into firewall, proxy or server logs, restrict the search to that session's exact start and end times. Anything outside that window may belong to another user.
- **vpn-monitoring** (vpn-r3): Residential proxies and device checks. Attackers now route through local residential proxies, which defeats country and impossible-travel detections, so analysts need signals beyond geolocation.
  - Draft: Geolocation alone is weak, because attackers rent residential proxy exits in the victim's own country, so a stolen login can look local. Compare the device too: hostname, client version, operating system and any certificate. A default-looking hostname such as DESKTOP-XXXXXXX on an account that always uses a managed laptop name is a strong signal, as is a VPS provider's ASN on a user session.
- **vpn-monitoring** (vpn-r2): MFA weaknesses on VPN. Password-only logins and fallback profiles are how MFA gets bypassed on VPNs, and the room mentions an auth-method change only as a baseline bullet.
  - Draft: Many VPN deployments keep an old connection profile or local account that still accepts a password alone, and attackers look for it. Alert on any success that used a weaker method than the user's norm, and list which profiles allow password-only access. Also watch for repeated push prompts followed by an approval, which is the MFA fatigue pattern. Do not treat an MFA success on its own as proof that the login was legitimate.
- **vulnerability-management** (vm-r5): Checking whether a flaw was already exploited. This is the SOC's main contribution beyond ranking: a KEV-listed flaw left open may already have been used, and patching removes neither the attacker nor the evidence.
  - Draft: When a finding is KEV-listed or has a public exploit, ask whether it was used before the fix, not only when it will be fixed. Search the asset's web, WAF, EDR and authentication logs from the first_found date, or earlier if the flaw is older, for the exploit pattern and for follow-on activity such as a web server spawning a shell. If you find it, the ticket becomes an incident. A patch closes the door, but it does not remove anyone already inside.
- **vulnerability-management** (vm-r4): Reading a scanner finding's own fields. Analysts work from scanner records all day, and the room's log uses dates and plugin evidence that no reading explains.
  - Draft: A scanner finding carries more than a score. first_found and last_found show how long it has been open and whether the latest scan still sees it, which is how you measure aging against a deadline. plugin_output shows the evidence the scanner relied on, so a version banner tells you it inferred the flaw rather than proved it. scan_type tells you how far to trust the result.
- **vulnerability-management** (vm-r3): Vendor risk scores. Scanners display their own scores, and an analyst who cannot explain a gap between CVSS and displayed severity will misreport it to stakeholders; product scoring changes between releases, so verify current behaviour with the vendor.
  - Draft: Many scanners show their own priority score beside CVSS, such as Tenable's Vulnerability Priority Rating (VPR), a 0 to 10 score that adds threat intelligence to the technical severity. A displayed severity driven by such a score can sit in a different band from the CVSS base score. That is expected, so check which score the platform is set to display before reporting a number.
- **vulnerability-management** (vm-r4): Assets the scanner never saw. A clean report only covers what was scanned, and unscanned systems are the classic route to an unpatched breach.
  - Draft: A scan report says nothing about hosts it did not reach. Compare scanned assets with the asset inventory, and look for hosts that dropped out of scanning or failed authentication, because a failed credential can make a host look clean. Treat an unscanned internet-facing system as unknown risk, not as low risk.
- **wazuh-fundamentals** (wazuh-r3): Searching and triaging alerts. An analyst spends the shift in the dashboard, and the room lists its sections but never shows how to search or how to work an alert. Not sure of the exact version where Security Events was renamed Threat Hunting, so verify it before publishing.
  - Draft: In the dashboard's event view you filter on alert fields, for example rule.id: 5902 and agent.name: linux-srv-01. Read rule.description and rule.level, then full_log for the original line, then rule.groups and rule.mitre.id for context. Widen to the same agent and time window to see what came before and after. Section names have changed between Wazuh versions, for example Security Events became Threat Hunting in recent 4.x releases, so confirm on your version.
- **wazuh-fundamentals** (wazuh-r2): Windows alerts and Sysmon. Most SOC endpoints are Windows, but every example in the room is Linux, so an analyst could not read a Windows alert.
  - Draft: Windows events reach Wazuh through the eventchannel collector, and their fields sit under data.win. For example, data.win.system.eventID holds the event ID, and data.win.eventdata.targetUserName holds the account. Sysmon is collected the same way once its channel is added to the agent's configuration. Rule IDs for these events fall in the 60000 to 61999 block.
- **wazuh-fundamentals** (wazuh-r2): Tuning with custom rules. The room says custom rules start at 100000 but never shows one, and reducing noise without losing detection is daily SOC work.
  - Draft: Custom rules go in /var/ossec/etc/rules/local_rules.xml. A child rule uses if_sid to build on an existing rule, and adds conditions such as a field match. Setting a child rule to level 0 silences one known-good case, such as a named backup host, without changing the parent. Test with wazuh-logtest before you restart the manager, and keep the condition narrow, because a broad level-0 rule hides real attacks.
- **wazuh-fundamentals** (wazuh-r1): Devices with no agent. Firewalls and network gear cannot run an agent, so an analyst must know how their logs arrive and why they may be missing.
  - Draft: Devices that cannot run an agent, such as firewalls and switches, can send syslog directly to the manager once a syslog listener is configured, commonly on port 514. Those logs are decoded and ruled like any other. If a network device goes silent, check the listener configuration and the allowed sources, not agent status.
- **web-application-security** (webapp-r4): Scoping and confirming a web attack. An analyst has to say whether an attack worked and what else it touched, and no reading covers this step.
  - Draft: After spotting one malicious request, pivot on the real client address and session across WAF and edge logs for the whole attack window. Separate attempts from successes using status and response size, then confirm on the tier where the attack lands: the database audit log for SQL injection, EDR on the web server for command execution. If it succeeded, hand the application owner the exact parameter and raise an incident, since exposed data or credentials may need action.
- **web-application-security** (webapp-r1): Investigating POST-delivered attacks. The room says the access log omits the body but not where to look instead, and many real attacks arrive by POST.
  - Draft: When an attack travels in a POST body, the access log shows only a normal-looking line, so use other evidence: application logs, database audit logs, or a packet capture if one exists. Do not assume the WAF kept the body either, since AWS WAF logs record the URI, arguments and headers but not the full body, though a matched rule may show a fragment. Odd timing, a burst of POSTs to one page, or an error spike tell you which requests to chase.
- **web-application-security** (webapp-r3): Web shells. A web shell is one of the most common results of a web compromise, and an analyst must recognise it as a confirmed breach.
  - Draft: An attacker who can write a file into a web folder can plant a small script page and run commands through it. In logs this looks like a POST to an upload page, followed by repeated requests to a new .aspx or .php file nobody else visits, while w3wp.exe spawns cmd.exe on the host. ATT&CK tracks it as T1505.003, Server Software Component: Web Shell. Treat it as confirmed compromise and hunt for other planted files.
- **web-application-security** (webapp-r3): Server-side request forgery. SSRF is an OWASP Top 10 2021 category (A10) that the room's mapping skips, and it is a direct route to cloud credentials.
  - Draft: SSRF is a parameter that makes the server fetch a URL of the attacker's choosing, so the request comes from inside your network. The log tell is a parameter holding a URL or internal address, such as 169.254.169.254, the cloud metadata address, which can hand out instance credentials (T1552.005). Confirm with outbound requests from the application server to places it never normally calls. On AWS, requiring IMDSv2 blocks the simple form of this attack.
- **web-application-security** (webapp-r3): Credential stuffing against web logins. Login abuse is daily web SOC work, and the room's A07 section covers sessions only.
  - Draft: Credential stuffing replays stolen username and password pairs against a login page (T1110.004). In the log it is a run of POSTs to the login path, often from many addresses, where failures and successes differ only by a redirect or response size because many apps return 200 for a failed login. The usernames sit in the body, so you need the application's own authentication log. Look for one success among many failures, then treat that account as compromised.
- **web-attacks-practice** (web-r2): Web shell detection. The room promises process telemetry and tests the web shell chain, but no reading covers how to find a shell.
  - Draft: A web shell is a script placed inside the site that runs commands for the attacker. Hunt for it from three sides: a new .aspx or similar file written by w3wp.exe in a web folder, POST requests to a file that did not exist before and that only one source ever calls, and w3wp.exe starting a command interpreter. Sysmon Event IDs 11 and 1, Security 4688 or EDR telemetry supply the host side, and the IIS log gives the requests that used it.
- **web-attacks-practice** (web-r2): Responding to a confirmed web compromise. Blocking one IP is the only response the room describes, and it leaves the database credentials and any planted shell in place.
  - Draft: After a confirmed web attack, block the source, then preserve the IIS, WAF and database audit logs before they roll over. Search the site for planted files, and isolate the server if a shell ran. Rotate the credentials the application holds, such as the database connection string in web.config, because anything the shell could read must be treated as stolen. Fix the code last, and watch for the attacker returning from a new address.
- **web-attacks-practice** (web-r1): Path traversal in the logs. The room names path traversal as a payload class but teaches outcome reading for SQL injection alone.
  - Draft: Path traversal appears in the URI or query string as ../ or its encoded forms such as %2e%2e%2f, aimed at files like web.config or win.ini. The same outcome rule applies: 400, 403 and 404 mean it failed, while a 200 with a response size unlike the normal page suggests a file came back. Compare sc-bytes with the expected file size and with the endpoint's baseline.
- **web-attacks-practice** (web-r2): Reading a WAF record. The tasks show action and terminatingRuleId, but only the client IP field is taught, and ALLOW is easily misread as safe. Check the body-inspection limits for the resource type in use.
  - Draft: In an AWS WAF record, action is the result, terminatingRuleId names the rule that decided it, and NONE means no rule stopped the request, so ALLOW only says nothing matched. AWS WAF inspects only the first part of a request body, 8 KB for an Application Load Balancer by default, and the rule's oversize handling decides what happens to larger bodies. That is why a big POST can pass a WAF unexamined.
- **web-attacks-practice** (web-r2): Other sources of the client IP. The reading calls the WAF the only place the real source survives, which is not true for an ALB deployment, and an analyst may need these logs when WAF logging is off.
  - Draft: The WAF record is not always the only copy of the client address. Application Load Balancer access logs record the client IP and port for each request, if access logging is enabled. An X-Forwarded-For header is also useful when captured, but its left-hand values are supplied by the client and can be forged, so trust only the entry added by your own load balancer.
- **windows-event-logs** (win-evtlogs-r2): PowerShell script block logging. The room says attackers rely on PowerShell and shows encoded commands, but gives no event that reveals what the script actually did.
  - Draft: When Script Block Logging is enabled, Event ID 4104 in the PowerShell Operational log records the script text, including the decoded form of commands passed with -EncodedCommand. Use it after a suspicious 4688 to see what the code really did. Look for download calls, in-memory execution and attempts to disable security tools. It is not on for every script by default, so check your Group Policy.
- **windows-event-logs** (win-evtlogs-r3): Tying events to one session with Logon ID. Without this link an analyst cannot prove which commands belong to which logon, which is the core of reconstructing an attack on a host.
  - Draft: A 4624 event carries a TargetLogonId that identifies the new session. The same value appears as SubjectLogonId on 4688 process events run in that session, and as TargetLogonId on the 4634 or 4647 logoff. Pivot on it to list what a logon did and how long it lasted. Logon IDs are only meaningful on the host that issued them, so do not match them across machines.
- **windows-event-logs** (win-evtlogs-r2): Which machine records which event. Analysts lose time and draw false negatives when they search the wrong host for a logon.
  - Draft: A 4624 or 4625 is written on the machine being logged on to, not the one the user sits at. For a domain account, the domain controller also records the credential check: 4776 for NTLM and 4768 or 4769 for Kerberos. So a logon to a file server leaves its 4624 on the file server and its validation on the DC. If you only collect from domain controllers, you will miss most 4624 and 4688 events.
- **windows-event-logs** (win-evtlogs-r2): Account lockout and audit policy changes. Lockouts are often the first sign of a password attack, and a policy change is another way to blind logging without triggering 1102.
  - Draft: Event ID 4740 records an account lockout on the domain controller and names the caller computer, which is the host to investigate first. Event ID 4719 records a change to the system audit policy. An attacker can use that to stop logging without clearing anything, so it never produces a 1102. Treat an unticketed 4719 as seriously as a cleared log.
- **windows-event-logs** (win-evtlogs-r1): Key Sysmon event IDs. The room tells analysts Sysmon is valuable but gives no IDs, so they could not use it on a real host.
  - Draft: The Sysmon events you will use most are 1 (process creation, with hashes and command line), 3 (network connection), 10 (process access, such as a tool opening lsass.exe), 11 (file created), 13 (registry value set) and 22 (DNS query). Unlike 4688, event 1 records file hashes, so you can check a binary directly. Sysmon only logs what its configuration file includes, so confirm the configuration before treating a missing event as proof of absence.
- **windows-fundamentals** (win-fund-r4): Windows Event Log essentials. Every Windows investigation runs through these event IDs, and the room mentions only a few of them in passing with no map of which log holds what.
  - Draft: The Security log is the core record. 4624 is a successful logon and 4625 a failed one, and the Logon Type field tells you how: 2 at the keyboard, 3 over the network, 10 by Remote Desktop. 4688 is a new process, which shows the command line only if that audit setting is enabled. 4720 is a new user, 4732 a member added to a local group, and 1102 means the Security log was cleared. In the System log, 7045 is a new service, a common sign of attacker persistence.
- **windows-fundamentals** (win-fund-r2): Scheduled tasks and the Startup folder. Scheduled tasks (ATT&CK T1053.005) are among the most used Windows persistence methods and the room never mentions them, so a trainee checking only Run keys and services will miss them.
  - Draft: Run keys are not the only way to survive a reboot. A scheduled task can launch a program at logon or on a timer: list them with Get-ScheduledTask or schtasks /query, and read the stored definitions in C:\Windows\System32\Tasks. Event 4698 records a task being created when object access auditing is on. Also check each user's Startup folder under AppData\Roaming\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs\Startup, since anything placed there runs at logon without touching the Registry.
- **windows-fundamentals** (win-fund-r3): What Sysmon is. The room cites Sysmon event IDs as detection steps without ever saying what Sysmon is, and analysts meet it constantly in SIEM data.
  - Draft: Sysmon is a free Microsoft Sysinternals tool that writes detailed activity to its own event log, once an organisation installs it. The IDs you will meet most are 1 for process creation with full command line and hashes, 3 for network connections, 10 for one process opening another, 11 for file creation, 13 for Registry value changes and 22 for DNS queries. Event 10 targeting lsass.exe is the classic way to see credential dumping attempts.
- **windows-fundamentals** (win-fund-r3): The normal process tree. The room gives suspicious parent-child pairs but only part of the healthy baseline, and benign comparison is how an analyst tells a real finding from noise.
  - Draft: Healthy Windows has a predictable family tree. smss.exe starts csrss.exe and wininit.exe; wininit.exe starts services.exe and lsass.exe; services.exe is the parent of svchost.exe. Check parent, path and user together: an lsass.exe whose parent is not wininit.exe, or an svchost.exe not started by services.exe, is a finding even if the path looks right.
- **windows-fundamentals** (win-fund-r4): First response on a suspect host. The room stops at spotting indicators, so a trainee has no idea whether to delete, kill or escalate, and doing the wrong one destroys evidence.
  - Draft: When you find a likely malicious process or persistence entry, do not delete it first. Record the path, hash, parent, user, command line and time, then escalate and isolate the host through your EDR so it stays reachable for investigation while it is cut off from the network. Export the relevant events and persistence entries to a case file, and leave removal until the scope is known, since one implant often means others exist.
- **windows-privilege-escalation** (privesc-r5): Responding to a confirmed escalation. The whole room stops at recognition, and an analyst must know how to contain and scope once a process has reached High or SYSTEM.
  - Draft: Assume anything reachable from that privilege level has been touched. Isolate the host, stop the process tree, and use process ancestry to list what the elevated process did, including any LSASS access, new service or scheduled task. If LSASS was reachable, treat the credentials used on that host as exposed and rotate them. Fix the cause last: remove unneeded local admin rights, quote the service path, or tighten the ACL, then rebuild if the host cannot be trusted.
- **windows-privilege-escalation** (privesc-r5): Service abuse telemetry. The room explains unquoted paths and weak permissions but not what they look like in logs, so an analyst could not detect them.
  - Draft: A new service appears as Event ID 7045 in the System log, and as Event ID 4697 in the Security log when that auditing is on. A changed ImagePath shows as a Sysmon Event 13 under HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services, often from sc.exe config or reg.exe. For an unquoted path, watch for a new executable created at an intermediate path (Sysmon Event 11), then the same file launched by services.exe as SYSTEM (Sysmon Event 1).
- **windows-privilege-escalation** (privesc-r4): Potato telemetry. The room explains how Potato works but gives no log signature, so a web-shell escalation would go unseen.
  - Draft: Look for the web worker process, w3wp.exe on IIS, starting cmd.exe, powershell.exe or whoami.exe, and for a child running as NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM when its parent ran as an IIS APPPOOL identity. PrintSpoofer-style tools create a named pipe, which Sysmon Events 17 and 18 can record, depending on your configuration. Any service-account process suddenly producing SYSTEM children on a web server deserves escalation.
- **windows-privilege-escalation** (privesc-r5): Other common privesc paths. A trained analyst should know the room's list is not complete, especially DLL hijacking and vulnerable drivers, which appear in real intrusions.
  - Draft: Two more paths appear often. DLL hijacking (T1574.001) places a malicious DLL where a privileged program will load it, visible as Sysmon Event 7 for an unsigned DLL loaded from an unusual folder. Exploitation of a software or kernel flaw (T1068), including loading a vulnerable signed driver, visible as Sysmon Event 6, reaches SYSTEM with no misconfiguration at all. Both produce the same unexplained jump in privilege, so apply the same triage question.
- **windows-protocols-lateral** (winproto-r6): Share access events 5140 and 5145. The log analysis is built on Event 5145, yet no reading names it or says it may not be enabled.
  - Draft: Named pipe access is visible in the target's Security log. Event 5140 records a network share being accessed, and Event 5145 records each object opened on it, with ShareName and RelativeTargetName, so IPC$ plus svcctl appears there. Both come from Object Access auditing: 5145 needs the Detailed File Share subcategory, which is noisy and often off, so a missing 5145 does not prove nothing happened. In that record IpAddress is the remote client and the computer name is the host being accessed.
- **windows-protocols-lateral** (winproto-r6): RDP and WinRM detection. These are among the most used lateral movement paths and the room gives them no log guidance at all.
  - Draft: RDP shows on the target as Event 4624 with logon type 10 and as Event 1149 in the TerminalServices-RemoteConnectionManager Operational log. WinRM and PowerShell Remoting use TCP 5985 and 5986 and run remote commands under wsmprovhost.exe on the target, so a shell whose parent is wsmprovhost.exe marks remote execution. As with SMB, the deciding question is whether this account and source normally administer this host.
- **windows-protocols-lateral** (winproto-r4): Pass-the-Hash in logs. The room covers NTLM and Pass-the-Ticket but never shows the far more common NTLM credential replay.
  - Draft: Pass-the-Hash uses a stolen NT hash to answer the NTLM challenge, so no password is needed. On the target it appears as Event 4624 with logon type 3 and authentication package NTLM, usually between workstations that have no reason to talk. On the source machine, tools that inject the hash into a new session often leave a 4624 with logon type 9 and logon process seclogo. The hash stays useful until the password changes.
- **windows-protocols-lateral** (winproto-r4): Detecting NTLM relay. The room explains why relay works but gives the analyst nothing to look for in logs.
  - Draft: In a relay, the target's Event 4624 shows the attacker's relay host as IpAddress, while WorkstationName still carries the victim's computer name, because that name came from the victim's own Type 3 message. A mismatch between the name and the address it should resolve to is the classic tell. Follow up by enforcing SMB signing and Extended Protection, and by disabling LLMNR and NBT-NS.
- **windows-protocols-lateral** (winproto-r3): Containing Kerberos credential attacks. A password reset alone does not end ticket replay, and analysts often close the case too early.
  - Draft: A password reset does not cancel tickets already issued, so a stolen TGT keeps working until its endtime. Disable the account while you investigate, purge cached tickets on affected hosts with klist purge and end active sessions. For Kerberoasting, rotate every targeted service account to a long random password, or better, move it to a group Managed Service Account, and allow AES only where the service supports it.
